# The End Is the Beginning

*A tale of the sands, the jinn, and the lost city of Ubar*


---

# PART ONE: THE NAMELESS


---

# Chapter One
## The Boy the Sand Forgot

> *When the grey comes creeping and the names are lost,*
> *A child of the feather will carry the cost.*
> — from the Song of the Phoenix-Born, as sung over cradles in the goat-hair tents of Liwa

---

*The fire is ghaf wood, and it burns slow and sweet, and the children sit close because the night on the sands of Liwa is colder than any of them expected. Above them the stars are so thick they look spilled. Suhail is up, low and red in the south. The heat is breaking.*

*The old man pours the coffee himself. He will not let the boys do it. His hands shake, and he spills, and he does not care.*

*"You'll say it's a tale," he tells them. "Your fathers said so too, and their fathers. I tell you it happened. I was there. I was younger than some of you are now, and stupider than all of you put together."*

*At his feet lies a saluki, so old that the feathering on its ears has gone white as salt. One of its eyes is amber. The other is clouded silver, like a coin left in the sea. The children do not look at that eye for long. Something in it looks back.*

*"I won't tell it my way," says the old man. "My way has too much of me in it. I'll tell it the way the dog would tell it, for the dog saw all of it. All of it." He touches the dog's head, very gently, the way you touch a thing you owe. "And the dog forgives less than I do."*

*The saluki sighs through its nose. The fire settles.*

*"Listen, then."*

---

He is burning.

The moon hangs full and white above Tal Moreeb, the badr, so close and so swollen that it looks like something that has fed. The great dune of Liwa lies under it, the highest sand in the world, and on its crest a boy stands with his arms open, as if he is about to catch something falling out of the sky.

The fire doesn't start at his hands. It starts in his chest, under the mark, the small golden feather that has glowed in the dark his whole life. It splits from the inside, like a date-stone in wet ground, because the thing inside has grown too big to stay. Light comes through the cracks in his skin. It runs down his arms and up his throat. It comes out of his eyes, and his eyes go with it.

You can smell it. Nobody ever tells you that part in the songs. The light smells of honey and hot metal, and under that, very faintly, of hair. My nose is better than yours. I smell it still.

And the boy doesn't scream.

That's the part I always remember. I used to think it was courage. Lately I've wondered whether the fire takes the scream first, the way a thief cuts the bell from a camel's neck before he leads it away into the dark.

The light grows until it isn't a boy any more, only two great wings of gold unfolding across the night, wide enough to cover the sands from the Hajar to the sea. Then it falls. Gold rains down over the desert, and everywhere it lands, the grey draws back.

Draws back. I want to be careful with my words, because you'll hold me to them later.

Somewhere below, someone is shouting a name. The wind is listening to every letter.

---

But that is the end.

Let me begin where the boy had no name.

---

You'll want to know what I am. Everyone always does, and they never like the answer.

I am a dog. Don't make that face. I am a saluki, the hunting hound of the Bedouin, the only dog they let sleep inside the tent: sand-coloured, lean as a tent-pole, with feathered ears and a feathered tail. They call me Ghareeb. The stranger. It's the one name of mine they ever got right. I have one amber eye and one silver. We'll get to the silver one later. Don't look at it too long.

I cannot speak to you, or to any man. Everything you'll hear from me is the inside of my head, and you've been invited in only because I'm tired of being alone in it.

What I can do is smell a lie. A lie smells sour, like milk on the turn, like the sweat of a man who has just put a stone in his sleeve. When I smell it, the hair rises along my spine and a growl starts in my chest before I can stop it. When I smell the truth, nothing happens at all. That's the trouble with the truth. It doesn't raise the hackles.

I'm older than every fort in this desert. I'm older than the city under the sand that the old men call Ubar. I have walked beside eleven children who carried the Light, and I have watched nearly every one of them climb the same dune under the same full moon. Children. Notice that word, because the songs don't. *The Phoenix-Born*, *the chosen*, *the children of the 'Anqa'*: very good words for not saying *child*. The Boy of Salt was nine. He asked me, at the top, whether it would be like putting his hand in the cooking fire. I couldn't answer him. I licked his hand. He took that for *no*. I let him.

Some of them were brave. Some of them ran. I'm still angry at the ones who ran. I'm angrier at the sky for asking.

This is the story of the twelfth.

He was the one I loved most. Don't tell the others.

---

The village was called Hilat al-Sarab. The settlement of the mirage. Somebody's grandfather had named it as a joke, and the joke had outlived him.

It sat on the last hard gravel before the great sands of Liwa began, forty houses of mud brick and palm frond and a grove of thirsty date palms, crouched around a single deep well like old men guarding one coin. To the south and east the dunes rolled away gold and red and endless, and they moved. Every night the shamal pushed them, whole hills sliding in the dark with a sound like something very large breathing through its nose. Some mornings there were long dragging furrows in the sand that ended at the doorsteps and went no farther. Nobody talked about it. You only murmured a word to the One who made the sands when you poured water on the ground, so that nothing under it would be scalded, and you went on.

The people feared the sand the way you'd fear a large animal that hadn't decided yet whether to eat you.

They feared the boy a little more.

He was fifteen that year, as near as anyone could guess. The desert had dropped him on the edge of the village after a sandstorm, a baby wrapped in a grey cloth that no woman in Hilat al-Sarab could name the weave of. No mother. No camel tracks. Only a baby, lying in a perfect circle of smooth sand in the middle of the storm's wreckage, crying.

And a dog, lying a short way off, watching.

I'll admit nothing about that.

The village kept him the way you'd keep a stray kid-goat that wandered in during a storm. They fed him when they remembered and kicked him when they didn't. They called him *Laqeet*, foundling, when they were feeling kind, and *the jinni's whelp* when they weren't, which was most days. Nobody ever gave him a real name. You don't name the lamb that's set aside for the feast.

So he gave himself one. He was seven years old, sitting alone on the lip of the well, and he decided he would be called *Theyab*. Wolves. He didn't know where the word came from. It simply arrived in his mouth one evening, already shaped, as if someone had left it there for him. He told no one. A name was the only thing in the world that belonged to him, and he had already learned that the people of Hilat al-Sarab took things.

He told me, of course. Everyone tells the dog.

---

On the morning this story begins, Theyab was underground.

There were ruins an hour's walk into the dunes, half swallowed: the bones of some settlement so old that even the stones had forgotten who stacked them, with a dry channel where a *falaj* had once run. Every few weeks the wind opened a new room.

The village said the jinn held their weddings in the ruins. Which was convenient, because it meant the only person willing to dig through them was the boy they already thought belonged to the jinn. Theyab was a scavenger. It was the only work anyone would give him.

He crouched now in a chamber no taller than a goat, a blade between his teeth: an old khanjar whose silver hilt somebody had long since sold, bound now in rag, rusted along the spine. He was prying at a strip of copper along the foot of a wall. Every time he moved, sand hissed down from the ceiling in a long slow exhale, and then stopped, and then, a moment later, hissed again. As if the room were breathing with him. As if it were learning how.

I lay in the doorway with my nose working. The room smelled of dust and bat and very old smoke, and of something much deeper that I didn't like. Cold. Like the air at the bottom of a well that has been dry a hundred years and has had something lying in it all that time.

"Don't look at me like that," Theyab said around the knife. He talked to me all the time. He was the loneliest boy between the Hajar and the sea, and I was the only creature in the village that didn't throw things. "If I don't get this, we don't eat."

The copper came loose with a crack. Theyab grinned around the blade, the brief, crooked grin of a boy who doesn't get many reasons to smile and doesn't waste them.

Then the wall groaned.

It was long and low and unhappy, a great weight changing its mind. Dust poured from the ceiling. Theyab froze. His hand went to his chest, to the spot just under his collarbone where the mark sat beneath his shirt. He always did that when he was afraid.

The mark was a feather. A birthmark the colour of honey, with fine lines in it like the barbs of a real one. On dark nights it glowed. Faintly, so faintly you could pretend you hadn't seen it. He had spent fifteen years pretending he hadn't seen it.

Under his palm it was warm. Warmer than skin should be.

The wall held. The groaning stopped.

And in the silence afterwards, from somewhere deeper in the ruin, from a part of it no one had dug out in a thousand years, something knocked. Twice. Politely. The way a guest knocks at a tent pole when he wants to be let in and knows he is owed it.

My hackles went up from skull to tail, every hair. Not a lie. Worse. Something down there was pretending to be a guest.

Theyab didn't move for a long time.

Then he tucked the copper into his bag and crawled out into the sun, and he did not look back, and I loved him a little more for having the sense not to.

---

By the time we walked back into Hilat al-Sarab the sun was high and merciless, and his bag held enough copper to buy rice and dates for three days.

He got enough for one.

"Copper's cheap this month," said Ghanim, under the palm-frond roof of his stall, sliding two worn copper annas across the mat. Ghanim was the only man in the village who ever saw silver. He kept his Maria Theresa thalers in a strongbox with a lock the size of a fist, and counted them at night by lamplight like a man telling the beads of his own heart. "Too many scavengers."

There was exactly one scavenger in Hilat al-Sarab, and both of them knew it.

I smelled it rise off him, sour and quick. My hackles lifted. A growl started in my chest, low as a camel's grumble.

"Get that animal away from my stall," said Ghanim, "or I'll have its other eye out too."

Theyab put his hand on my neck, and the growl went back down where it came from. He picked up the coins and turned to go. He never argued with Ghanim. Ghanim's brass scales had a thumb in them, and everyone knew it, and Ghanim was getting very slowly rich on it.

We made it halfway across the square before someone said, "Look. The jinni's whelp is back from his grave."

Nadim.

There's one in every village. Nadim was Theyab's age, broad in the shoulder and quick in the tongue, the son of Saqi the well-keeper, which made him almost a sheikh's son in a place where every drop came up on Saqi's rope. He sat on the low wall of the well with three other boys around him like flies around a date.

"Find any ghosts down there, Laqeet?" Nadim called, hopping down. "Find your mother? Maybe that's where they found you. In a hole, with the rest of the rubbish."

Theyab kept walking.

There was a scar on Theyab's back, a pale, puckered line just below his left shoulder blade. He'd been six. A crowd of children had decided that the strange boy with the too-light eyes had a jinn in him, and everyone knows you drive out a jinn with stones. Nadim had been five years old that day and standing at the front. Neither of them ever spoke of it. Neither of them ever forgot.

"Hey." A stone hit the gravel by Theyab's foot. "I'm *talking* to you, monster."

That stopped him.

It always did. He could take *Laqeet* and *rubbish*. But *monster* went into him like a hook. Maybe because, on the dark nights, when the feather glowed under his shirt and he lay awake feeling it pulse in time with something that was not his heart, a small and frightened part of him wondered whether it was true.

He turned around.

Nadim was grinning. Waiting. Hoping.

And Theyab said, very quietly, "I'm not a monster."

He said it because it was true. That was the thing about Theyab, and it's the first thing you must know about him, so pay attention: **he could not lie.** Whenever a lie got as far as his tongue, the feather on his chest would burn as if a coal from the coffee hearth had been pressed into it, deep enough that he could smell it, sharp enough to bring water to his eyes. Once, at eight, he'd tried to push a lie through the pain and woken face down in the sand with a blister the shape of a feather.

So he told the truth, always, even when the truth got him hit. Mostly *because* the truth got him hit.

"Not a monster," Nadim repeated, in a high, mocking voice. The boys laughed.

Theyab turned and walked away with his hand pressed flat against his chest, and nobody followed.

I stayed a moment in the shade of a doorway. I watched Nadim's grin fade once Theyab was gone. He looked down at his hands for a moment, as though he wasn't sure whose they were.

I smelled no lie on him. Only a boy, and something else, faint, like a bruise you press to see if it still hurts. People are rarely only one thing. Remember that. You'll need it, later, when you want to hate him.

---

Theyab's tent sat a little way outside the village, on a patch of hard ground between the last house and the first dune. It was patched from nine scraps that had been nine different things: black goat hair, an old sail from a pearling boat, a sack that had once held rice from Basra. The wind came through the seams at night and whistled, and on some nights the whistling sounded almost like words. It was his.

There was a bundle waiting outside it.

It appeared every few days, with no footprints leading to it. A square of faded indigo cloth, knotted at the top. Inside was flatbread, thin and blistered, still faintly warm from the baking plate. And sometimes, on the good days, a twist of waxed paper holding four sticky dates soaked in dark sidr honey from the mountains.

Today was a good day.

Theyab sat down in the doorway of his tent and held the dates in his palm for a long time without eating them. Dates and honey were the finest thing he knew of in the world. He always saved them, for the hardest days, as if he could store sweetness the way you store water against a drought.

He tore the bread in two and gave me half. He always did that. I never once asked.

He didn't know who left the bundles. When he was small he had decided that it must be the One who made the sands, who had made a mistake with him, and left bread to say so.

I knew. I'd followed the smell of the bread back to its oven the very first morning.

It was Umm Hanan.

She was the old widow who baked bread for half the village on an iron plate behind her house, whose husband and two sons had gone out to the pearl banks one summer and come home in nobody's boat. She never once spoke to Theyab in the square or defended him. She was old and alone and afraid, and in Hilat al-Sarab kindness out loud could cost you everything. So she was kind quietly, before the doves woke, every few days for fifteen years. She was never thanked. She did it the way you'd drop a date-stone in wet ground and walk away, not even thinking of a tree.

The world is held together by people like Umm Hanan: too frightened to be brave, good anyway in small, secret ways. But the sand remembers. That's what he used to tell himself on the worst nights, lying alone in his patched tent and listening to the wind try out its words.

*The sand remembers.*

He meant it as a comfort. He didn't think very hard about the other side of it, which is that a thing that remembers everything also forgets nothing. Not a kindness. Not a cheat on a set of scales. Not a name.

---

When the sun went down and the first stars came out, Theyab went to see Old Bakhit.

Bakhit's tent sat at the far edge of the village, farther out even than Theyab's, a long low tent of black goat hair hung with bunches of dried harmal and wild thyme, and with dozens of tiny brass bells on threads. The bells were silent most of the time. They rang only when someone in the tent told a lie.

He was the oldest man anyone in Hilat al-Sarab had ever seen. His skin was brown and creased like a dry wadi bed, his beard was white as salt, and his eyes were milky and pale. He was blind, and had been for longer than anyone could remember. Bakhit means *the lucky one*. That's how the old Arabs named a blind man, gently, the way you'd step around a hole in the dark. The village came to him for fevers and scorpion stings, and left as fast as they could.

Only Theyab came for the stories.

"You're late," Bakhit said, before Theyab had even lifted the tent flap. "And you smell of copper and disappointment. Ghanim cheated you again."

"Two annas," said Theyab.

"For three days' worth of copper." Bakhit clicked his tongue. "Sit. There's tea."

There was always tea. Not coffee: coffee was for guests, and Theyab had never in his life been anybody's guest. Bakhit's tea was black and bitter, smoky with something green he would never name. Theyab drank it every night and hated it every night, and would have died before he admitted that. Hold on to that taste for me. You'll remember it longer than he does.

I lay down across the doorway, the way I always did, with my back to the inside and my nose to the dark.

"Ah. And the stranger." Bakhit's blind face turned toward me. "Evening, Ghareeb."

I thumped my tail on the sand once. I never did that for anyone else. I'll tell you about Bakhit and me another time.

"Which one tonight?" Bakhit asked.

"The Phoenix-Born," said Theyab. He asked for that one every time.

Bakhit smiled. Fond and sad at once, the way a man smiles at a letter from someone he once let drown.

"Again?" he said. "You know it better than I do."

"Tell it anyway."

So he did.

He told of the time before time, when the One who made the sands first made them, and of how a darkness came up out of the deep places, an Ifrit's darkness, born from fear, that made people forget who they were. Whenever the darkness grew too strong, a piece of light was set inside one child, marked with the golden feather of the 'Anqa', the fire-bird that burns and rises. That child would carry the light across the desert to the highest dune in the world. There, under the full moon, the child would set the light free and drive the darkness back into the ground.

"Whenever the darkness grows too strong," Theyab repeated. "How often is that?"

Bakhit's hands paused on his cup. "Often enough," he said.

The bells didn't stir. My hackles lay flat as wet cloth. True. Horribly, precisely true.

"The Phoenix-Born," Bakhit went on softly. "That's what the old songs call them. They rise like the fire-bird from its ashes. They save everyone."

Theyab's eyes were shining. They always shone at this part.

You have to understand what that story meant to a boy who was nameless, thrown away and called a monster. It said that sometimes the forgotten ones are chosen. He had never told anyone his secret dream, except me.

He wanted to be a hero.

Not rich. Not even loved. He wanted to *matter*. He wanted the people who threw stones to look up one day and understand that they had been wrong about him.

I have heard eleven children want that. The sky has never once failed to give it to them. That's the cruelty of it. It always keeps its word.

"What happens to them?" Theyab asked. "After? When the darkness is gone?"

He had never asked that before.

Bakhit went very still. On their threads, the brass bells waited. I watched one of them tremble, very slightly.

"They are remembered," Bakhit said at last.

No bell rang. My hackles never moved. It was true, after all. Just not the whole truth.

*The whole truth* and *true* are two very different animals, and the space between them is where most of the pain in the world lives. Children fall into that space. Bakhit knew exactly what happened to the Phoenix-Born. He chose not to say. But that's later.

"It's late," Bakhit said, and the moment passed. "Go home. And Theyab—"

The boy paused at the tent flap. Bakhit was the only soul who knew that name, and he had never once said it where anyone could hear.

"Remember the saying," said the old man. "Never say your name to the wind."

It's an old saying of the Bedouin, older than the caravan roads. The darkness has ears, they say, and the wind carries everything to it in the end, and a name spoken carelessly can be found by things that collect names. In the old tents, mothers gave their newborns ugly names for the first year, *Jahsh*, little donkey, *Kulaib*, little dog, so that if anything came calling in the night it would pass over a child nobody seemed to love.

It never passed over. But it was a kind thought.

Theyab nodded. He stepped out into the night.

And because he was fifteen, and lonely, and because the only thing in the world that was truly his was a name nobody else would ever say, he climbed to the top of the nearest dune, faced the open desert, and whispered it.

"Theyab."

The wind took it, gently. Too gently. It carried it away south and east over the moving sand, toward the dark.

I stood at the foot of the dune and smelled the wind change.

It came back over the crest from the deep sands, much colder than a desert night has any right to be, and it smelled of nothing. Wind out here always smells of something: camel, salt, smoke from a far fire. This smelled of nothing at all. It brushed past his ear, and in it, very faint, very far away, something said:

*"Theyab."*

In his own voice. Exactly his own voice. Only empty, the way a water-skin is empty, the way a tent is empty after the funeral meal is cleared away. Said back to him the way you'd repeat a word in a language you don't speak, to see how it feels in the mouth.

Then he saw it.

Far out on the horizon, where the gold of the dunes met the black of the sky, there was a line of grey. It was low and flat, like the mist that lies on the coastal sabkha in winter dawns, except the sabkha was four days away. It hadn't been there in fifteen years of nights. It wasn't moving fast. It didn't need to.

It had no edge. Where the dunes ran into it they went on being dunes for a little while, paler and flatter, as if forgetting how.

Above it, the stars were going out.

Not dimming. Going out. One by one, low on the sky, the way lamps go out in a house where someone is walking from room to room, pinching the wicks. Al-Thurayya was rising, the little cluster the herders call the sisters, and as he watched, one of the sisters went out. Then another. And he understood, with the cold, sure understanding you have in nightmares, that the thing on the horizon was not coming toward the village. It was coming toward the place where his name had been spoken.

The feather on his chest began, very faintly, to burn.

And for one instant, no longer than a blink, he wasn't on the dune at all. He was somewhere higher, much higher, with a white moon swollen above him, and there was heat in his mouth, and the smell of honey and hot metal and hair. Then it was gone, and he was on an ordinary dune with his heart going like a trapped bird. He thought he'd imagined it. He was very good, back then, at that.

He did not see me at the foot of the dune behind him, standing with my legs braced and every hair on my body lying flat and still. Not one hackle. Everything about that moment was true, and I have never hated my own nose quite so much.

Twelve. I scratched it into the sand with one claw, the way I always do, one stroke for each of them, and then I lay down on the marks before he could see them.

The grey was coming. The old women would have had a word for it, if any of them had still been alive who remembered: *al-Ghasaq*. The dark that comes after the light has gone and before anything else has arrived. The Ifrit's breath.

And this time, it had learned his name. It was practising.

---

*End of Chapter One*


---

# Chapter Two
## When the Grey Came Creeping

> *The grey does not bite. The grey forgets for you. And then it wears what is left.*
> — words cut into the stone lip of an old well in the sands, most of them worn away

---

Nobody in Hilat al-Sarab believed him.

Nobody there had believed anything Theyab said in fifteen years. Never mind that he couldn't lie. They'd never bothered to notice.

He ran into the village before dawn with sand in his hair and banged on the wooden door of the sheikh's house until a lamp was lit. It was the only house in the village with a wind-tower and a majlis, and Theyab had never been inside it. He stood on the step and told the sheikh what he had seen. A line of grey on the horizon, low and flat, and the stars going out above it one by one.

He didn't tell him about the voice in the wind. There was no way to say it that didn't sound like a jinn had got into him, and everyone already thought one had.

Sheikh Mazyad, a heavy man with a beard like a thornbush hennaed orange at the ends, listened with his eyes half closed. Then he said, "Sea fog. Off the sabkha."

"The sabkha's four days away," said Theyab.

"Dust, then. A storm out of the south."

"It wasn't moving like a storm. It was—" Theyab searched for the word. "Listening."

Mazyad looked at him the way you'd look at a goat that had started reciting poetry. Then something crossed his face, quick and ugly, and he glanced past Theyab's shoulder toward the east. I smelled it come off him. Not a lie exactly. Fear, which is the lie's older brother. "Go back to your tent, Laqeet," he said, and shut the door. I heard the bar drop across it on the other side.

I was lying in the shade of the wall across the lane. I could have howled the village awake. I could have barked in the square until every child woke screaming.

But I have rules. The bearer has to see it first. He has to be the one who knows. The times I've broken that rule, things went worse. Or so I tell myself. I've had a great deal of time to practise.

So I did nothing. I'm not proud of that. It's becoming a theme.

---

The grey came the next day.

Not all at once. The Ifrit's curse doesn't charge like a raiding party. It *seeps*. It arrives the way cold arrives in a tent on a winter night in the sands: first at the edges, then along the floor-mats, until one moment you realise you can't remember the last time you were warm. Or what warm was. Or why you'd want it.

It started with the colour.

Umm Hanan noticed first, though no one asked her. She came out of her house before the sun with a basin of dough on her hip, and stopped. The door of the house beside hers had been painted indigo by a man who had died twenty years ago, painted the deep blue that keeps off the eye. It wasn't blue any more. It wasn't any colour at all. It was the flat dead grey of old ash in a cold hearth.

Every morning since the boats came home without her men, Umm Hanan had said their names under her breath as she kneaded the dough. Her husband. Her two sons. Three names, in the same order, slapped into the bread like salt. That morning she said her husband's name. She said her eldest son's name. She reached for the third and found only a smooth place in her mind, like a tooth-socket the tongue keeps returning to.

She stood in the lane with her hands in the dough for a long time. Then she went back inside and didn't come out again until evening.

By noon, the whole eastern edge of the village had gone grey.

The red cloth over Ghanim's stall was grey. The date palms in the grove were grey, the clusters of ripening dates hanging from them like bunches of small stones. A goat wandered out of the grey part of the village and stood in the square, and its brown coat was the colour of cold porridge, and its eyes were the white of a boiled egg. It stood with its head at a slightly wrong angle, as if it were listening to something underneath the ground, and when a child threw a date-stone at it, it didn't flinch. It simply turned its white eyes, very slowly, toward the child, and kept them there.

The child's mother carried him inside. The goat watched the door all day.

And it was quiet. In the grey part of the village there was no sound at all. No wind in the palm fronds. No doves. No creak of doors.

People began to gather in the square. They talked in low, nervous voices. An old woman said she'd heard stories from her grandmother's grandmother about a thing that came up out of the deep sands, an Ifrit's breath that made people forget, and that you knew it was close when you couldn't say the names of your dead. Sheikh Mazyad told her to stop talking nonsense, too loudly. I noticed that he did not try to say the names of his own.

Theyab stood at the very edge of the crowd, where he always stood, close enough to hear and far enough to run.

His hand was pressed flat against his chest.

The feather mark had been burning since the dune. A slow, low heat, like a coal banked under ash, and it got hotter whenever he looked east. Every time he closed his eyes he smelled honey and hot metal. *My birthmark is warm and I keep smelling something burning* wasn't the kind of thing that made people trust a boy they already thought was jinn-born.

"It's moving," someone said.

Everyone turned.

At the eastern end of the square, the grey had reached the first house. It climbed the mud wall the way damp climbs a cloth. It slid across a child's toy left in the dirt, a little camel knotted out of palm frond, dyed yellow with turmeric. The toy had been yellow. It wasn't any more. It had been lying on its side. When the grey had passed, it was standing upright, facing the crowd.

Nobody had touched it.

"Everyone inside," said Sheikh Mazyad. "Shut your doors. Put out your lamps. Keep the children away from the windows."

As if a door could stop it.

---

The sun went down red and swollen over the western dunes. Night came on. And with the night came the emptied.

They were people. That's what you must understand first. Every one of them had been a person once, a herder or a date-farmer or somebody's son, until the Ifrit's curse found them and ate their names. It takes the names first, then the memories, then all the small invisible ropes that tie one person to another, the way a tent is tied down against the wind. What's left still walks. It still has a face. But the eyes are white and blank as boiled eggs, and the skin is the colour of ash, and the face doesn't do anything.

Except, sometimes, smile.

That's the worst of it. Not the eyes. The smile. Somewhere in what's left there's a memory of what faces are *for*, and every so often one of the emptied will try. The corners of the mouth go up, too far and too slowly, like a tent flap being lifted by someone who has never seen a tent. The eyes don't change. Nothing behind them changes. It's a smile the way a footprint is a foot.

And they talk. Not to you. They say the last thing they said before the grey took them, over and over, in their own voices, worn smooth, like a bead rolled between fingers until its pattern is gone. A trader might walk for a hundred years saying *"Two annas, and that's my last price."* A mother might say *"Come in now, it's getting dark."* It isn't a message. It's what's left when the person is gone: one sentence, rattling around in the empty house like a date-stone in a dry gourd.

They are drawn to light, like moths that never, ever stop.

They came out of the grey just after the last of the sunset went. First one, walking slowly up the eastern lane. Then three. Then a dozen, and more behind them, a grey crowd moving between the houses with no sound but the murmur of their dozens of last sentences, all overlapping, like a market heard through a mud wall.

They had no smell. That was how I knew them before I saw them. Forty bodies walking up a lane and not one of them smelling of sweat or camel or smoke.

Theyab saw them from the top of the dune behind his tent. He saw the shapes drifting through the dark lanes, stopping at doors, laying their palms flat against the wood and standing there, as though listening for a heartbeat. He saw them turn, all together, toward the one house that had forgotten to put out its lamp.

He was running before he'd decided to.

---

I went with him. Of course I went with him. I'd been going with him for fifteen years.

*This is a bad idea*, I thought at him, loping alongside him down the slope with my ears flat. *I want that on record. For the funeral. A short speech, I thought: he was warned.*

He didn't look at me. His whole face was fixed on the village, his knife already in his hand. The rag-hilted khanjar, rusted along the spine, its edge sharpened on a flat stone from the wadi.

A knife. Against an Ifrit's curse.

I ran faster, and listened to the voices coming up the lane to meet us, and tried very hard not to recognise any of them.

---

The house with the lamp belonged to the well-keeper.

Nadim's house.

Theyab came round the corner of the lane and stopped so suddenly that I nearly went into the backs of his legs.

The emptied had surrounded it. Twenty of them, perhaps more, standing in a silent half-circle around the door, their white eyes all fixed on the yellow light leaking from the shutters. They swayed together, the way the long grass sways on a dune after rain. From inside, Theyab could hear someone crying. A small child, by the sound. And a man's voice, low and frightened, saying *hush, hush, hush*.

And out in the lane, under the murmuring, three or four of the grey shapes had begun to say it too.

*"Hush. Hush. Hush."*

Not mocking. Just learning it. The way the wind had learned Theyab's name.

In front of the door, between the emptied and the house, stood Nadim.

He had the long iron spade they used to clear blown sand from the mouth of the well. He held it like a sword, shaking so badly the blade rattled.

"Get back," he was saying. His voice cracked in half on every word. "Get *back*. I mean it. Get back."

The emptied didn't get back.

One of them stepped forward. Slow. Unhurried. It had been a woman once, a grey woman in a grey dress, with grey silver bangles still on her grey wrists, and the black mask that the Bedouin women wear, the burqa'a, hanging grey across the bridge of a grey nose. Her lips were moving.

*"Mind the step, my eyes,"* she said. *"Mind the step, my eyes. Mind the step, my eyes."*

She said it kindly. She'd said it to some child once, at some tent's edge or some doorway, and then the grey had come up behind her, and her life had stopped on that one sentence like a camel kneeling in the sand that will not get up again.

She reached out a hand toward Nadim's face. And she smiled.

Nadim swung the spade.

It connected with a dull, hollow thud, like hitting a sack of wet sand. The woman's head went sideways, much too far sideways, and stayed there. She didn't fall. She didn't stop smiling. She straightened, slowly, her head coming back up with a soft grinding sound, and reached out again, and this time her fingers brushed Nadim's cheek.

*"Mind the step, my eyes."*

Nadim went rigid.

I watched it happen. The colour drained out of the side of his face where she'd touched it, as if someone had wiped away henna with a wet thumb and left the clay underneath. His eyes went wide and lost. The spade slid out of his hands and fell in the sand.

"I—" Nadim said. He looked down at his hands. "I was… I was doing something. What was I…"

He had forgotten. Not everything. Not yet. A touch from the emptied steals a little at a time: a memory here, a feeling there. I could see him searching for what he'd lost, patting at his own mind the way you pat your sash for a coin that isn't there. He had forgotten *why* he was standing there. He had forgotten there was a child crying behind the door who needed him.

The child cried harder. Nadim didn't turn around. He'd forgotten there was anyone to turn around for.

The grey woman reached for him again.

And Theyab moved.

---

He didn't know anything. He was fifteen and terrified, and the boy who'd thrown stones at him since he was five was being erased in front of him one memory at a time. Theyab simply could not stand there and watch.

So he ran into the middle of twenty of the emptied with a rusty knife.

He slashed at the grey woman's arm. The blade passed through her sleeve and hit something that wasn't quite flesh, cold and dense like the packed mud of a house wall. It left a thin line. No blood. Inside the line, for a moment, there was nothing at all. Not darkness. Nothing. Then it closed.

She didn't even look at him.

But the others did.

All of them, at once. Twenty grey faces turned slowly from the lamp in the window toward the boy in the middle of the lane, and twenty mouths stopped murmuring in the same instant, and the silence that came down was the loudest thing I have ever heard.

Then, one by one, they began to smile at him.

They were drawn to light. I've told you that.

And somewhere under Theyab's shirt, the feather mark had started to glow.

---

He felt it before he saw it. A heat in his chest that wasn't fear, though there was plenty of fear too. It was a *burning*. It started at the centre of the mark and spread outward along his ribs and up his throat and down into his arms, like swallowing a coal from the coffee hearth and feeling it catch.

The emptied came toward him. Their voices started again, all at once, all different, all close. *"Two annas and that's my last price." "Come in now, it's getting dark." "Mind the step, my eyes." "Who left the goat pen open?" "Hush. Hush."*

He backed up until his shoulders hit the wall of the house. The knife shook in front of him, and the burning in his chest was getting worse—

And the light came.

Not gold. Not at first.

It flickered out of his skin, out of the cracks of his clenched fists, and it was *grey*. A thin, dim, sickly grey, like light seen through a dirty water-skin. It sputtered. And the emptied didn't slow. They came faster, the way a falcon comes faster at a bustard that has started to limp. Some of them tilted their heads at it, almost tenderly, as if the grey light were a word in their own language.

That's what fear does to the Light. It makes it smell like home to them. I should have warned him. I couldn't have. I should have anyway.

The grey woman reached for Theyab's face. Her fingers were an inch from his eye. He could smell her: dust, and cold stone, and very faintly, underneath, the rose-oil she must have worn once on feast days.

Theyab shut his eyes.

And behind the door, the child cried out. One word, high and thin and broken.

"*Nadim!*"

Something in Theyab's chest *turned over*.

I saw his eyes snap open, and I saw him look past the emptied to Nadim. Nadim, standing lost and grey-cheeked in the lane, staring at his own hands. A boy who had forgotten someone he loved.

Theyab knew exactly what that was like. He'd spent his whole life being the someone nobody remembered.

He stopped thinking about himself.

And the light went *gold*.

---

It came out of him like the sun coming up over the dunes.

One moment the lane was dark and grey and full of smiling shapes. The next it was blazing. Golden light poured out of Theyab's hands, out of his chest, out of the cracks between his fingers, out of his eyes. It filled the lane from wall to wall. It lit every face, every grey robe and every white eye, and threw their shadows huge and black against the mud walls. For one instant I saw the shadows clearly, and they were not the shapes of the people casting them. They had too many joints. They were all leaning toward him.

The emptied *recoiled*.

They didn't scream. They can't. But they flinched back from the light the way your hand flinches from a hot stone, all of them at once, stumbling and lurching away into the dark. The grey woman with the bangles staggered backward. And for one heartbeat, I saw her eyes change. The white faded, and underneath was brown, ordinary brown, the eyes of a woman who had once worn silver bangles because she liked the sound they made when she ground the coffee.

She looked at Theyab. Her mouth opened. For a moment I thought she would say something new, something that was hers.

"*Where are my*—" she said.

Then the grey closed back over her like water over a dropped stone, and the smile came back, slow and wrong, and she said, *"Mind the step, my eyes,"* and was gone with the others into the night.

I have wondered for a long time what the end of that sentence was. Children, probably. It usually is.

---

The light faded.

It drew back into Theyab the way water drains into sand.

He slid down the wall and sat in the dirt.

His hands were shaking. There were thin golden lines on his palms, like the cracks in a glazed water-jar, faintly glowing. They hurt the way a burn hurts the next day. He stared at them as if they belonged to someone else.

And then it happened. The thing that would happen every time he used the Light, for the rest of his life.

He saw fire.

A full white moon, enormous, filling half the sky. A dune higher than any dune he had ever seen. And heat, inside him, rising up his throat like something climbing out. He could feel his own skin tightening. He could feel the fine hairs on his arms curl and shrivel. He could taste honey, and under the honey, smoke.

It lasted less than a breath. Then he was sitting in the dirt of Hilat al-Sarab, gasping, his hands slapping at his own arms to put out a fire that wasn't there.

He thought it was a trick of a tired mind. He would learn otherwise. I lay down beside him with my flank against his leg, and I counted, as I always do, and the number was still twelve.

---

The door of the well-keeper's house opened.

Saqi stood there, an oil lamp in one hand and a butchering knife in the other, his face white. Behind him, peeking around his legs, was Nadim's little brother Shibl, a boy of seven, his face streaked with tears.

Around the lane, other doors were opening. A light bright as noon had come through the shutters and brought the neighbours out.

They looked at the lane. At the empty sand where twenty of the emptied had stood, now marked with footprints that all faced the same way. At Nadim, still standing there, blinking slowly, one side of his face grey as ash.

And at Theyab, sitting against the wall with golden cracks glowing in his palms.

Nobody said anything for a long moment.

Nadim turned his head and looked down at Theyab. His eyes were clearing. What the grey woman had taken was seeping back. I saw him remembering the spade, the door, his brother crying. I saw him remembering the light.

He opened his mouth.

I like to think, even now, that it might have been *thank you*.

But he never got the chance, because that was when I did the thing I had told myself, for fifteen years, I would never do in front of them.

---

I looked at forty frightened faces staring at the boy against the wall, and I smelled what was rising off them, and it wasn't gratitude. It was the beginning of stones. I wanted them looking at something else.

So I climbed onto the well-keeper's garden wall, where the lamp could reach me, and I sat back on my haunches, and I lifted my muzzle at the black sky where the stars were going out, and I howled.

A saluki does not howl like other dogs. We bay, high and broken. But that is not what came out of me.

What came out of me was a man's voice.

An old man's voice, cracked and deep, rising and falling in the long wordless keening the Bedouin women make over a grave, except it was a man making it, and it was coming out of a dog. It went up the lane and over the roofs and out into the grey, and the grey didn't swallow it. It was the only sound in the world the grey didn't swallow. It went on far longer than any animal has breath for. Somewhere in it there were almost words, old ones. I don't always know what lives in the silver half of me.

The camels in the pens behind the houses screamed. Every goat in the village went silent at once.

When I stopped, nobody in the lane was breathing.

Then I jumped down off the wall and trotted, quite calmly, past Theyab, past Nadim, past the line of footprints, to the end of the lane where the grey began. I walked into it. I felt it close over me, cold and smooth, the way it always does, like being licked by something with no tongue. I walked ten paces in, and turned around, and walked ten paces out, and sat down in front of the crowd and scratched behind my ear.

I was still sand-coloured. My one amber eye was still amber. The grey had not taken so much as a hair.

That's the thing about the grey and me. It knows what I am, better than I do. It doesn't eat its own.

Somebody screamed.

I think it was Ghanim. It was a very high scream for such a large man.

"*Jinn*," a woman whispered. "There's a jinn in the dog."

"There's a jinn in both of them," said someone else, and I smelled it come off the whole crowd at once, the thing I'd been trying to stop, only worse now. I'd poured oil on it. Good work, Ghareeb. Fifteen years of silence, and the first time you open your mouth you hand them a reason.

And then everyone was shouting at once, and mothers were dragging children back indoors, and Sheikh Mazyad came barrelling down the lane in his nightshirt bellowing for order. Through all of it, Theyab sat in the dirt against the wall and stared at me with his glowing hands in his lap.

I came and lay down beside him again. Our shoulders touched.

"You," he said. His voice was barely a whisper. "What are you?"

I looked at him with both my eyes, the amber and the silver.

"You knew," he said. "All this time. You always knew what was coming. Didn't you."

I couldn't tell him anything. I never have been able to tell any of them anything. But I could have howled sooner.

I looked at the golden cracks in his palms, and at the mark on his chest, still faintly warm under his shirt. I looked at his face. It was very young, and very frightened.

I have sat beside eleven children like this, at the moment they understood their life was not going to be the life they'd planned. The Falconer asked me, very politely, whether he could go home now.

It doesn't get easier. You'd think it would.

I put my head on his knee. I wanted him to have had a little longer. That's all. Just a little longer, before it started.

He put his hand on my neck, the way he'd done in Ghanim's stall. He didn't say anything else. But his hand stayed there, and it was shaking, and he didn't take it away.

---

Somewhere at the edge of the village, out where the grey was thickest, I heard a sound that brought every hair on my body up at once.

It was a child's voice. Small and confused, calling out into the empty night.

"Yumma?" it said. "Nadim? Where… where is everyone? Who…"

A pause.

"Who am I?"

And then, from all around it, very softly, from the grey shapes standing out there in the dark, a dozen voices that were learning said it back.

*"Who am I?"*

*"Who am I?"*

*"Who am I?"*

Beside me, Nadim had gone completely still.

Because his little brother, the one who had been crying behind the door, wasn't behind the door any more. In all the shouting, in all the screaming about jinn and dogs, he had slipped out past his father's legs to find his big brother.

And he had walked straight into the grey, the way he'd just watched a dog do.

---

*End of Chapter Two*


---

# Chapter Three
## Monster

> *Beware the child who glows; the desert has plans for him, and the desert is always hungry.*
> — a saying of the grandmothers of Liwa, told to children who stay out after the sunset call of the doves

---

Nadim ran first.

I'll give him that. Whatever else he did, before and after, Nadim was the first one into the grey that night.

"*Shibl!*"

The grey swallowed the name. Sound doesn't carry in the Ifrit's curse. You shout, and the shout leaves your mouth and simply stops, like a stone dropped into wet date-paste. Nadim shouted his brother's name three times, and each time it died a hand's width from his lips.

And each time, somewhere deeper in, something said it back to him. Softly. Getting it a little more right each time.

Theyab was on his feet before he knew it. His legs were shaking. His palms still burned where the golden cracks ran across them. He looked at the flat dead place where the lane stopped being a lane, and took one step toward it.

I took his sleeve in my teeth.

Gently. I set my weight back on my haunches and looked up at him.

*No. You're half dead on your feet, and that is the curse itself. Not a story. You walk in there tonight and something else might walk out wearing you.*

He looked down at me. "He's seven," he said.

I let go of his sleeve.

He went in after Nadim, and I went in after him, because apparently that's what I am.

---

The grey feels like nothing.

It's the feeling of a tent after everyone you love has ridden away in the night without waking you, spread over the whole world. The colour goes first. Then the sound. Then the smells. Then, if you stay long enough, the reasons you came. It doesn't eat me. But every time I come out of it, I'm a little less sure which of my memories are mine.

Theyab walked with his hand flat against his chest, and the feather under his palm was the only warm thing for a hundred paces in any direction. Every few steps he whispered something, too low for the grey to steal.

*The sand remembers. The sand remembers.*

There were emptied in there. Standing between the grey houses like tent-pegs, faces turned up toward a sky with no stars left in it. As we passed, their heads came round, slowly, one after another, following the warmth of the mark. They smiled their wrong smiles and murmured their last sentences into the dead air. One of them was a boy no older than Shibl. He was saying, *"I'm not tired, I'm not tired, I'm not tired."*

Theyab didn't look at him. I was proud of that, and sick about it.

We found Nadim on his knees at the end of the lane, near the dead grey palms of the grove.

Shibl was sitting in front of him in the sand, cross-legged, the way small children sit when they've been told to wait and haven't been told for how long. Four of the emptied stood around him in a neat square, one at each corner, facing inward. Keeping him. The way you'd pen a lamb with four stakes and a rope.

He wasn't crying any more. That was what frightened me most. His face had gone the colour of old ash, and his eyes were open and calm and terribly white.

Nadim had him by the shoulders, shaking him gently, desperately.

"Shibl. Shibl, it's me. Look at me. *Look* at me."

Shibl looked at him. Politely, the way you'd look at a stranger who had stopped you in the souq at Buraimi.

"Who are you?" he said.

And around him, the four emptied said it too, in four different voices, at exactly the same moment. *"Who are you?"*

Nadim made a sound I've heard many times and still have no word for. It's the sound of something tearing that isn't cloth.

---

They got him out of the grey between them. Nadim carried him and Theyab walked behind, his glowing palms held up like lamps, and the grey drew back from them a little. The four emptied didn't follow. They stayed exactly where they were, standing around the empty square of sand, facing inward at nothing. I looked back once. They were still guarding it.

Saqi the well-keeper was waiting at the edge with a lamp. He took one look at his younger son's face and sat down in the dirt as if his legs had been cut from under him. Behind him people were gathering: Sheikh Mazyad, Ghanim with a camel-stick, men with old matchlocks they didn't know how to load in the dark.

Nadim laid his brother on the hard ground outside the house.

Then he turned and looked at Theyab.

It wasn't hatred. It was the look of a man at the bottom of a dry well, seeing a rope come down and hating that it was this particular rope.

"You did something," Nadim said hoarsely. "Before. You did something with the light and that woman's eyes went *brown*. I saw it."

Theyab said nothing.

"Do it again."

"I don't know how."

It was true. I stood at Theyab's side with every hair on my back lying flat, and I'd have given my silver eye for Nadim to know what that meant. He didn't. But he saw something in Theyab's face, and something in his own face crumpled.

"Please," said Nadim.

I don't think he'd ever said that word to Theyab before. It came out of him rusty and broken, like a well-wheel that hasn't turned in years.

"Please. He's my brother. *Please.*"

Theyab knelt.

---

I want to tell you he laid his hands on that child like the heroes in Bakhit's stories. His hands were shaking so badly that when he set them on either side of Shibl's face, the boy's head rocked. He was thinking of the emptied, and the grey, and forty people watching him from behind their lamps and their rifles. He was thinking about the word *jinn*, and what they'd do with it. He was thinking about himself.

So the light came grey.

It leaked out of his palms thin and sickly, the colour of the thing he was trying to fight. It lay over Shibl's face like dirty water. And Shibl, under it, smiled. Slowly. Too wide. The smile of the emptied, on a seven-year-old's mouth.

Theyab snatched his hands back as if he'd been bitten.

"I can't," he whispered. "I can't, I'm making it worse, it won't—"

I couldn't tell him. I've never been able to tell any of them. So I did what a dog does. I shoved my long nose up under his chin, hard, so his head came up, and I turned my muzzle and pushed, and pushed, until his face was turned away from the crowd and the lamps and the rifles, and there was nothing in front of his eyes but the child.

*Not them. They're not the ones who need you. Him.*

He looked.

And I watched it happen for the second time that night. I watched him stop being a frightened boy surrounded by people who hated him, and become a boy looking at a child who had forgotten his own name. A child who was, in that moment, exactly as alone as Theyab had been every day of his life.

The light went gold.

---

It came up out of him gently this time, softer and warmer than the blaze in the lane. It flowed from his chest down his arms and into Shibl's face like honey poured into warm milk, and the grey began to lift.

And Theyab saw.

He saw a baby born in the killing heat of the summer, red-faced and furious, while a boy of eight pressed his nose to the doorframe. He saw that same boy carrying the baby on his shoulders to the top of the dune behind the well, pointing up at the night and naming the stars the way the Bedouin name them. *That one's Suhail. When he comes up, the heat breaks. Those are al-Thurayya, the sisters. That one's the camel, see, and the little one following it is the calf. That's you. The little one, right next to me.* He saw Nadim sneaking him extra bread, and sitting alone behind the house one night after their father had beaten him, crying silently into his knees where nobody could see.

Nobody but the small boy watching from the window.

Then the pictures turned and ran forward. Shibl taller, with his brother's shoulders, laughing across a fire. Shibl's hands, older, lifting an oil lamp. Blurred, like a reflection in the well when someone drops a stone. The future always is. But warm. Full of colour.

And then, behind all of it, the other vision.

It didn't wait its turn this time. It came up through Shibl's future like a hand coming up through water and took hold of him.

The moon, enormous above him, so close it filled half the sky, pale and pitted like a skull left out on the sands. Sand beneath his own bare feet, higher than any dune he'd ever climbed, falling away on every side into the dark. His own arms, opening; he couldn't stop them opening. Light running out of the feather on his chest, cracking him from the inside, and this time he felt it. Not a beautiful heat. A real one. His skin tightening across his ribs like a drum-hide drying in the sun. His lips splitting. The wet in his eyes boiling, and the world going white and staying white. The smell of honey, and hot metal, and hair, and now, under it, the smell of meat on a spit.

He tried to scream and found that the fire had already taken that.

Somewhere below, someone was shouting.

He couldn't hear what. He thought, wildly, through it, that it might be his name. Then he thought: *which one?*

---

Shibl gasped.

A huge, ragged breath, the breath of a pearl-diver coming up from the bottom with his lungs on fire. The colour flooded back into his face, brown and alive. His eyes cleared, white to grey to the ordinary dark brown of a well-keeper's son.

He blinked up at the sky.

Then he saw Theyab.

I understand why he screamed. Imagine you're seven, and you've just come back from the place where there is nothing. The first thing you see is a face bent over yours, and the face is burning. Golden cracks running up its neck and across its cheeks like a clay jar about to shatter in the kiln. Light pouring out of its eyes like tears. Its mouth open in a scream with no sound in it. And beside it, a long pale dog that walked into the grey and came out again, staring at you with one amber eye and one silver.

Shibl screamed for both of us. He twisted out from under Theyab's hands and scrambled backward across the sand until he hit his brother's legs, and he clung to them and kept screaming.

Theyab's light snapped out like a blown lamp.

He knelt there with his hands still raised, slapping at his own arms, his own face, putting out a fire that had already gone. Tears were running down his cheeks. Some were his and some belonged to the vision, and he couldn't have told you which.

And across Shibl's cheeks, where Theyab's fingers had been, lay two thin golden lines, faintly shining.

Nadim saw them.

He looked down at his brother, clinging and sobbing, with gold scored across his face like a brand. He looked at Theyab, cracked and glowing and weeping light. He looked at me, the dog that had howled with a dead man's voice.

He was fifteen. His brother had forgotten him and been given back, and the price was written in gold across that small face, and Nadim understood none of it. He'd been taught his whole life to fear the strange thing at the edge of the village. That's what fear does, in the end. It reaches for the oldest word it knows.

"*Monster!*"

It cracked out of him like a camel-whip.

"Get away from him! You *monster*, you jinni's filth, get away from my brother, get *away*—"

---

It went into Theyab the way it always did. Like a hook.

He flinched. Just a flinch, as if he'd been slapped. Then he said, quietly, the way he'd said it at the well, the way he'd said it his whole life:

"I'm not a monster."

My hackles lay flat as a sleeping cat. Nobody was looking at them.

They were looking at the light still flickering in the cracks of his skin. And somewhere out in the dark, at the edge of hearing, something howled. Not me. A long thin sound out in the dunes that rose and broke like a child crying itself out, and was answered by another, and another. Jackals, you'd think, if you'd never heard a jackal. I have heard ten thousand jackals. These were not jackals.

"He's calling them," said Ghanim. His camel-stick trembled in his fist. "You hear that? Him and the dog. They're *calling* them."

"The grey came when he did," said a woman behind him. "Two nights ago. I saw him. Up on the dune. He was saying something to the wind."

A murmur ran through the crowd: the ugly sound of frightened people discovering they all agreed.

Theyab's hand went to his chest.

Because she was right. He had whispered his name to the wind, and the wind had whispered it back, and the grey had come. Every denial that came to his tongue, the feather burned before it reached his lips.

So he said nothing. And to the people of Hilat al-Sarab, his silence sounded exactly like guilt.

"Sheikh," said Ghanim.

Sheikh Mazyad looked at Theyab a long moment. I'll say this for Mazyad: he didn't enjoy it. He had the hopeless face of a man who has decided to feed one thing to the dark so the dark will leave the rest alone. Villages have been making that bargain since before there were villages. It has never once worked.

"Get out, Laqeet," he said. "Take your jinn-dog and get out of Hilat al-Sarab, and don't come back. You have no guest-right here. You never had."

The first stone came from somewhere at the back of the crowd.

---

It hit Theyab's shoulder. Not hard. A test.

He didn't move. He was still on his knees, looking at Shibl, who had stopped screaming and was staring at him over his brother's leg with huge wet eyes.

The second stone hit his ribs, and the third his cheek, and after that I lost count.

I barked at him. I barked the hunting bark, the one that means *now, now, it's running, go*, and I seized his shirt and hauled, and when he still didn't move I bit his hand. Not hard. Hard enough.

He got up. He didn't run. He walked, backward at first, then turned and walked away through the crowd with stones coming at him from every side. The crowd parted, then closed behind him, the way a fist closes. A rock struck his back just below the left shoulder blade. I heard the sound it made. It hit within a finger's width of the old scar, the one he'd carried since he was six.

He staggered. He kept walking.

He never raised his hands. Not once. I want you to know that.

A man lunged for Theyab with a camel-stick raised over his head like a sword. I took him by the ankle. To the bone. He went down howling, and I let go and kept walking, and I'm not ashamed of it, and I could smell from the blood whose ankle it was. I won't tell you. He has grandchildren. Some of them may be at this fire.

---

At the edge of the village, where the last houses gave way to the gravel, there was a small mud house with a baking-plate and a clay oven behind it.

The door opened as Theyab passed.

Umm Hanan came out. She was very small, with a face folded like a dried fig, and the black burqa'a of an old woman across her nose, and her hands still white with flour at midnight because she could not sleep and did not know what else to do with them. She didn't look at the crowd.

She walked straight up to Theyab and pressed something into his hands.

Flatbread, still warm from the plate, a thick round of it, and a fistful of dates, all wrapped in a square of faded indigo cloth knotted at the top.

He knew that cloth. He stared down at it, then up at her, and I watched him understand. Fifteen years of bundles with no footprints leading to them.

"Eat, child," said Umm Hanan. Her voice was dry and thin and fierce. "The sand remembers kindness too."

Then she went back inside and shut the door. I imagine she sat on her mat and shook for a long time. I imagine she tried to say the names of her dead, too, and found only two of them, and said them twice.

Theyab stood there holding the bread.

It was the first time in his life anyone in Hilat al-Sarab had touched him gently. He stood at the edge of the desert, bleeding from a dozen places, holding that bread against his chest as if it were a sack of silver thalers.

I pressed against his legs and leaned, the way a saluki leans, all our weight on you at once. *Come on. We can't stay.*

He walked out into the dunes. Behind us the lamps of Hilat al-Sarab went out one by one, as if the village had closed its eyes. Or as if something were walking from house to house, pinching the wicks.

I looked back once more. One lamp stayed lit. Small, low, in the window of the little mud house with the oven behind it.

---

The jackals found us before midnight.

I heard them first. Well, of course I did. A low sound under the wind, like breath drawn through teeth. Then a howl, close, from behind the dune to our left. Then another, ahead. Then one directly behind us, so near I felt it in the feathering of my tail.

"Ghareeb," Theyab whispered. He had never said my name like that before. He'd only heard Bakhit say it.

I pressed against his leg. *Keep walking. Don't run. Running makes them happy, and you don't want to see them happy.*

They came over the dune like smoke poured from a jug.

Jinn-jackals. Six of them, shaped like the golden jackals of the wadis, if a jackal were made of the darkness between the stars. Long and low, their bodies fraying into black mist at the legs and tail, too many ribs showing through the dark of their sides. Their eyes were coals, burning, the only colour in them. They spread out in a slow circle around us, and the sand didn't dent under their feet, and the air inside the circle went cold enough that Theyab's breath came out white, there in the hot months, on the edge of Liwa.

They track the Light. Every time a bearer heals, it rings out across the desert like a bell, and the jinn-jackals follow. Theyab had healed a child three hours ago. He might as well have lit a signal fire on Tal Moreeb.

He pulled his rusty knife. His other hand went to his chest, and he tried, I watched him try, to call the light.

It came grey. A sputter in the cracks of his palms, and then nothing. He was too frightened, too full of stones and the word *monster*. He'd just watched himself burn. Nobody reaches eagerly for the thing that's going to kill them.

The jackals saw it falter, and their coal-eyes brightened.

The first one sprang.

Theyab slashed at it. The knife passed through its shoulder like a hand through smoke, and the jackal hit him full in the chest and knocked him flat. Its jaws opened above his face. There was no mouth inside. No teeth, no tongue, no throat. Only more dark, going down and down, much farther than the jackal was long, and a cold breathing up out of it like the air from the bottom of a dry well where something has been lying a long time.

And at the bottom of that dark, very small and far away, I heard a voice. A child's voice. *"Who am I?"*

I hit it from the side.

I'm a saluki. I can take a jackal by the throat at a full run and break its neck with one shake. I took this one by the throat.

My teeth closed on something cold and slippery that wasn't there, and the jinn-jackal twisted in my jaws like a snake, and shook me off with a flick of its head, and I went tumbling down the slope.

Rolling through the sand, I felt something stir in the silver half of me, huge and hungry, uncoiling. It liked the jackals. It recognised them, the way you'd recognise cousins. My shadow, thrown long across the sand by the starlight, was for one moment longer than it should have been, and the wrong shape.

I didn't let it out. Not that night. That's a story for later, and you won't like it, and I like it even less.

As it turned out, I didn't need to.

Because that was when the spear came out of the dark.

---

One moment the jinn-jackal was crouched over Theyab's chest with its jaws spread. The next, there was a long shaft of pale wood standing straight up out of its back, and the jackal was coming apart.

It didn't bleed. Jinn things don't. It crumbled into a drift of black ash that fell over Theyab's face and chest like soot from a cooking pot, and it was cold, and it smelled, at last, of something: a lamp that has burned out and been left in a tomb.

Then the footfalls, soft and enormous, felt through my paws before I heard them.

A camel came over the crest of the dune at a full run, pale, almost white in the starlight, a long-legged Omani racer like something carved from bone. On its back rode a figure wrapped from head to foot in grey cloth. There was no face to see. Only the wrappings, and a headcloth wound into a veil, and a dark slit where the eyes should be.

The rider leaned from the saddle, pulled the spear from the sand in one smooth movement, and drove it through the second jackal without slowing. The third leapt at the camel's flank. The rider took it out of the air.

The last three ran, their coal-eyes winking out one by one in the dark.

---

The pale camel halted at the top of the dune perhaps twenty paces away. It blew through its nose and shifted its feet.

The rider looked down at us.

Theyab was sitting up in the sand, black ash all over his chest, knife still clutched in his fist. In the dark, the golden cracks along his jaw were faintly glowing.

The rider didn't move or speak. But I could feel the weight of that look from where I lay, and it wasn't a hunter's look. It went on a long time. Longer than it should have. The spear-hand, I noticed, was not quite steady.

The wind was coming down off the dune toward me. I put my nose up into it.

I'm not going to tell you what I smelled. Not yet.

"Who are you?" Theyab called.

The wind answered him. Nothing else did.

Then the rider turned the camel and rode away north into the dark, the long spear held upright like a war-banner. Soon there was nothing left but three drifts of cold black ash and a line of broad camel tracks the dunes were already filling.

"Who was that?" said Theyab.

I licked the ash off my foreleg. Who the rider was is a story for its proper time. If I tell it now you'll skip ahead, and then where will we be? Be patient. He was. Fifteen years, he was patient. Patience is just fear that has learned to sit still.

---

Theyab sat in the sand a long time after that, holding Umm Hanan's bread in his lap.

The golden cracks on his face and arms were fading slowly, the way colour goes out of a coal. His back was bleeding where the stone had hit. Every so often he turned his hands over, checking they were still skin.

He looked back toward Hilat al-Sarab. At its eastern edge the grey lay over the houses like a shroud over a man laid out for washing. Two lights still burned in that whole black huddle of mud and palm frond. One was the small lamp in the window of the house with the oven. The other was out at the far western edge, a single stubborn glow through the black goat-hair walls of a tent hung with herbs and bells.

Bakhit was awake.

Theyab looked at that light for a long time.

"I saw it," he said at last. He wasn't talking to me. He was saying it to the dark. "When I healed him. I saw myself. On a dune. I was burning, and I couldn't stop it, and I couldn't scream." His voice didn't shake. That was the worst of it. "All those stories. The Phoenix-Born. He *knew*."

I couldn't answer that. But I came and lay down against him, along the length of his leg, with my head on his knee, and every hair on my body lay flat and still in the cold.

Theyab stood up. He tucked the bread inside his shirt, against the feather mark, where it was warm. He picked up his knife.

Then he started walking back toward the light. Toward it. They always do. That's the whole trouble with them.

---

*End of Chapter Three*


---

# Chapter Four
## The Bell and the Feather

> *The bell rings for the liar, and the liar is often you. It does not ring for silence. Nothing does.*
> — words stamped around the rim of an old healer's bell, in a script no one in Liwa can read

---

His tent was gone.

Not burned. The people of Hilat al-Sarab were too frightened that night even for fire. Fire makes light, and light, they had just learned, brought things out of the dark.

So they had torn it down. Somebody had pulled up the pegs and slashed the nine patched scraps and trampled the whole thing flat into the gravel, the way you'd stamp on a scorpion you weren't brave enough to pick up. His blanket and his water-skin were gone. The little clay jar where he kept his annas had been smashed, and the annas taken. Fear doesn't stop people from being practical.

And all around the wreckage, someone had poured salt. A thick white ring of it, unbroken, glittering faintly under the stars. That's what the grandmothers do around a place where a jinn has been, so that whatever lived there cannot follow you home. In the middle of the ring, on the flattened cloth, someone had smeared a hand in soot. Five black fingers. *Keep out. What was here is not of us.*

Theyab stood looking at it for a while in the dark.

I stepped over the salt ring, quite deliberately, and lay down in the middle of the wreckage, and yawned. You see the trouble with salt, for something like me. It's mostly for show.

He didn't laugh. But he stepped over the ring after me. He knelt in the wreckage and began to dig through it with his hands, quietly, methodically, the way he dug through the ruins. A scavenger to the last.

He found the twist of waxed paper first. Four dates soaked in honey, the ones he'd been saving. Somebody had stepped on them. They were squashed flat and full of grit. He brushed them off carefully and put them inside his shirt with Umm Hanan's bread.

Then he found the cloth.

It was buried under the broken tent-pole: a square of grey fabric, softer than anything else he owned, woven with small strange marks no woman in Hilat al-Sarab had ever been able to name. The desert had wrapped him in it fifteen years ago. He'd slept with it under his head every night of his life.

He held it against his face. Then he went still.

"It smells of smoke," he said.

It always had. He'd simply never noticed, the way you never notice the smell of your own tent. Burned cloth, very old, very faint. I could have told him what kind of smoke, if I'd had the words, and I'm glad I didn't. I'd smelled it on the night the desert left him, rolling across the dunes from the place where his family had been, along with another smell, the one that clings to the robes of men who burn their own names.

He folded the cloth small and tucked it away with the rest.

"That's everything," he said.

A knife, a cloth, some bread, four ruined dates, and a dog. Fifteen years, and that was everything.

---

Bakhit was waiting for us.

Of course he was. The lamp was still burning when we reached his tent, the brass bells turning slowly on their threads in the draught. Ghaf wood smouldered in the hearth-pit, and tea was already poured, two glasses. Bakhit sat cross-legged on his worn rug, his milky eyes fixed on nothing, his white beard falling over his folded hands.

"Sit," he said, before Theyab had even ducked under the tent flap. "You're bleeding on my harmal."

Theyab didn't sit.

He stood in the opening with the firelight on his face. The golden cracks along his jaw had faded to a faint shimmer. His shirt was torn at the back and stiff with blood. He was shaking. Not from cold.

He was looking at the fire. At the little flames licking up the ghaf wood. He couldn't seem to stop looking at them, and he couldn't seem to bear it, and every time one of them flared, his hand jerked toward his chest.

"You knew," he said.

Bakhit turned his face toward the sound. For a long moment he said nothing.

Then he said, "Sit down, child. Let me see to your back. And then you can shout at me, and I promise to listen to every word."

The bells were silent. I lay down across the opening of the tent, and my hackles lay flat. Every word of it was true.

---

He sat, in the end. I think he was too tired not to.

Bakhit's hands were old and dry and very gentle. He peeled the torn shirt away and washed the wound with water from a clay bowl, then packed it with a paste of myrrh and burnt harmal seed. Theyab hissed through his teeth. He didn't cry out.

"Close to the old one," Bakhit murmured, his fingers moving over the pale scar under Theyab's left shoulder blade. "The sand does like to write in the same place twice."

He turned his blind face toward the opening where I lay.

"And you," he said. "You've finally let them see you, I hear. Howling like a dead man. Walking into the grey and back for an evening stroll."

I lifted my head from my paws and looked at him. My tail thumped the rug once.

"Ghanim screams very loudly," said Bakhit. Something moved at the corner of his mouth. "It's been a long time, Ghareeb. Since I've seen you do anything at all."

Theyab twisted round to stare at us. "You *know* him? Not just— you *know* him?"

Bakhit and I said nothing at the same moment, which was its own kind of answer.

I'll tell you this much, since you're listening and he wasn't: yes. We knew each other. We had known each other for a very long time, longer than the boy could have guessed. The last time Bakhit and I had stood in the same place under an open sky, he'd had eyes, and he'd been younger than Theyab, and he'd been running. He ran so fast that when he finally turned around, the last thing he ever saw was the place he was running from, white and enormous under a white full moon. Then the dark came down over his eyes and never lifted.

That's Bakhit's story, and he never told it to the boy. So I won't tell the rest of it to you. Not yet.

"Drink your tea," said Bakhit, and pressed the glass into Theyab's hands.

Theyab drank it. Black and bitter and smoky and green, boiled half to tar. He hated it as always. It was the first thing all night that felt like home.

Remember that. I asked you to, before.

---

"What am I?" he said at last.

The tea was finished. The fire had burned down to embers. Outside, the wind was whispering along the goat-hair walls, testing the seams.

"You know the story," said Bakhit.

"The Phoenix-Born." Theyab's voice was flat. "A piece of light gets put inside a child. The child carries it to the highest dune in the world and sets it free and drives the darkness back into the ground."

"Yes."

"That's a story. It's just a story you tell."

Bakhit said nothing.

"I'm not one of them," said Theyab. "I'm not—"

He stopped. His face twisted. His hand went to the feather mark and pressed hard, and I saw him bite down on the inside of his cheek until it bled.

It was burning.

He'd been about to say *I'm not one of the Phoenix-Born.* He'd wanted it to be true so badly he'd nearly said it. And the mark had caught the words before they reached his lips and set them alight, and for a moment the smell of scorched skin filled the little tent, sharp and unmistakable, and the bells all trembled on their threads without ringing.

The smell of the lie that had nearly got out of him went up my nose like a needle, and the growl came out of me before I could stop it.

He sat with his eyes squeezed shut, breathing through his nose, waiting for the pain to fade. When he opened them, there was a thin brown mark on his shirt, over his heart, where the cloth had singed.

"Ah," said Bakhit softly. "Yes. That's the trouble with you, isn't it? You can't even lie to yourself."

"I saw it," Theyab said. "When I healed Shibl. I saw myself on a dune. Burning." He swallowed. "Is that the story too?"

Bakhit's hands went very still in his lap.

"What did you feel?" he asked. Not *what did you see*. *What did you feel.*

"Everything," said Theyab.

The old man didn't answer. He turned his blind face away toward the wall of the tent, and for a moment he looked like someone listening to a sound very far off, a sound he had spent three hundred years trying not to hear.

---
