Bakhit rose. It took him a while. His knees cracked like dry ghaf twigs. He felt his way past the hanging herbs to an old wooden chest in the far corner, a sea-chest studded with brass nails gone green. Theyab had never once seen it open.

Bakhit opened it now. I caught a glimpse inside before his body blocked it. Other bundles. Small ones, wrapped in old cloth, laid in a row like loaves. A child's sandal of plaited palm-leaf, burned black along one side. I looked away. Some things I've had a lifetime to stop looking at, and I'm still not good at it.

Bakhit brought out a bundle wrapped in oiled leather.

"When the desert left you on the edge of this village," he said, "you were wrapped in that grey cloth you carry. And tucked inside it, against your chest, was this. Mazyad's father would have sold it. So I took it before anyone saw, and I've kept it for fifteen years. Open it."

Theyab opened it.

It was a feather.

Gold, or something like gold, as long as his hand and so fine you could see the firelight through its edges. Every barb was perfect. But it was broken, snapped clean across the middle, the two halves clinging together by a few bent barbs. Along one side the barbs were twisted and crushed, as if a hand had closed on it and tried to wring it in two. At the break, the gold had gone dark. Not tarnish. Grey, the dead flat grey of the lane outside Nadim's house.

And it was warm.

Theyab held it in both palms and felt the warmth soak into his skin. It was the same warmth as the mark on his chest. The same shape. The same fine lines. And as he held it, very faintly, it beat. Once. Twice. Out of time with his heart, as if it were keeping time with something else.

A feather ought to smell of bird. This one smelled of honey and hot metal.

"It's broken," he said finally.

"Yes," said Bakhit.

"Can it be fixed?"

"Some things mend themselves," the old man said, "when they're ready."

True. The bells didn't stir. My hackles stayed down.

I'll tell you now, because it doesn't spoil anything: it did mend. Slowly. One barb at a time. Every time the boy learned something true about himself, something hard and painful, the feather grew a little more whole. I watched it the whole way. I've watched eleven of them, and they all mend, and they're always whole on the same night, under the same moon.

A feather is mended so it can fly. That's all I'll say. Every time it healed, I hated it a little more.

---

There was a second gift.

Bakhit reached up without looking and unhooked the smallest bell from its thread, no bigger than the tip of Theyab's thumb, brass worn smooth with age, strung on a cord of braided camel hair.

"This one," Bakhit said, "is for you."

He tied it around Theyab's neck himself, old fingers fumbling at the knot.

"You cannot lie. You know that by now. But the rest of the world can, and the rest of the world will. This bell will ring when someone lies to you. Not loudly. Not always in time. But it will ring."

Theyab touched it. It lay against his chest just above the feather mark, cold against the heat of his skin.

"How do I know it works?"

Bakhit lifted his glass and smiled. "My tea," he said, "is the finest in all of Liwa."

The bell rang.

A small, clear sound, like a single drop of water falling into a brass bowl at the bottom of a well. And at the same moment the lie hit my nose, sour and harmless and a little bit funny, and my hackles went up from my neck to the base of my tail, and a growl rolled out of my chest, low and long, like a camel complaining at being loaded.

Theyab looked at the bell. Then at me. At the ridge of hair standing up along my back like the crest of a dune.

"He…" Theyab said slowly. "Ghareeb. When somebody lies, he…"

"Yes," said Bakhit. "Every lie in his hearing. He's always done it. You've seen it a thousand times and thought he just didn't like Ghanim." He drank his terrible tea. "Nobody likes Ghanim. But that isn't why. Now you have two ways of knowing when somebody's lying to you. Most people don't get one."

I could see Theyab going back over every time. Ghanim's stall. The sheikh's door. Fifteen years of a dog telling him, and him not knowing how to listen. Most people don't. It's one of the great sorrows of the world. Not the greatest. I'll get to that.

"And does he ever lie?" Theyab asked. "Can a dog lie?"

Bakhit laughed, a dry little cough of a laugh. "A dog? A saluki? Never. Everyone knows a dog can't lie. Look at him. He couldn't if he tried."

The bell didn't ring.

Of course it didn't. Bakhit believed it. The bell rings for the liar, and Bakhit was not lying. He simply didn't know.

I put my head back down on my paws.

Later, that would matter more than any of us knew. A dog who has never once lied only has to do it once.

---

"Would you die for them?" Bakhit asked.

He said it quietly. He was turning his empty glass slowly between his palms. He might have been asking whether Theyab wanted more tea.

Theyab looked up. "What?"

"The people of this village. Mazyad, who called you Laqeet and sent you out with no guest-right. Ghanim, who cheated you every week of your life. The children who threw stones when you were six, and the men who threw them tonight. Nadim." A pause. "Would you die for them?"

The tent was very quiet. Even the wind outside had stopped, as though it, too, were waiting for the answer.

Theyab opened his mouth.

I'll always see it. He opened his mouth to say *yes*, because it was what a hero would say, what the Phoenix-Born said in every story Bakhit had ever told him. The feather burned before the word was out. He flinched, and the singed mark on his shirt darkened.

He closed his mouth, and opened it again, to say *no*. Because they had stoned him. Because they had called him monster and salted the ground where he slept. Because he owed them nothing at all.

That burned too.

And between the two burnings, for just an instant, the embers in the hearth filled his eyes, and I saw him flinch from them as if they had reached for him. The vision came uninvited. Only a flicker. The white moon. The heat in his mouth. He made a small sound, like a child woken from a nightmare who doesn't yet know he's awake.

He sat with his hand pressed hard against his chest, eyes wet, mouth half open. Neither word would come.

"I don't know," he whispered finally.

The bell was silent. My hackles lay flat.

Bakhit nodded slowly, as if that were the answer he'd expected, and dreaded.

"Good," he said. "That's honest. Hold on to it. It won't be your answer forever."

"Why are you asking me that?"

Bakhit didn't answer. He turned the empty glass in his hands, round and round, round and round, the way the dunes turn, the way the moon turns. The way everything out here comes back to where it started.

---

It was almost dawn when Theyab asked the question that mattered.

"Where do I go?"

"Ghidd al-Zujaj," said Bakhit. "The glass ridge."

Theyab blinked. "That's a story too."

"Most things are, until you walk into them. It lies out in the sands, farther than anyone from Hilat al-Sarab has ever gone. Ruins, older than Ubar, and the sand around them is glass, black and green, in runnels and blisters, the way sand goes when lightning strikes it, only miles of it. The old caravan men say it's where the first bearer said no to the Light, and the sand around him melted." His blind eyes moved, as though following something a long way off. "There are carvings on the rocks there. The rocks remember. Everything you need to know about what you are is cut into them. The faces of the ones who came before you. Older than me. Older than the dog, almost."

I lifted my head and looked at him. *Almost*, I thought. *Careful.*

"How do I find it?"

"You don't. You walk, and the dunes find it for you. They'll open a way if you're meant to reach it."

Theyab turned the broken feather over in his hands. "Will you come with me?"

Something passed across the old man's face. Quick, gone almost before I saw it. But I've known Bakhit a long time, and I smelled it at the same moment I saw it. Shame. And under the shame, older and worse, fear.

"I don't leave the edge of the sands," he said. "I can't. The desert and I have an agreement. I made it a long time ago, and I'm still paying for it."

True. The bell didn't stir.

Theyab was quiet. Then he asked it. The same question he'd asked two nights ago, when his tent had still been standing.

"What happens to them? The Phoenix-Born. When they get to the dune and set the light free. What happens to them, after?"

I held my breath.

And Bakhit, who had sat among his truth bells for more years than the boy could have guessed and never told a lie that mattered, looked at him with his blind eyes and said:

"Go to Ghidd al-Zujaj. The answers there are older than mine, and truer. You'll understand it better when you see it carved."

The bell didn't ring.

Of course it didn't. Every word was true. My hackles never twitched. There was nothing in it for either of us to catch.

But I looked at Bakhit across that tent, and Bakhit, who could not see me, turned his face toward me anyway. And we both knew.

He had not lied to the boy. He had done something worse. He'd stood in front of the truth with a cloak spread out and let the boy walk past. He'd sent a child who can't read to a place made of writing, and he knew exactly what was written there.

That night an old man I had once loved like a son pushed a fifteen-year-old boy into the gap between *true* and *the whole truth*, and wished him well.

I didn't growl. Remember that, when you're judging him. I was lying right there, and I could have growled until the bells shook, and the boy would have understood. I didn't. I never have. Eleven times, I never have.

---

"May the sand remember you," said Bakhit at the tent flap. It's what the old ones say at partings in Liwa. The answer to it is *and you*, but Theyab didn't know that. Nobody had ever said it to him before.

The stars were fading. The eastern sky was turning the deep, bruised blue that comes just before the sun. Beyond the mud houses of Hilat al-Sarab the grey lay low and flat along the edge of the world. It hadn't moved in the night. It hadn't needed to. Along its edge, quite still, stood a line of small grey shapes. From that distance you might have taken them for the stumps of dead palms. They were all facing the tent.

"Will it follow me?" Theyab asked. "If I go. Will it leave them alone?"

"I don't know," said Bakhit.

The bell was silent. He truly didn't.

Theyab nodded. Then he did something I'd never seen him do in fifteen years. He stepped forward and put his arms around the old man, quickly and clumsily, like someone who's never been taught how. Bakhit went very still. Then his dry hands came up and rested on the boy's back, either side of the new wound, and held there a moment too long. The way you hold someone at a graveside.

"Go," Bakhit said roughly. "Before I make you drink more tea."

Then he bent, slowly, his knees cracking, and found my head with his hand, and held it. He didn't say anything to me. He didn't need to. He smelled of myrrh and smoke and three hundred years of being sorry.

We went.

---

We walked east into the deep sands as the dunes were finishing their night's work.

If you have never seen the sands of Liwa move, I'm sorry for you, and also not. In that last grey hour before dawn the dunes were settling into their new places, whole hills sliding sideways with a sound like a vast animal breathing in its sleep, the crests smoking with blown sand. Paths that had been there yesterday were gone, and new ones lay smooth and fresh between the dunes, untouched by any foot.

One of those new paths opened right in front of us.

It ran straight out from the edge of Hilat al-Sarab, between two great red-gold slopes, smooth as a swept floor. It hadn't been there an hour ago. Theyab stopped at the start of it and stared.

"It's for me," he said.

I sniffed the start of it. Nothing had ever walked on it. Nothing had been allowed to.

It was very straight. Desert paths are never straight. This one went east like a line drawn with a ruler, toward the place where the sun would come up over the far Hajar, like a road laid down to one single place by someone who did not intend you to wander. Like a throat.

He looked back once.

Hilat al-Sarab sat behind us in the blue light, small and dark and huddled around its well. He could make out the white ring of salt where his tent had been. At the western edge, a thread of smoke rose from Bakhit's fire straight up into the still air. And from the little mud house with the oven behind it, another thread. Umm Hanan was baking. It was the hour she always baked. Even then. Even that morning.

And along the eastern side of the village, grey.

He touched the feather mark. He touched the bell. He touched the folded grey cloth inside his shirt, where the broken feather now lay wrapped against Umm Hanan's bread, beating faintly, out of time.

"The sand remembers," he whispered.

Then he turned his back on the only home he'd ever had and walked out into the desert, and the dunes closed softly behind us. I listened for it, and I heard it: a long, slow, satisfied sound, like a mouth closing.

---

About an hour later, as the sun came up red over the eastern dunes, I caught a *dhub*.

It was a fat one, a spiny-tailed lizard as long as my foreleg, waddling out of its burrow to warm itself in the first light. The Bedouin roast them in the embers. I didn't. I took it behind the head with one snap and sat down in the middle of the path to eat it.

I took my time. I chewed with my mouth open. The spines of the tail made a wonderful noise, a wet, splintering crunch that echoed off the dunes, and I made sure to crunch every single one individually.

Theyab stopped and turned around.

"Do you have to?" he said.

I looked at him with the tail hanging out of my mouth. I crunched it.

"That's disgusting."

I swallowed. I licked my chops, both sides, slowly. Then I came and sniffed, very pointedly, at the back of his shirt, where Bakhit's paste was, and the blood, and the bitter mountain herb, and I sneezed, hugely, all over him.

He stared at me. And for a moment, just a moment, something happened at the very corner of his mouth. It wasn't a smile, not yet. He wouldn't truly laugh for a long time, not until he met someone who could make him. But it was the place a smile would go, one day, if things went well.

Things never go well. But they go, and sometimes there are smiles along the way, and I've learned to take those where I find them, like water seeping into a hole dug under a dune.

He turned and kept walking. I finished my lizard, and licked my lips, and trotted after him.

My tail was up. Salukis carry their tails low, mostly, curled under. Mine was up, feathering in the breeze like a flag. I'll let you work out why. And I'll tell you that I didn't let myself think the other thing, the thing I wanted to think, that this time would be different. I knew what my own hackles would do.

---

*End of Chapter Four*


---

# PART TWO: THE DESERT THAT WRITES


---

# Chapter Five
## Where the Dunes Speak

> *The sand writes; the wise read; the rest are buried. The sand writes on them too.*
> — a proverb of the old caravan guides of the Omani interior

---

The desert doesn't care about you. That's the first thing you learn out there, and the last thing most people forget.

It isn't cruel. Cruelty takes attention. If you happen to die in it, it covers you with the same patience it covers everything else, and a few hundred years later uncovers you again, just to see. I've seen faces come up after a winter of shamal winds, dry and perfectly kept, mouths open, as if they'd been calling for help the whole time.

Theyab knew the desert. It was the one advantage he had.

He knew to walk in the cool hours and lie up through the white glare of noon in the shade of a dune's slip-face, his headcloth over his eyes. He knew the bright yellow *handhal* gourds that trail across the hollows, round as oranges and bitter enough to kill you, and never to touch them however thirsty he was. He knew that a little brown water would sometimes seep into a hole dug at the bottom of a dune hollow, if you dug, and waited, and were lucky. And he knew to lick the dew from the undersides of flat stones at dawn.

---

We walked for six days.

The straight path lasted only the first morning. After that, every dawn the way ahead looked different. Theyab would climb the highest crest he could find and turn slowly in a circle until something told him which way to go. A tug in the feather mark. Some mornings he stood up there a long time, and I knew he was wondering whether the thing pulling him was the One who made the sands or something else, and whether there was a difference.

At night he kept us straight by the stars, the way every herder's child in Liwa learns before he can count: al-Thurayya rising in the east before dawn, the Jadi that never moves, and low in the south, burning red through the dust, Suhail. The caravan men say a man who keeps Suhail in his eye will never be lost. They don't say what happens to a boy who's being pulled by something else.

On the second evening, with the sky going violet, he looked at me for a long time. Then he said, "Bakhit says you understand. Everything."

I looked back at him.

"All right," he said. "Then we'll do it like this. If the answer is yes, you bark. Once. If it's no, you look away. Can you do that?"

I barked. Once. It echoed off the dune, and a sand-grouse went up out of a hollow with a clatter of wings.

He stared at me with his mouth open. None of the others had thought of it. Eleven children had talked to me, and wept on me, and slept against me, and not one of them had thought to ask me anything that could be answered.

Remember that bark. One bark, *yes*. I would use it to lie to him exactly once.

"Are you a jinn?" he asked, much more quietly.

I didn't bark. I didn't look away. I lay there with my chin on my paws and looked at him with my amber eye and my silver one, and after a while he looked away instead.

"Why did you watch me? All those years. In the village. Why did you stay?"

There's no bark for that. I came and lay down against his side, the whole length of me, the way a saluki lies against the person it's chosen, and put my head on his knee.

He put his hand on my neck.

"Because somebody had to," he said, answering for me.

My hackles lay flat as water. He looked down at them. He knew what that meant now. He didn't ask anything else for the rest of the night.

---

He didn't sleep much. When he did, he woke with his hands slapping at his chest, putting out a fire. Once he sat up and said, perfectly clearly, "It's so high up," and didn't remember it in the morning. I did.

He ate Umm Hanan's bread a little at a time, a strip at dawn and a strip at dusk, and a date from her fistful, the stone kept in his cheek for an hour after to fool a thirsty mouth. He shared it with me every time.

The bread was what kept him on his feet. I want to be plain about that. There was no water to speak of out there, and no food for a boy with a rusty knife who can't run down a hare. There was a round of flatbread and a fistful of dates wrapped by a frightened old widow at midnight, and it was enough. Exactly enough. Not a strip more. I've thought about that many times since.

On the fourth night he finished it. The four squashed honey-dates from the wreck of his tent he left where they were. He knew harder days were coming. He kept the indigo cloth, folded small in with the grey one, and didn't say why.

---

The dunes spoke on the fifth night.

We'd stopped at the foot of a long crescent ridge, the slip-face rising steep and smooth above us like a wall. Theyab had scooped a hollow in the sand to sleep in. The moon had risen late and lopsided. I lay with my nose on my paws and watched him not sleep.

Then the sand began to move.

Something small. Close. On the smooth face of the slope in front of us, the sand was rippling, the way a camel's flank ripples when a fly walks on it. Fine lines were drawing themselves across the slope. Curves and hooks and dots and long straight strokes, crossing and joining, and they weren't being drawn on the sand. They were being pushed up from beneath it, from inside the dune, as if by a single fingertip moving very slowly just under the surface. As they formed, they began to glow.

Gold.

Theyab sat up so fast he sprayed sand over me.

A line, then a second line beneath it, glowing across the dark face of the dune like fallen stars. They had the shape of meaning, the way a voice in the next tent has the shape of words even when you can't make them out. They ran from right to left, the way real writing does.

I could hear it, too. A faint dry whisper as the strokes moved, like many reed pens on old paper, as if a great many hands were writing at once under the sand, all of them patient, all of them in the dark. And it smelled, not of a lie, but of honey, and hot metal.

Theyab got up and stood at the foot of the slip-face for a long, long time.

Then he said, very quietly, "I can't read."

Nobody had ever thought to teach a foundling scavenger his letters, and it had never mattered before.

It mattered now. I could see it go into him like the word *monster* did.

"Ghareeb," he said, without turning round. "Can you read it?"

I could have looked away. He'd have believed me. I barked.

"Then tell me what it says."

I didn't move.

"You can't," he said. "Of course you can't. You're a dog." He laughed, a short ugly sound with nothing in it.

It was partly that. But it was also the thing I'd tell him if I had a tongue for it: it isn't written for me. The sands write, and the bearer reads. Never the dog. And I didn't want him to know what it said. Not that night. Not any night, if I could help it, and I knew I couldn't.

"It's written for me," he said, "in a way I can't understand. So it's written for nobody."

He said it flatly. That was worse.

"Something forgot me in the sand when I was a baby," he went on. "And now it writes me letters I can't read. That's very funny. That's the funniest thing I've ever heard."

He turned his back on the dune, walked to his hollow and lay down in it, facing away from the light.

The writing glowed on behind him for a long time. The whispering went on with it. Then, slowly, the sand smoothed itself over, and the gold faded, and the sound stopped. Except that just before it stopped, the strokes under the sand went still all at once, every one of them, the way a majlis goes quiet when the man everyone was talking about walks in.

---

I'll tell you something now that I didn't tell him.

I read it. Of course I did. The bottom line was a single word, written large.

I'm not going to tell you what it was. It isn't mine to tell. But I'd seen that line before, in that place on the dune, at the bottom, written large. Eleven times, over eleven different children, and each time the word was different, and each time it meant the same thing.

I lay awake the rest of that night watching the place where it had been, and I don't think I've ever hated the sky quite so much. It writes like someone who loves you. It writes like that every time.

If he'd been able to read it, would anything have been different? No. I know better. Nothing would have been different. It never is. That's not despair, by the way. That's arithmetic.

---

The ghoul came the next afternoon.

We were crossing a wide flat basin between two lines of dunes, a dead-pale floor of hard sand crusted white where some ancient water had dried and left its salt. It was the hottest hour, there was no shade, and the far dunes looked close. They always look close.

A lake lay across the middle of the basin, silver and still, the dunes upside down in it. Every child in Liwa knows that lake. It's the *sarab*, the mirage. His village was named for it. He didn't even look at it.

Then I heard a camel bell.

*Tonk. Tonk. Tonk.*

Theyab stopped. Out in the shimmer, at the edge of the false water, there was a man with a pack-camel, waterskins sagging dark and swollen against its sides. The man wore a white robe and a red-checked headcloth, and he was waving, big slow friendly waves, the way you wave across a wadi to a cousin.

"Ya walad!" he called, clear and warm. "Boy! Come! You're thirsty, I can see it from here. I have water. Come and drink. Come and be my guest."

Water, and the guest-right: the two holiest words in the sands, side by side. Nobody had ever called him guest. He took a step forward. Then another.

Every hair on my body went up at once.

All of me, and the growl that came out hurt my chest. The wind had turned and I could smell the man with the camel, and he smelled the way the inside of a lie smells. Not sour. Rotten. A lie so complete there was nothing else in it at all. No sweat. No camel. No man.

The bell around Theyab's neck began to ring, over and over, tiny and frantic, like a bird beating itself against a cage. Theyab stopped.

"Come," said the man, smiling. "Come, little one. Don't be shy. Your mother's here. She's been waiting for you so long."

The bell rang so hard it jumped on its cord.

And I looked at the man's feet.

The hem of his robe swung as he walked toward us, and under it, where his feet should have been, the sand was printed with small, neat hooves, like a donkey's. Every grandmother from the Hajar to the Empty Quarter teaches that sign: the *ghoul* can wear any face and any voice in the world, your father's, your wife's, a lost traveller's, but it cannot hide its feet.

The man stopped walking.

He looked down at his own hooves, as if he'd only just noticed. Then he looked up at me, and the smile on his face kept going. It went wider, and wider, past the corners of his mouth and up toward his ears, and the face around it began to sag inward like a waterskin being emptied.

"Run," Theyab whispered. He'd seen it too. "Ghareeb, *run*—"

We ran.

The ghoul dropped.

Man and camel together, straight down into the basin floor, the way water drops into dry sand. The bell rang once more, under the ground. *Tonk.* Then the sand behind us bulged in a long low ridge, like a blanket over something moving, the pale crust going black as if something were soaking up through it.

Then the ground *erupted*.

It came up out of the basin like a fountain of black sand, rearing, impossibly long, and it was still, in some horrible way, trying to be a man. There was the suggestion of a headcloth. There were arms, too many of them, too long. But its body was the black sand itself, endlessly pouring and re-forming, and where its face should have been there was a wide blunt shape that opened in the middle like a split seam, and below it all, holding it up, a cluster of thin grey legs ending in small neat hooves.

And through the pouring black of its body, as it rose, I saw what it had eaten.

A camel's ribcage, turning slowly inside it, the bell still on its rotten halter. A child's anklet of silver bells. A face, pressed against the inside of the sand as it poured past, the mouth open in exactly the shape of the faces the desert sometimes gives back. They went round and round inside it like things in a slow flood, and none of them would ever come out.

The *ghilan* lie under the dunes the way sand-skinks do, and when they feel something warm walking above, they put on a face it will trust and call it in. Then they swallow it, and keep it, and go back down.

It came down at us like a falling tower.

Theyab threw himself sideways. The ghoul struck where he'd been and went straight into the ground, and the whole basin floor heaved and knocked him flat. He came up spitting salt, knife in hand.

It burst up in front of him, a black wall of hissing dust, and he skidded, fell, scrambled back. It was herding him, the way a falcon works a hare out onto the open flat. It knew this basin. He didn't.

The feather mark was burning through his shirt. He held out his hand, and the light came.

Grey.

He was terrified. A thin sputtering light flickering in his palm like a lamp-wick in a draught. The ghoul didn't even pause. It reared up over him, blotting out the sun, the split in its face opening wide, and the air that came out of it was cold and wet and smelled of the bottom of an old well, and of the people in it. Out of that split, in a dozen voices, a man's and a woman's and a child's, it said, kindly:

*"Come and be my guest."*

And that was when I did something stupid.

---

I went for it.

I didn't think. I ran straight up the loose pouring sand at the base of its body, between those thin grey legs, and I bit one of them just above the hoof.

It tasted of nothing. Dust and cold. And, very faintly, of the people inside.

A coil of black sand whipped round and caught me, pouring and squeezing at once. I felt myself being pulled in and down, toward the darkness under the dunes, and something brushed against my side in the black, and it was a hand. It was dry and light and it closed, gently, in the feathering of my ear, the way you'd stroke a dog that had just come in out of the heat. Welcoming me home.

I heard Theyab shout my name.

Through a gap in the black sand, I saw him get to his feet.

And I saw the light change.

It's the same every time. One moment it was grey, the light of a boy afraid for himself. The next, he was looking at a skinny old dog being dragged into the sand, and he forgot his own fear the way you forget a thorn in your foot when the tent is on fire.

The light went gold.

It roared up out of his chest and out of his hands, and he didn't throw it, exactly. He *reached* for me, and the light reached with him.

It hit the ghoul square in the body.

Where the gold touched the black sand, it turned glassy and then, with a sound like a thousand tiny bells breaking, it shattered. The coil around me burst apart, and the dry hand let go of my ear last. Above me, the light raced up the ghoul's body the way fire races up a dead palm. The whole vast shape shrieked, high and thin, and in that shriek, I swear to you, there were voices. Many. One of them, I'm almost sure, said *thank you*. Then it collapsed in a slithering rain of black glass and ash that pattered over the basin for a long time.

What it had been keeping fell with it. Bones. The bell, rolling to a stop at Theyab's feet. The child's anklet, its little silver bells full of sand.

When it was over, Theyab was on his knees.

Golden cracks ran up his forearms and down his neck. And for a moment his eyes went far away and white, and his mouth opened, and I knew he was seeing it again, the moon, the height, the heat. It had come without a healing, this time. Just from using the Light. It was learning the way in.

He came back. He looked at me.

"Are you hurt?" he said.

I spat out a mouthful of black grit. I'd just been half eaten by a ghoul and petted by a dead man, and he wanted to know if I was *hurt*.

"Are you?" he said. "Bark for yes."

I looked away.

He let out a long breath and sat back on his heels.

"Good," he said.

And I had to keep looking away, at the black glass scattered across the basin, because there was something in my eye. Sand, probably. There was a great deal of sand.

---

I didn't smell him until it was too late. I'll never forgive myself for that. Light and noise bring things. We sat among the black glass, catching our breath, and the first I knew of it was a shadow falling across Theyab's shoulders, and a smell. Burned cloth. And under it something sharper and stranger, like hair singed in a lamp, like ink boiled off a page. I've smelled it in a great many places. It's the smell of a name being burned.

He looked up.

A figure stood on the dune-crest above us, against the white glare of the sun.

Grey robes, a grey headcloth wound round and round, and under it, where a face should have been, a mask. Smooth and white and perfectly blank, like a polished camel's shoulder-blade. Two narrow slits for the eyes, and behind them, darkness. Where the edge of the mask met the skin of his neck, the skin was raw and red and puckered, as though the mask had been worn so long it had begun to grow there, and every time someone had tried to lift it the face had come with it.

In each hand he held a blade, long and slightly curved, and grey. Not the grey of steel. The grey of the curse, dull and dead; they had been quenched, I'd learn later, in the Ifrit's own ash. Looking at them was like trying to remember a dream.

The Nameless. That's what they call themselves, the men in white masks. They burn their own names in a fire and take numbers instead, and they serve forgetting the way other men serve their tribe. They hunt the bearer the way the jinn-jackals do. But the jackals are animals. The Nameless know exactly what they're doing, and the worst of them do it kindly.

This one, we'd learn later, they called the Seventh.

He came down the slope.

---

He moved like water running downhill. Theyab scrambled up with his rusty knife and blocked the first blade, more luck than skill. The second blade was already coming low, at his ribs. He twisted. It missed his side by a breath and opened his sleeve from wrist to elbow.

It didn't touch his skin. It didn't need to.

Theyab staggered. For an instant his face went blank and puzzled, as if he'd walked into a tent and forgotten why. I saw him reach for something in his mind and close his hand on nothing.

That's what the ash-blades do. They don't cut flesh so much as memory. Even a whisper of one, passing close, takes something with it, and it doesn't leave a hole you can feel. It leaves smooth sand.

He swung at the masked figure, wild and desperate. The Seventh stepped inside the swing, hooked his ankle, and put him on his back. One grey blade came down and rested against his throat.

Theyab froze.

There was light in his hands, but it was grey again, flickering, useless. He'd spent everything on the ghoul. And blades quenched in the Ifrit's ash, I should tell you, are the only thing in the world that can cut the Light.

I went for the back of his knee at a full run. A knee caught me in the ribs and sent me tumbling. When I got up, nothing had changed. The white mask looking down. The blade against Theyab's throat, and a thin line of grey spreading on the skin beneath it, like frost.

The Seventh didn't move.

---

He didn't press down. He didn't draw back. He stood looking at Theyab through the slits of that blank white mask. I'm not sure there was anything left behind them to see.

Under the burned cloth and the burned names there was something else on him, faint and old and buried very deep, a smell I knew and couldn't place. It frightened me more than his blades.

It went on long enough for the hot wind to drop.

And then, under the mask, very softly, he began to hum.

You could barely hear it over the hiss of settling sand. A few notes, low and slow, climbing. A pause. The same notes again, a little higher, like something lifting off the ground. Like wings. It came out of a mask with no mouth, as if from somewhere very far back in him, a room he'd been locked out of.

Theyab's eyes went wide. I don't think he knew why. But something in his face changed, the way the grey woman's eyes had changed in Hilat al-Sarab. Something long buried turning over in its sleep.

I knew that song.

I'm not going to tell you who used to sing it, or to whom. Not yet. I stood in the sand with my fur up along my spine and made no sound.

Add it to the list. It's getting long, I know. Everything I don't tell is a stone in my belly.

The humming stopped.

The Seventh took the blade away from Theyab's throat.

He stepped back. One step, then another. The blank white mask tilted slightly to one side, as if he were asking himself a question and had forgotten what questions were for.

His hand came up to the edge of the mask. Touched the raw skin there. Pulled, a little, the way you'd worry at a scab.

Then he let go, and turned, and walked away up the dune, and over it, and was gone.

---

Theyab lay on his back in the sand a long time, staring at the sky.

"He let me go," he said finally.

I came and stood over him and licked the grey line on his throat. It tasted of cold. Of nothing.

"*Why?*" he said. "Do you know why?"

I looked away.

True, as far as it went. I knew a great many things about that masked figure. But why he lowered his blade that day, and would again and again, I truly didn't know.

Theyab turned his head and looked at me. He was learning to read the shape of what I didn't do.

"Do you know who he is?"

I got up and shook the sand out of my coat, very thoroughly, from my ears to the end of my tail, for a long time.

He looked at me a moment longer. I saw him decide not to push.

He got up, a pale mark on his throat like a line of old frost, and started walking toward the far dunes, and somewhere to sleep that wasn't the middle of a ghoul's larder.

That night, by a fire of dry *'arfaj* twigs, he sat for a long time with his knees drawn up, frowning into the flames.

"Ghareeb," he said. "There's something missing."

I lifted my head.

"In my head." He pressed the heel of his hand against his forehead. "There's a place where something was. I keep going back to it. I don't know what it was. I don't know if it was important." He looked at me, and his eyes were frightened in a way the ghoul hadn't made them. "How can I miss something if I don't know what it is?"

I knew what it was. It was the taste of Bakhit's tea. Black and bitter, smoke and something green. He'd hated it and loved it every night for years, and now it was gone, and he would never know it had been there. They always start with small things.

I put my head down on my paws and closed my eyes, which is how a dog says *go to sleep*.

He didn't. He lay on his side with his back to the fire, and after a while, very softly, almost without meaning to, he began to hum. A few notes, low and slow, climbing.

He got it wrong. He didn't know the rest.

I lay beside him and didn't correct him. I could have howled it for him, the whole of it, in a voice that wasn't mine. I didn't. And out in the dark, beyond the edge of the firelight, the sand shifted very slightly, as if something under it had turned over to listen.

---

*End of Chapter Five*


---

# Chapter Six
## The Valley of Echoes

> *Shout your name in the wadi, and it will shout back your fear. Whisper it, and it will learn it.*
> — a warning the goatherds of the Hajar give their sons, and their fathers gave them, and no one remembers who gave it first

---

The sands ended on the ninth day, and the ground fell open like a wound.

One morning we were walking the long red spine of a dune. By noon the sand had thinned to black flint that cut my pads, and the Hajar stood up in front of us out of the haze. Imagine the bones of the world left out to dry: folded rock the colour of rust and old liver, split by dry riverbeds that run with water one day in ten years and on that one day drown whoever is camped in them.

The one in front of us was Wadi al-Sada. The Wadi of the Echo.

Its mouth was a crack in the mountain no wider than a tent is long. The walls went up so sheer and so high that the sun could not reach the bottom, and the cold came out of it to meet us, carrying the smell of stone and standing water and, underneath that, something sweetish and old.

At the mouth, half buried in drifted gravel, stood a cairn of piled stones. Around its base lay a ring of small things. A sandal with the thong worn through. A wooden coffee cup. A child's clay whistle with the end bitten off. A string of blue beads, the kind mothers tie on babies against the eye. Offerings, or things dropped by people running. The desert doesn't label what it keeps.

Theyab looked at the cairn a long time. Then at me.

I had sat down. I want to be honest about that. I am a saluki, and a saluki will run a gazelle to death across open sand and think it sport, but I sat down in the gravel at the mouth of Wadi al-Sada and I put my ears flat to my skull and I would not get up.

"You don't want to go in," he said.

No. I did not.

"We have to, though." He touched his chest, where the feather mark lay under his shirt. "It pulls that way. Straight in."

It did. And on every other side the dunes had crept up behind us in the night, steep and soft and very patient, the way a crowd closes round a man the moment before they pick up stones. He knew about that. He had the scar under his shoulder blade to prove it.

---

I'll tell you what I would have told him, if I'd had a voice a man could hear.

The old men of Hilat al-Sarab say it too, around the coffee hearth, when the boys are listening: *Do not speak in Wadi al-Sada.* Not a word. Not a shout. If you fall, curse inside your head. If you see something *else*, hold your tongue harder still. Because the wadi answers. Not with your voice. With what's under it. And it remembers what it hears. Say your name in there, and the wadi keeps it, and goatherds will hear it calling up out of the rock fifty years after you're bones.

Theyab had heard those stories, sitting outside the edge of the men's majlis on cold nights, too low to be invited in. So when he looked down at me and said, quietly, "We don't talk in there. Do we," I only looked back with my one amber eye and my one silver one, and thumped my tail once on the gravel.

He had learned, by then, that I understood him. He hadn't yet learned how much.

I had been to Wadi al-Sada before. More than once. The last time, the boy I walked beside had not walked out.

I got up. I went in first. That's the only kind of answer a dog can give.

---

It was cold at the bottom.

Not cool. *Cold*, the creeping wet cold of a cistern, which is wrong in this country the way a smile is wrong on a corpse. There were pools in the shadows, black and still, fringed with maidenhair fern and the pink poison flowers of oleander, and a wild fig had forced its roots into a crack and grown pale and twisted, reaching up for a sun it would never touch. Somewhere ahead, water dripped. *Tik. Tik. Tik.* The sound came back from everywhere at once, like a great many small fingers tapping on stone.

We walked in silence.

The walls rose higher as we went. The sky narrowed to a ribbon, the way the light looks from the bottom of a well you have fallen into. Our footsteps came back to us from the rock, every one of them. They came back a little *late*. And there were more of them than we'd made.

Theyab noticed. He stopped once, and the footsteps went on for three more paces without him, and then stopped too, as if whoever was making them had paused to listen.

He looked at me. I looked at the path. We kept walking.

High on the walls were the old carvings the mountain people left before anyone remembers: stick camels, men on horses, an ibex. Those I didn't mind. The other marks were newer. Handprints in the soft red stone, long streaks of them, as if someone had walked along this ledge with a palm on the wall, slowly sinking. And scratched again and again into the rock, in many different hands, a few short words I couldn't read. I didn't need to. I've seen people write them before. They are names. It's what people scratch when they're trying to keep hold of their own.

Around midday the path narrowed to a ledge along the wall, with a long drop to the dry bed on one side. There were small bones down there. Goat, mostly. Mostly.

A loose stone turned under Theyab's foot.

He caught himself. He didn't fall. But he gasped, the small sharp breath anyone would make, and with it, before he could stop it, came a sound.

"*Ah*—"

It went up the wall like a bird let out of a box.

We both froze.

The echo came back. It should have come back as *ah*. That's what echoes do. They return what you give them.

This one didn't.

"*Thrown away*," said the wadi.

---

It wasn't loud. That was almost the worst of it. It was soft, like someone whispering a finger's width behind his ear, and it was in his own voice. Theyab's voice, but younger. The voice of a very small child who has been crying a long time and has stopped expecting anyone to come.

"*Thrown away*," it said again, farther up the gorge, and again, fainter, from down among the little bones. "*Thrown away. Thrown away.*"

His hand went to his chest. He pressed on the feather mark so hard his knuckles went white as the bones below.

"*Mistake*," said the wadi.

This voice was older. A man's voice, and I didn't know it. It said the word the way you'd speak of a camel calf born with a twisted leg. Without anger. A thing not worth the water.

"*Mistake. Should have left him in the sand. Should have left him for the foxes.*"

I pressed my shoulder against the back of his knees and pushed. *Walk.* It's what a dog does with a flock that wants to stop at the wrong place. He stumbled forward a pace, and another, and kept going.

Something moved in a hollow in the rock ahead. A shape the size of a crouching man, pressed back into the dark with its face to the wall. As we passed, it didn't turn. But under the other whispers I heard it panting. Panting like a dog. My own breath, already taken, and worn like a borrowed cloak.

"*No name*," said the wadi. "*No name. Nobody's son. Nothing.*"

Then came a voice I *did* know. Everyone in Hilat al-Sarab would have known it. Sharp, cracking, the voice of a boy who'd been taught his whole life that he was better than someone, and had chosen Theyab to be the someone.

"*Monster.*"

Theyab stopped walking.

---

"*Monster*," said Nadim's voice, high up the gorge. And then the others joined it, dozens, from every ledge and crack in the stone. The whole village, the way they'd shouted with the stones in their hands. Sheikh Mazyad. Ghanim the trader, who'd cheated Theyab on every scrap of copper he'd ever dug out of the ruins. Saqi at his well. Small children who'd learned the word before they learned to count.

"*Monster. Monster. Monster.*"

And under it, very faint, the thud of stones. Over and over. Landing on something soft.

He turned to face it.

I knew what was going to happen. I'd known since the cairn. You can warn a boy against a thing all you like, and some boys will do it anyway, because they would rather die than let the thing stand unanswered. In my experience, they usually get the chance.

"*I'm not!*" Theyab shouted.

It tore out of him, loud and raw. The wadi caught it in its red mouth and turned it inside out like a waterskin and threw it back.

"*NOT A HERO*," the wadi roared. "*NOT A HERO. NOT A HERO. NOBODY. NOBODY. FORGOTTEN.*"

It was his own voice again. Not a child's now. His own, exactly as it was, and that was the cruellest thing of all. Because none of the others had been true. Not *thrown away*, not *mistake*, not *monster*. Those were other people's words. This one was *his*. It was the fear he kept in the deepest cellar in him, under all the dreams he'd dreamed in his patched tent of being the hero in the old men's tales.

That he would do every terrible thing asked of him, and in the end it wouldn't matter. Nobody would remember him at all.

"*FORGOTTEN*," howled the wadi. "*FORGOTTEN. FORGOTTEN.*"

And then it did something I had never heard it do. It said his name.

Not *Laqeet*, foundling, which is what the village called him when they called him anything. The name he had given himself at seven, sitting on the lip of Saqi's well. The name he had never once said to the wind.

"*Theyab*," the wadi whispered, almost tenderly, from every wall at once. "*Theyab. Theyaaab.*" Tasting it, the way I'd taste a track. "*Wolves. He named himself for wolves. Who was Theyab? Nobody. Nobody remembers Theyab.*"

He went down on his knees on the ledge with both hands over his ears. It didn't help. It was coming from inside his hands.

---

I bit him.

Not hard. On the wrist, the way a mother dog takes a pup that's wandered toward the fire. Enough to sting. He yelped, and the yelp went up the gorge too, and the wadi sent back something wordless and wet, like someone crying with their face pressed into the dirt.

Then I took his sleeve in my teeth and I pulled. I braced all four feet on the ledge and pulled the way I'd pull down a hare that didn't want to die, and I growled at him through the cloth. Not the growl I keep for liars. The other one. The one that means *now*.

He got up. He walked.

And I'll tell you something I'm not proud of. When I growled, I growled a little louder than I meant to.

The wadi heard me.

"*Again*," it whispered.

I stopped dead.

I have never had a voice. Not one a man could hear. Whatever I am inside, it has only ever come out as a whine, a bark, a growl, the noises any dog makes, and once, in Hilat al-Sarab, a dead man's keening with no words in it. But I knew that voice the way you know the smell of your own bed. Old and thin and very, very tired. It was the voice I talk in, in here, to you. The wadi had reached down my throat and found it.

"*Again. Again. Again.*" And then, so faint I've sometimes told myself I imagined it: "*Too late. Too late. You're always too late.*"

Theyab turned his head and looked at me. He'd heard it too. He couldn't have known whose it was. But he looked at me as if he did.

I didn't look back. I kept my eyes on the path and my mouth shut, and I walked, and after a while he walked too.

Behind us, as we went, I heard the wadi practising. Trying out what it had learned that day, side by side, the way a child tries new words. *Theyab*, it said. And then a dog's short, sharp bark, perfectly made. *Theyab.* Bark. *Theyab.* Bark. Learning us. For later.

---

The wadi let us go a little before sunset.

The walls opened slowly, the way a fist unclenches when its owner has finally died, and spilled us out onto a wide plain of gravel where a few *samr* acacias grew crooked and black against the sky, every one of them leaning away from the gorge. There was a pool under an overhang of rock. Real water, brown and cold and tasting of iron, like a split lip. Theyab drank until he was sick, then drank again, more carefully. I drank beside him with my ears turned backward the whole time, toward the crack in the mountain.

The sun went down. The stars came out. The moon rose late, a little thinner than the night before, a pale paring low over the gorge.

Theyab sat with his back against a rock and the grey cloth from Hilat al-Sarab around his shoulders. Very quietly, he said his own name under his breath. Once. Testing. As if checking it was still there.

From the mouth of the wadi, faint across the gravel, something said it back.

He didn't say it again.

"Was it true?" he whispered. Not to me, quite. To the dark, and to me in it. "What it said. That I'll be—"

He couldn't finish. Not because the mark would burn. Because he was afraid it wouldn't.

I lay down against his side, the full length of me. The wadi doesn't tell the truth. It tells you what you're afraid of. That isn't the same thing. But nothing a dog can do says that, so I gave him what I had: warmth, and weight, and the sound of my breathing.

After a while he put his hand on my neck, in the feathered fur behind my ear, and left it there, and slept.

---

He dreamed.

He was standing on a plain, and the plain was grey.

Not the grey that had crept up to the palms of Hilat al-Sarab. Softer. A flat endless grey that ran to every edge of the world. No sky. No sound. Not even his own heartbeat. He put his hand to his chest to feel for it, found nothing, and his hand sank a little way into himself as if he were made of ash, before he snatched it back.

And there were goats.

Black goats, the long-eared mountain kind, moving slowly across the plain around him, grazing on nothing. They were made of grey mist. When they turned their heads he could see through them. They had no eyes, and their mouths kept chewing, and when one came close he heard that it was chewing words. Tiny, mumbled, half-swallowed words. *Mother. Water. Home. My name is—* and then the grinding of teeth, and the word was gone.

Among them, leaning on a long herding stick, stood a boy.

Small. Smaller than Theyab. Twelve, perhaps, thin as the stick he leaned on, in a patched brown *thawb* that was the only colour in that whole grey world. Barefoot. Face turned away.

"They always come back," the boy said. "Have you noticed? However far they stray. You just stand still and wait, and they come back to you. Everything comes back to the dark in the end."

His voice was soft and ordinary and a little hoarse, as if he hadn't used it in a very long time.

Theyab found he could speak. "Who are you?"

The boy frowned, the way you frown trying to remember where you put something down.

"I don't remember," he said. He didn't sound upset. "I used to. I'm almost sure I used to." He shrugged his thin shoulders. "It stops hurting, after a while. Not having one. Like taking off a sandal that's been rubbing."

He turned his head.

It was a boy's face, thin and brown and dusty, with a narrow chin and a crooked front tooth. All but the eyes. Where his eyes should have been there were two small round holes, very neat, as if someone had pressed them out with a thumb. Through them Theyab could see the grey. Going on forever inside his head.

"I heard you in the wadi," the boy said gently. "*Forgotten.* You're right to be afraid of it. I'm sorry. I wish someone had been honest with me, when I was where you are."

"I don't know what you mean."

"Don't you?" The boy smiled. It was a kind smile. That was the terrible thing. The kindest smile anyone had given Theyab in his whole life, except Umm Hanan's, at the edge of the village, when she thought nobody saw her leave the bread. "They've told you the tales. The bearers of the Light. They burn like the fire-bird and rise again, they save everyone, their names are sung forever." He shook his head. "No one remembers the bearers."

The grey goats had stopped chewing. They had all turned their eyeless faces toward Theyab.

"In the great city they light lamps for them, once a year," the boy said. "The Night of Lamps. A whole sky of little flames. And not one person holding one could tell you a single bearer's name. Not their faces. Not whether they screamed at the end." He tilted his head. "They did, you know. Some of them. You'll want to know that later, when it's your turn. You won't be the first to scream."

"That isn't true."

The words came out of Theyab, and as they did, the feather mark gave one sharp throb. Not quite a burn. Not quite. A warning.

The boy saw it. Something in the grey behind his eyes stirred, like a fish turning in deep water.

"Isn't it?" he said softly. "You can't even say it without it hurting."

He came closer, walking without leaving footprints, and stopped an arm's length away and looked up with those two holes full of nothing. Up close he smelled of goat and woodsmoke and something burnt. Hair, maybe.

"Ask the dog," he whispered. "Ask the dog how many. Ask him what he isn't telling you."

And he reached one small dusty hand toward Theyab's chest, toward the feather, and where his fingers came near, the light under Theyab's skin began to go grey. Not dim. *Grey*. And the boy's fingers, where the light touched them, grew a little more solid. A little more real. As if he were drinking.

"There," the boy said, with his eyes closed. "Oh, there. I remember warm."

---

Theyab woke with a shout.

He was sitting bolt upright on the gravel with the grey cloth tangled round his legs, and his hands were glowing. Not gold. Grey. A dim cold sickly light, like the light on that endless plain. It flickered between his fingers and went out.

The stars were fading. Dawn was close.

And there, in the dust beside where his head had lain, were marks. Small, cloven, careful. Goat tracks, in a ring all round him, as if something had stood over him in the night and grazed.

I was standing over him with every hair along my spine up, growling at the tracks, a low ragged sound with something underneath it, like a second animal growling inside the first. The tracks smelled of goat, and smoke, and burnt hair, and under all of it a boy I had loved before the mountains had names.

"What is it?" Theyab whispered. "Ghareeb. What is it?"

He told me the dream. He told it to a dog, in the dark, because there was no one else, and I listened to all of it with my hackles up and my lips drawn back off my teeth. The grey plain. The goats chewing words. The boy with eyes like thumbprints. *No one remembers the bearers.* *Ask the dog how many.*

When he said that, I turned my head away.

"You know him," Theyab said slowly. He was watching me. He'd begun, by then, to watch me the way you watch weather. "Don't you? You know who that was."

My silver eye ached as if someone had pressed a coal into it.

"He said ask you how many. How many what?"

I turned my back on him and walked away across the gravel, up onto a tall flat rock at the edge of the plain, and I sat there with my tail wrapped round my feet and my face to the mountains.

He didn't follow. He'd learned that much about me, too.

---

I sat on that rock until the sun came up.

I looked at the sky the whole time, until al-Thurayya faded last of all. I don't know who listens up there. I know only that it's where I look when I need to be angry at something that doesn't answer.

*You let him in,* I thought at it. *Nine days out of Hilat al-Sarab, and you've already let him walk into the boy's sleep. Did you open the tent flap for him yourself?*

The sky said nothing. It never does.

I'll tell you what I couldn't tell Theyab, since you're listening too.

His name was Ghadban. *The angry one.* His mother named him for the way he came out of her, red-faced and roaring. He grew up quiet instead. A small, poor goatherd's boy in these same mountains, a very long time ago, before the glass ruins were glass. He had a crooked front tooth and a patched brown *thawb* that had been his father's, and he was afraid of the dark, and he talked to his goats because nobody else would listen.

Nobody except me. I was the dog that ran his flock. I slept across the door of his father's stone hut and walked behind him up every goat track in the Hajar.

He was the first. The very first child who ever carried the Light. And I loved him the way you love the first of anything, before you've learned what loving things is going to cost you.

At the end, he stood on a hill with the light pouring out of his chest and his eyes still brown and still his, and he looked at me and said, *You knew. You knew, and you walked me here anyway.*

He wasn't wrong. That's what the wadi found, on the ledge, when it spoke in my own voice. *Again.* That I would walk beside one more child to that dune, and watch it happen again. That I'd be the one at their heel every time, the faithful dog, bringing the lamb.

Behind me, I heard Theyab get up and come and stand at the foot of my rock. He didn't say anything. I noticed he was careful not to step in the goat tracks.

After a while, I climbed down.

He crouched so our faces were level. He had a dog-bite on his wrist, already bruising, and he hadn't mentioned it.

"Ghareeb," he said. "Was he lying? The boy?"

I looked at him. I didn't growl. I didn't do anything at all.

A dog can only say so much. And every liar I have ever known has told the truth sometimes. That's how they get you to keep listening.

He looked at me a long time, reading what wasn't there. Then he nodded slowly, as if I'd answered, and stood.

"There's a caravan track somewhere north of here," he said. "The old men talked about it. Buraimi to the coast. We need water. And people." He tried to smile. "So I'll stop talking to things in my head."

We walked out across the gravel toward the acacias, with the sun rising red on our right and Wadi al-Sada silent at our backs.

It was silent. I listened very carefully. But I could have sworn, just as we passed beyond hearing, that somewhere deep in the red stone, in a child's voice, sweet and patient and in no hurry at all, something was calling.

*Theyab. Theyab. Come back, Theyab. We'll wait.*

And after it, perfect, a dog's short bark.

---

*End of Chapter Six*


---

# Chapter Seven
## The Caravan and the Beast

> *When the sky goes dark at noon, couch your camel and cover your face. If you hear your mother calling from inside the dark, do not answer, and do not turn around.*
> — a saying of the caravan men between Buraimi and the coast, older than any caravan still on the road

---

You smell a caravan long before you see it.

Camel, mostly. Cud and camel piss. Then dung smoke, and cardamom, and dried shark from the coast, and sixty men who haven't washed in a month because water is money. After a wadi full of voices, it smelled like hope.

We found the track in the middle of the morning, worn by a thousand years of feet and marked with little cairns, running north and west toward the sands and the sea. And on it, strung out like beads on a long brown thread, came the caravan of Abu Kharbash.

Sixty camels roped nose to tail in strings of seven, carrying dates from Buraimi to the coast. Riders with old Martini rifles across their backs. A merchant from Sharjah on a white riding camel with a hooded saker falcon on his fist. And two salukis trotting along the flanks, who looked at me as I came over the ridge and then looked away, quickly, the way dogs look away from something they'd rather not know about.

Theyab watched it pass with his mouth slightly open. I sneezed at him. It's the closest a saluki comes to sarcasm.

---

Abu Kharbash was a broad, bald, sun-blackened man with a beard dyed orange with henna and a voice that could have cracked flint. *Father of scratches*: one cheek was ploughed with old white lines where a leopard had caught him in the mountains as a boy. He looked Theyab up and down the way he'd look at a camel he suspected of being lame.

"Name?"

"In my village they called me Laqeet."

"Foundling. That's not a name, that's a complaint." Abu Kharbash spat. "One of my boys ran off at the last well. You'll work for water and dates and a place by the fire. You don't like it, the desert's that way." He squinted down at me. "Now *that's* a dog."

He crouched and took my muzzle in one hard hand and turned my head to the light. Then he saw my eyes. One amber. One clouded silver, like a coin left in the sea.

He let go rather fast, and wiped his hand on his *thawb*. "My grandmother would say there's a jinn sitting behind that eye. I'll give you twenty thalers for him anyway."

"He isn't mine to sell," said Theyab.

Abu Kharbash laughed and stood, already bellowing at someone about a loose saddle girth. "Keep him away from my falcon," he called back. "And at dusk, *couch the camels in a ring*, all of you, or by my beard I'll leave you for the wolves."

*Couch the camels in a ring.* He said it at every halt, the same way, like a man saying his prayers. You'll see why I remember.

---

Theyab worked.

He'd spent his life doing the jobs no one else wanted, and a caravan is nothing but those. He loaded bales and gathered dung for the fires and never complained. He kept his ghutra wound high around his face and his shirt tied close at the throat, so nobody would see the faint glow of the feather mark after dark.

He was happy. I don't think he knew it. I tried hard to remember his face like that, the way you'd press a flower in a book you knew was going to burn.

On the second night a boy came and sat beside him at the fire. Jasir, the youngest of the camel men, sixteen or so, with a gap in his front teeth and far too much laugh. He rode a milk-pale she-camel called Shaha and talked about her the way some men talk about their mothers. He held out a cloth of dates without being asked, and after a moment Theyab took one.

"You're from the Liwa villages," Jasir said. "I can tell. You walk like the sand's going to bite you."

"It does, sometimes."

Jasir laughed, easy and loud and unworried.

They talked late. Or Jasir talked, and Theyab listened. A lot of grey out east this season, Jasir said. A well-keeper's family near Ibri found round a cold hearth with their coffee cups in their laps, smiling at nothing, saying *more coffee, more coffee, more coffee*. And deep in the sands, if you believed the old men, the sheikh of Ubar had thrown his own eldest son out for hiding one of the emptied in the palace and trying to cure him.

"You can't cure the grey," Jasir said. "You take them out to the sands and leave them, quick, before they start smiling at you."

Theyab's hand had gone to his chest. I lay with my nose under my tail. Every word was true as Jasir knew it. My hackles stayed flat. I've never been sure that's a comfort.

---

On the third day, at noon, the falcon wouldn't fly.

It went up three wingbeats and came straight back to the merchant's fist and sat there with its feathers flattened, shivering, its head turned east.

Then the camels knelt. No one told them to. All down the line they sank onto the gravel, groaning, and stretched their necks flat along the ground and closed their eyes. The way camels lie for the knife at a wedding feast.

The two salukis crept under the nearest camel with their tails between their legs. I stood out on the open gravel and howled, long and high and broken, the warning-cry I hadn't given in a hundred years.

The wind had stopped. Out here the wind is never still. Now the air hung hot and dead, and the sky was clear in every direction but the east, where, low on the horizon, there was a line of black.

At first the caravan men took it for a sandstorm, and relaxed a little. A sandstorm you know. Abu Kharbash was already bellowing: *couch the camels in a ring.*

But a storm runs *with* the wind. This came out of air with no wind in it, and it was black, like the inside of a closed eyelid. And as it came, it drew itself up. The middle rose, and kept rising, and became a pillar.

The little dust devils dance across the flats every summer afternoon, and the old women say every one has a jinn riding in it, and spit, and call them *zawba'a*.

This was a zawba'a the height of a mountain.

It stood on a single spinning foot that tore the gravel up in a roaring skirt, and went up from there, black and turning, until its top was lost above the sun. It *walked*. It came across the plain the way a man walks, swaying from side to side, and it was silent. The silence came ahead of it like a smell.

And the light was going.

Noon, and the sun at its height, and the light was draining out of the world. The colours went first. The red of the camel blankets. The orange of Abu Kharbash's beard. Everything went the colour of old bone. Then the shadows went, all at once, as if something enormous had put its mouth to the sky and was drinking the light out of it like milk from a bowl.

"When the sky goes dark at noon," said Jasir, very softly.

I took Theyab's sleeve in my teeth and pulled him away, so hard the cloth tore. Nobody else moved. Nobody ever does.

---

It hit us like the end of the world.

The wind came first, a wall of it, knocking Theyab flat and rolling me twice across the gravel. Then the dark came down over the caravan like a lid on a cooking pot.

And inside it, something moved.

I have seen most of the jinn's children. I'd seen nothing like this. No one who has seen it all at once has kept their name.

It was the whirlwind, and it was something *in* the whirlwind. Its body was the black wind itself, spinning and folding on itself like a great gut, and in the folds were the suggestion of arms, huge and slow and jointed in too many places, trailing out of the spin and feeling across the ground the way a blind man feels for a dropped coin. One passed over us. It was *heavy*, the way grief is heavy, and where it brushed the top of a fallen bale the dates inside went grey and soft and slumped like wet bread.

High up in the spin there were lights. Red ones. Dozens, scattered across the black, blinking slowly and not together. Some were large as a well-mouth. Some were small and set in pairs, at about the height of a man's head, and there were a great many of them, and they went round and round with the wind, round and round, and every time they came past they were looking.

Eyes. All the eyes of everyone it had ever taken, still open in there, still looking out.

And below them, in the very heart of the spin, a hole.

Not a mouth. A mouth has lips. This was a great round silent place where even the darkness was darker, the still eye at the centre of the whirlwind, and everything went into it. The roaring wind went in and didn't come out. Gravel flowed toward it in long hissing streams, like water to a drain. My ears popped. Something warm ran out of my nose. And the hole made a sound, at last, the only sound I ever heard it make.

It *breathed in*.

---

The front of the caravan went first. The dark closed over Abu Kharbash's kneeling camel, and when it lifted there was smooth gravel, raked as neat as the floor of a mosque.

Then the camels behind. The zawba'a took the end of the rope and *pulled*, and they went up one after another like beads off a string, still kneeling, eyes still closed, turning slowly in the air as they rose into the black. Not a sound out of any of them.

The men screamed. And then the screams changed.

They didn't stop the way screams stop when someone dies. They *frayed*. A man would be shrieking for his brother, and halfway through the brother's name the scream would forget what it was for. It would trail off into a puzzled little note, then a hum, then nothing.

That's what a zawba'a does, a great one, one that has fed long enough. It doesn't eat you. Eating would be kind. It swallows you up into the spin, and inside the spin is the Ifrit's grey, all of it at once, like being drowned in a well of everyone's forgetting. Whatever comes out the other side, if anything does, walks on your legs and doesn't know your face.

Theyab was on his feet. I don't know how. His ghutra had torn away and the feather mark was blazing through his shirt, so bright it lit a circle of gravel round him in the dark.

And high up in the spin, the red eyes turned toward it.

All of them. At once. The great ones and the small ones in their pairs. They stopped going round with the wind. They held still while the black turned around them, and they looked at him.

I put myself in front of him, a dog the weight of a goat snarling at a mountain, and behind my silver eye something woke and *pulled* against my skin like a camel against its hobble. *Let me out. I could hold it.* I didn't. I was more afraid of that than of the zawba'a.

Behind me, the light in Theyab's hands came grey.

It came grey and thin and terrified, a dead lamp-wick in a dead wind. I could feel the fear go down into him, like a stone dropped down a well that never hits the bottom.

And then the voices started.

Soft and close, the way the wadi had spoken, but these weren't echoes. These were the people it had taken, and it was *wearing* them.

"*Couch the camels in a ring*," said Abu Kharbash, very calmly, from the black. "*Couch the camels in a ring. Couch the camels in a ring.*"

"*Where is my*—" said a man's voice. "*Where is my*— *Where is my*—"

And a child's: the merchant's little daughter, going home to her mother on the coast. I never knew her name. "*Yumma, it's dark. Yumma, it's dark. Yumma, it's dark.*"

Last words. That's all that's left of you, inside it. Your last words, going round and round in the black like a bell that won't stop swinging.

Through the flying sand I saw Shaha down, kicking, and Jasir was pinned beneath her by one leg, screaming the only name he had for Theyab. *Laqeet! Laqeet!* And the foot of the whirlwind was sliding toward him across the gravel like a tide that knows exactly where you've buried your feet.

Theyab ran.

He ran into the wind, toward the boy who'd shared dates with him, and in those few steps the light went from grey to gold, like a coal catching. He threw himself down over Jasir and the kicking camel with his arms spread wide, and the light came out of him in a dome. A shell of gold, closing over the three of them like cupped hands over a moth.

The zawba'a came down on top of it.

---

I was outside the light. I want you to know that. I lay flat in the lee of a dead camel with my silver eye screaming at me, and I stayed small. I saw the whirlwind stand on the little gold glow, its spinning foot grinding down until the gold went thin as a soap bubble, and the eyes crowded down the spin toward it, round and round.

He told me much later, as if a dog could tell anyone, that from inside the dome it wasn't black at all. Close up it was *grey*, soft and folded and turning, like the inside of a lung. And pressed against the gold, whirling past and catching, were faces. Hundreds. Abu Kharbash, his orange beard gone the colour of ash. The little girl, her small hands flat on the gold in front of his face as the wind dragged her past and brought her round again, saying *Yumma, it's dark* with no sound, smiling, and not knowing what a smile was for.

They were trying to get in. Not to hurt him. They were cold, he said. They wanted the warm.

He held the dome with the gold cracking open across his skin like the glaze on a dropped jar. He said it was like being leaned on by every lonely person who had ever lived.

And then, slowly, like a tide going out, the darkness lifted.

The zawba'a walked away west, swaying, its red eyes winking out one pair at a time. It was still noon. It had taken less time than a pot of coffee.

The caravan of Abu Kharbash was gone. Sixty men. Sixty camels. No bodies. No blood. One wooden saddle-frame lay on its side, still rocking, *creak, creak, creak*, in the dead air.

Far off across the gravel, small grey figures were walking away, slow and aimless, the way the emptied walk. Their arms didn't swing. One of them had an orange beard. As he went, faint on what little wind there was, I heard him calling, cheerful and flat: "*Couch the camels in a ring. Couch the camels in a ring.*" He didn't look back.

In the middle of that great swept emptiness, a small golden glow went out.

---

Theyab lay in a drift with his arms still spread, golden cracks even across his eyelids, his teeth clattering. All round him, pressed into the sand in a ring, were hundreds of small hand-shaped hollows, where they had leaned.

Of Jasir there was only a hand, sticking up out of the drift.

Theyab dug with his bare cracked hands, and I dug beside him the way I'd dig out a jerboa, until we found a face, and he dragged the boy out by his collar.

Shaha was dead beneath him. Her open eyes were grey all the way through.

Jasir was alive. Mostly.

His leg was broken. And the left side of him, arm and shoulder and face, had gone grey where the edge of the dark had licked him before the gold closed over. His left eye was white as a boiled egg. His mouth smiled on that side only, slack and sweet, and he was mumbling a name, half finished, trailing off. "*Sha*— *Shaha*— *Sha*—"

I put myself across his lap and leaned all my weight on his arms. *Don't. You have nothing left.* He moved me off gently, and laid his hands on Jasir's face anyway. Of course he did.

---

It came gold this time without being asked. Warmth, flowing out of him and into the boy, and the grey began to lift, peeling back from Jasir's cheek the way frost peels from a stone on a winter morning.

He saw Jasir's life. A small boy refusing to cry when a camel calf stepped on his foot. Then forward: Jasir older, lean and grey at the temples, riding ahead of other people through a dark place with his hand raised, picking a way between walls. A guide. The people behind him were frightened, and following.

And then the other vision.

Longer now. Much longer. It didn't flash. It *took* him.

The full moon above him, so vast and white it seemed to be falling. A dune under his bare feet, higher than any dune he'd ever seen, red sand going up into the sky. He knew its name now, though no one had ever told him. *Tal Moreeb*. The Hill of Fear. He felt his arms rise. And then the fire started in his chest, and this time it didn't stop at a flash, and this time he *felt* it.

Not light. Fire. The skin of his chest tightened and split. The heat climbed his throat and filled his mouth so he couldn't scream, and came out through his eyes. He smelled his own hair burning, and under it something like meat on a spit. Below him, a man's voice, cracked down the middle, was shouting a name.

Then he was on his knees on the caravan track, retching, with the smell of burnt hair still real in his nose. It was his own. The front of it had crisped where the light came out of his eyes.

---

Jasir opened his eyes. Both of them brown.

The grey was gone. His leg was straight. His lips were split and bleeding, his tongue thick in his mouth. He tried to speak and nothing came out but a dry click.

Theyab reached behind him for his waterskin.

I knew what was in it. I'd listened to it slosh against his hip for two days. A mouthful, perhaps two. All the water he had in the world. The caravan's water had gone up into the black, we were three days from any well I knew, and the sun was climbing toward the worst of the afternoon.

He didn't think about it. I watched him not think about it. He pulled the stopper with his teeth and held the skin to Jasir's mouth, and held the boy's head up so he wouldn't choke, the way you'd water a lamb. Jasir drank all of it, and went on sucking at the empty neck, and Theyab let him.

It was a poor thing, that skin, old goat leather patched twice, with a knot of red wool round its neck that Umm Hanan had tied there so it could be told from the others at the well. Theyab put it into Jasir's hands and closed the boy's fingers round it.

Then Jasir looked up.

At Theyab, cracked all over with glowing gold, light still leaking from the corners of his eyes, his hair smoking. At the swept and empty plain where an hour before there had been everyone he knew.

I watched him put it together. I watched him do the sum, the way frightened people always do, quick and wrong.

"It looked at you," Jasir whispered. His voice was wet now. Theyab's water had given it back to him. "I saw it. All those eyes. It came, and it stopped, and it looked straight at *you*."

Theyab didn't answer.

"It came for you." Jasir was scrambling backward, the empty waterskin clutched to his chest. "Didn't it? You brought it. You're what it was looking for. Everyone's gone, Abu Kharbash is gone, Shaha's gone, they're all *gone*, and you—"

His face twisted.

"*Monster.*"

Theyab flinched. Like a slap.

"I'm not a monster," he said.

The feather mark didn't burn. He couldn't have said it if it weren't true. But I'll tell you the thing I have never been able to forgive, and Theyab saw it, because by then he had begun to watch me.

When Jasir said *it came for you*, I didn't growl.

My hackles stayed down. Flat. Not a single hair along my spine moved.

Jasir got to his feet and ran, stumbling, out across the empty gravel with Theyab's waterskin bouncing against his back, and he didn't look back. Not once.

I hope some old man by some fire tells it differently now. I hope he tells it wrong.

Theyab knelt where Jasir had left him and watched him go, with his hands open on his knees and nothing in them.

---

The ground began to sink and turn under us like water going down a drain. From the bottom of the hollow, very faintly, came the creak of the saddle-frame, still rocking, somewhere underneath.
