Far off to the west, the black pillar had stopped walking. Its red eyes had turned back toward us.

And very faintly, on no wind at all, a little girl's voice: "*Yumma, it's dark.*"

I barked at him, high and sharp. *Up. Now.*

He tried. There was nothing left. The ground slid out from under him, pulling him toward the centre of the hollow, toward the creaking. I took his collar in my teeth and pulled, and felt the silver half of me straining to *grow*. *Let me. I could hold the whole desert in my jaws.* I almost let it.

The sand was at his waist. At his chest.

Then came the soft heavy padding of a camel at full gallop.

---

The pale camel came over the lip of the hollow at a dead run. A hand wrapped in grey cloth closed on Theyab's arm and hauled him up across the saddle face down.

"The dog—" he choked. "Ghareeb—"

The rider caught me up by the scruff, the way the Bedu lift a saluki pup, and dropped me across the boy, and the camel was already running.

Behind us, the zawba'a was coming back, faster now, eager, swaying as it walked. And on the low ridge between us and it, a grey robe, flapping. A smooth white mask with no face on it at all.

The Seventh was standing in our path.

He drew one of his two grey blades as the camel thundered down on him, and swung it at the boy slung across the saddle. A clean, fast, perfect stroke. It went past Theyab's throat a finger's width away and cut nothing but air.

The Seventh doesn't miss by accident. He stood on the ridge and watched us go, and the whirlwind came down over him and he didn't move, his white mask tipped up toward all those turning eyes as if they were old friends. Just before the dark took him, I heard it under the roar. Four notes, low and slow, climbing.

---

The rider didn't stop until the sun was going down and the zawba'a was a smudge behind us. He slid Theyab off onto the first red sand and set me beside him, and crouched over the boy without touching him, looking at the golden cracks and the singed hair a long, long time. His wrapped hands were shaking. I put that away with all the other things I wasn't saying.

He hung a waterskin on a dead *markh* bush, without a word, and pointed with his long hunting spear. North and west.

"Wait," Theyab croaked. "Who are you?"

The rider was gone into the dusk, the way he'd gone the first time.

"That's twice," Theyab whispered.

I lay down against him. You know by now that I knew who the rider was, and that I said nothing.

---

We walked the way the spear had pointed. Theyab made the rider's water last. Near dusk he ate one of the squashed honey-dates from the wreck of his tent, Umm Hanan's, from the bottom of his bag, sticky with sand. He held it in his mouth a long time, the way a child holds a hand.

He didn't say anything about Jasir, or about my hackles lying flat when Jasir said *it came for you*. He'd seen. I know he had.

Once he stopped and sniffed the back of his own wrist. He could still smell burnt hair on himself. So could I. I'm not sure he ever knew, after that, which fires were real.

The moon came up late that night, the thinnest I'd seen it since Hilat al-Sarab, a pale scratch of white low over the dunes. It gave almost no light at all.

And somewhere ahead of us, so faint only a dog could feel it, something was humming.

I felt it in my teeth, then in the pads of my feet. A deep, low note rising out of the sand exactly where the spear had pointed, like a mother humming a child to sleep. Or like a well, waiting.

I knew that sound, and the water that lies beside it, and what that water does to those who drink. The rider had not pointed us at it by accident. He was walking with his head down and his hand pressed to the feather mark, and he hadn't heard it yet. In a little while he would.

That night I didn't know the desert had saved one more thing for him beside the singing sand. Not a jinn, or a vision, or a stranger in a white mask.

Something worse, in the end. Something he could love.

If I'd known, I'd have made him walk faster. Or slower. I've never decided which.

---

*End of Chapter Seven*


---

# Chapter Eight
## The Singing Wells

> *Drink at Bir al-Ghina', and the past will drink from you. It is always thirstier than you are.*
> — a saying of the Bedu water-finders, older than any tribe that uses it

---

The moon was a thin old crescent the night we found Bir al-Ghina'.

Thin as the smile of a trader who has just cheated you and would like you back tomorrow. Ghanim smiled like that, in Hilat al-Sarab, every time he weighed Theyab's scraps of copper on his crooked scales. The moon hung low over the eastern dunes like that smile, a pale scratch on the dark, and gave almost no light at all.

That suited Theyab. He'd had enough of being seen.

He'd barely spoken since the zawba'a came walking out of a clear sky and the caravan was simply *gone*. Since he'd dug one boy out of the sand and poured the Light into him and given him his last water, and the boy had drunk it, and said the word, and run.

The cracks were still fading on his forearms. Every so often he'd sniff his wrist, and I'd pretend not to see. Dogs are good at that. People think we're stupid. It saves a great deal of trouble.

The rider's waterskin had been empty since the afternoon before. Once, in the dark, I heard him say very softly, "*Yumma, it's dark*," and then stop, appalled, as if someone else had used his mouth.

Then he stopped walking.

"Do you hear that?"

I did. I'd been hearing it for some time and hoping he wouldn't.

It was a hum. Low and steady and very large, as if the whole desert were humming to itself with its mouth closed. You felt it come up through your feet into the bones of your skull, until it was hard to tell whether the hum was outside you or whether you'd always been humming and had only just noticed.

"Water," Theyab whispered.

I sat down in the sand and sighed through my nose, long and heavy. It's what a saluki does instead of saying *unfortunately*.

---

You'll want to know why a dog would be unhappy about water in the middle of the sands.

There are dunes in this country that sing. On still evenings, when the sand along a great dune's crest lets go and slides down the steep face, the whole dune *booms*: a deep droning note like a hundred men humming, like a great drum struck softly far off. Men who know the sound say it is the jinn at their music, and won't camp within earshot.

Bir al-Ghina', the Well of Singing, lies at the foot of the biggest singing dune I know. Three old well-mouths in a hollow of half-wild date palms, with the dune standing over them like a wave about to break. Two are long dry. The third is deep and sweet and cold, and its water hums with the dune all the time, even when the sand is still.

What I know is this. The water remembers. Drink it, and it will hand you back something you had forgotten so completely you didn't know there was a hole where it used to be.

I once watched a man drink there and remember, in one swallow, what he had done to his brother when they were boys. He walked into the sands that night and took no water with him. The well doesn't give you your past. It gives your past *you*.

Then Theyab stopped again, and this time it wasn't the hum.

From ahead, past the black shapes of the palms, came a snarl like tearing cloth. Then a clang of steel. Then a sound I had not expected to hear within three days' walk of any jinn.

Someone was laughing.

---

We came over the last low dune and looked down into the hollow.

The well glowed faintly between the palms, a silver mist rising off the water like breath on a cold morning. Above it the great dune stood pale in the starlight, humming. And in the middle of all that quiet, beautiful wrongness, a young man was fighting five jinn-jackals by himself.

I've told you about the emptied. Let me tell you about the jackals.

They are jackals, or the memory of jackals, pulled long and thin and wrong, made of shadow the way smoke is made of fire. Solid in the middle and fraying at the edges. You feel the cold of them before you see them. Their eyes are coals, the angry red coals you find under the ash of a tent where someone didn't get out. They make one sound only, that low wet tearing snarl. And when they bite you they don't take flesh. They take whatever you were thinking at the time.

They hunt the Light. That's what they are for.

The young man didn't seem to care.

He was tall, a few years older than Theyab, with a short dark beard, a ghutra wound round his head like a fighting man's, and a jaw that had been cut once and healed into a pale seam. He held his sword the way only the guards of great sheikhs hold them, high and easy. Or the half of it he had left. It was an old straight *sayf*, snapped a hand's width above the guard, so what remained was barely longer than a khanjar, with a jagged end like a broken tooth.

He moved like water poured from a coffee pot.

"No, not you," he was saying, sidestepping a lunge. "You. The ugly one. Yes. You know who you are."

He spun. The broken blade went through the nearest jackal's throat. It didn't bleed. It burst, the way a dropped sack of flour bursts, into a cloud of fine black ash that drifted down onto the sand. Inside the cloud, for half a breath, I heard a small surprised voice. Somebody's last thought, let go.

"Four," said the young man cheerfully. "Who's next? Don't all rush at once."

---

Theyab had dropped flat behind the crest, his hand on his scavenged knife. The stranger was enjoying himself; nothing in me rose against that laughter. But he was also bleeding from a long scratch down his left shoulder, and one of the four jackals left had begun, very quietly, to circle round behind him while the others kept him busy.

And I'll tell you what else I knew, and couldn't stop knowing. Jinn-jackals hunt the Light. They weren't here for him. They'd been lying up at Bir al-Ghina' waiting for *Theyab*, nosing the wind, for days. This stranger had only got in the way.

Everyone who stands near Theyab gets in the way, sooner or later.

I must have looked at the jackals and then at Theyab. I must have put it all in my face, because he looked at me, and I saw him understand it.

He got up.

---

By then Theyab didn't trust the Light. Worse, he had begun to be afraid of it.

Every time it saved someone, the person it saved had looked at him afterwards with the same face, and that face had a word in it. And every time, it had shown him the dune, and the dune was getting closer, and now it came with the smell.

So when he ran down that slope, what he felt first was a cold little voice with a goatherd's patience in it, saying: *He'll see. He'll see what you are, and then he'll look at you like they all do. And then he'll be gone too. Everyone near you goes.*

The light came, and it came grey.

It dribbled out of his fists like dishwater, and the jackal circling behind the young man stopped. It turned its coal eyes toward Theyab. It lowered its head and grinned, the way only shadows can, a grin that went too far back along the jaw.

Fear does that. It turns the Light into something the dark can chew.

The jackal sprang. Theyab threw himself sideways and felt its teeth snap shut beside his ear, and for a moment he couldn't remember where he was. The palms, the well, the reason he had come down the dune: gone, bitten clean out, a gap like a pulled tooth. Then it came back in a rush. Most of it. I've never been sure it was all of it.

I hit the jackal from the side.

We were bred to bring down gazelle at a full run. I took it by the back of the neck and it was like biting a winter night, and my silver eye *burned*. But it let go of him.

Over its smoking back Theyab saw the young man stagger as a second jackal caught his wounded shoulder and dragged him half off his feet.

The young man didn't cry out. He set his teeth and swung the broken sword one-handed. He kept standing up.

And Theyab thought, without any words at all, *Not him too.*

The light went gold.

---

It came up out of his chest warm and steady, like the sun climbing over a dune, and ran down his arm into the pitted blade of his knife until the iron shone like honey.

The jackal in front of him flinched.

Theyab lunged, and for the first time in his life his knife went *into* one of the jinn's creatures instead of sliding off. It burst into ash. He slammed his shoulder into the jackal that had the young man's arm, and the light went through it like lamp-oil catching.

Then there was a back against his back.

A warm, solid weight, someone else's shoulder blades pressed to his own. He felt the other boy's breathing. The last two jackals paced in a slow circle, snarling their tearing-cloth snarl, and the young man shifted his feet so the two of them could turn together, like two men working a well-rope.

The young man glanced back over his shoulder. At Theyab's face, and Theyab's arms, and the gold pouring out of Theyab's skin and lighting up the palms, the well, the great pale dune and the drifting ash.

"You glow," he said.

A pause, while a jackal feinted and drew back.

"That's weird."

"I know," said Theyab.

"I didn't say it was *bad*." The young man turned forward again. "Duck."

Theyab ducked. The broken sword whistled through the air where his head had been and took the leaping jackal across the chest. Ash rained down on them, warm, like the ash that falls round a funeral pyre in the Indian quarter at Muscat. The last jackal seemed to do some quick arithmetic, and turned to run.

I ran it down before it reached the palms. It's what I'm for.

---

The hollow went quiet. Only the dune hummed, and the well under it, patient, waiting for someone to get thirsty.

The gold drained back into Theyab's chest and left him swaying, with new cracks running up both forearms, brighter than the old ones. And then it came, the thing that always came. It didn't wait any more for him to close his eyes.

The full moon, white and enormous.

The great dune, Tal Moreeb, so high its top was lost in the sky.

Heat. Not light. Heat, real heat, climbing up through the soles of his feet as if the sand had become a griddle for bread. His own arms opening, and the skin of his palms blistering as he watched. The smell again.

And something new. A sound, below him on the dune. Someone shouting. Shouting a *name*, over and over, in a voice cracked down the middle like a dropped bowl, and he couldn't hear the name. He leaned toward it with his hair already burning, and couldn't hear it.

"Hey. *Hey.*"

A hand on his shoulder. Theyab blinked, and the desert came back. Palms, well, stars, the thin scratch of moon in the east. He was sitting in the sand. He didn't remember sitting down. His palms were stinging. There was nothing on them. The young man was crouched in front of him with the broken sword across his knees, watching him with frank, dark-eyed interest.

"You went somewhere," he said. "Your eyes did a thing. And you were saying *it's hot, it's hot*. It's freezing."

Theyab looked up at the sky. At the moon. It was thin. So very thin. He let out a breath he hadn't known he was holding.

I didn't show him how fast a moon fills. I couldn't have, and it would have been unkind. Kindness and cowardice wear the same *bisht* in the dark.

"Does it hurt?" the young man asked.

Theyab stared at him.

He nodded at the golden cracks on Theyab's arms. As if it were a perfectly ordinary thing to ask someone who'd just turned into a lamp in front of you.

In fifteen years of being Theyab, no one had ever asked him that.

"Yes," said Theyab.

The young man looked at him for a moment, and then, oddly, at me, sitting at Theyab's side with my ears up and my hackles flat. Then he nodded slowly.

"Thought so," he said. "Your face looked like mine does when I've been punched." He stood and held out a hand, and when Theyab took it, pulled him to his feet in one easy movement. "Right. Excuse me a moment. I have to collect my wages."

---

He took a small clay jar from a saddlebag by the nearest palm, knelt, and began to scoop up jackal-ash with a flat wooden spoon.

Theyab watched him for a long time.

"What are you doing?"

"Making a living." He didn't look up. "An old perfume-seller in the souk at Nizwa pays a silver thaler a pinch. He burns it with frankincense, and they say the smoke keeps the other jinn off a house for a year." He tapped the jar. "A good jackal's worth a week of water. You've just doubled my income, glowing stranger. I'd thank you, but I'll have to give you half, and I'm sad about it already."

"You can keep it."

"Absolutely not. I have principles." He thought about it. "One. It's that one."

He sealed the jar and sat back on his heels. In the silver mist off the well I saw the ring on his right hand for the first time. Too heavy and too gold for a young man who scraped jinn-ash off the sand for a living. A seal ring, cut deep with a crest I'd seen before: a tall tower with a lamp burning on top of it. I had seen that crest painted on the gates of a city most men think is a story.

I kept that to myself. You'll have noticed I keep a great deal to myself. I'm not given a choice.

"Hazza'," said the young man, and touched his own chest.

He said it aloud. Right into the open night, with the wind moving in the palm fronds over his head and the great dune humming above him.

Theyab flinched and looked round at the dark between the palms. "You shouldn't— The wind *learns* them. There's a wadi, it—"

"The wind and I have an understanding," said Hazza'. "It's never done me a single favour, and I've never asked for one." He raised his eyebrows. "You've got one too, I assume. A name. Or do I keep calling you glowing stranger?"

Theyab's hand went to his chest. He looked at the wind in the palms for a long time. I knew what he was hearing. Red stone, and a child's voice, tasting him. *Theyab. Theyab. We'll wait.*

Then he leaned in close to Hazza''s ear and said it so quietly I only just caught it. "Theyab."

"Theyab," Hazza' repeated, at a perfectly normal volume. "Wolves. Good. Easy to shout when you're about to be eaten." He looked down. "And who's this?"

That was me, by the well, cleaning jackal-ash off my forefoot with considerable dignity.

"That's Ghareeb," said Theyab.

"*The stranger*." Hazza' crouched and held out the back of his hand properly, the way a man raised among hunting dogs does. I sniffed it. Steel, and lamp-oil, and an old sadness. "He suits it. Look at those eyes. One gold, one silver. My father's falconers would say there's a jinn behind that silver one."

"People say that."

"Is there?"

Theyab looked at me. I looked at him.

"He's not my dog," said Theyab, which was not an answer, and which was true. I have never been anyone's dog. Not even the first boy's, not really. I have only ever been theirs.

"Fine," said Hazza', and shrugged. "I've had a strange year."

And he went back to binding up his shoulder with a strip torn from his headcloth, one end held in his teeth.

I decided I liked him. I'd never have said so, even if I could. I know what happens to the people I like. It was already too late for him. It usually is.

---

Theyab's knees chose that moment to remember he hadn't drunk in a day and a half. He sat down rather suddenly by the well.

The water down in the stone throat was black and still, and the worn lip, grooved deep by a thousand years of well-ropes, shivered with the hum under his hand. For a moment he could have sworn the face looking up at him from the water was smaller. Younger. Not his.

"Careful," said Hazza'. He'd stopped binding his shoulder. "You know what this is?"

"Bir al-Ghina'," said Theyab. "The old men say it remembers."

"The old men are right." Hazza' crouched beside him, and for the first time his voice wasn't joking. "The first drink's the worst." He looked down into the black. "I drank once. My first night out here. I saw my mother's face. She died when I was three. I didn't know I remembered her at all." He was quiet. "I saw her dying. That's the part no one tells you. You don't get to choose which bit you get back." Then, lighter: "Now I boil it and strain it through my headcloth. It still hums a little. It doesn't sing."

Theyab looked at the water.

I knew what he was thinking. A boy found alone in the sand had nothing behind him to remember. Whatever the well gave him couldn't be worse than the emptiness he already had.

He was wrong. Emptiness is the kindest thing this desert gives anyone.

I came and put my head on his knee.

I could have stopped him. I could have stood in front of the well with my teeth bared, and he'd have listened; he'd begun to listen to me. I didn't. I've thought about why many times, and I'll let you decide whether it was kindness.

He took the leather bucket from its hook on the palm, let it down on the rope, drew it up dripping, cupped his hands in it, and drank.

---

It was cold enough to ache. He swallowed, and the humming came inside him, filling his chest and skull until it wasn't humming any more.

It was singing.

And the desert went away.

He was small. Smaller than he had ever been, wrapped tight in something soft, warm all the way through for the first time in his life. He couldn't see faces. Only the flicker of a fire, and the low black roof of a goat-hair tent above him, and the smell of smoke and milk. And outside, very far off, a sound he didn't have a word for yet. Many voices, chanting. No words in it. Just breath, rising and falling, rising and falling, coming closer.

But he could feel a hand.

A child's hand. Small, a little sticky with dates, very warm, and it had hold of his whole fist. It wasn't letting go. It squeezed, the way you'd squeeze something to make sure it was real. Someone small had decided he was worth holding on to, and was not going to let go, whatever came through the tent flap.

And above him, somewhere in the warm dark, a man was singing.

His voice was low and rough and a little out of tune. It was a song about fire, and about wings: about a great bird the old people called the *'anqa'*, that burns and falls into its own ashes and rises out of them again. The words slid away. But the tune went into him and stayed, the way a fish-hook stays.

The chanting outside got louder. The man's voice shook, and kept singing.

The small hand squeezed harder. And then—

Then it was gone. Not let go. *Pulled*. Torn out of his fist, the small fingers dragging across his palm, and there was a scream, a child's scream, high and furious, getting farther away. And the singing stopped.

He knew that tune.

He'd heard it hummed out in the dunes, behind a smooth white mask, by a man with two grey blades who had cornered him in the dark and then, for no reason at all, let him go. He'd heard four notes of it under the roar of the zawba'a, two days ago, as a sword went past his throat.

---

Theyab came up out of the memory gasping.

He was on his knees by the well, his face wet, and not all of it was well-water. His right fist was clenched so hard his nails had cut half-moons in his palm. He couldn't open it. He sat there trying, and it wouldn't. As if it were still holding on to something that had been torn out of it fifteen years ago.

Hazza' didn't say anything.

That was the second thing he did that night that no one had ever done for Theyab. He didn't ask questions. He sat down in the sand beside him, close enough that their shoulders nearly touched, and looked out at the palms, and waited. After a while, without a word, he reached over and began gently prying Theyab's fingers open, one at a time, the way you'd help an old man with a stiff hand.

When the last finger came loose, Hazza' said, "Good one or bad one?"

Theyab wiped his face with the heel of his hand.

"I don't know," he said.

It was the truest thing he could have said. I lay with my chin on my paws and every hair on me as flat as water.

Later, when Hazza' had built a fire of dead palm fronds between the dry wells, Theyab came and sat by me. He didn't look at me. He kept his eyes on the flames.

"Ghareeb," he said softly. "That song. You've heard it before. Haven't you."

He'd begun to watch for my answers in my ears and my eyes and the fur along my back. He was better at it than any bearer before him. That frightened me more than the jackals.

So I yawned, wide and bored, the way any dog yawns by any fire, and put my head down on my paws, and gave him nothing to read at all. It wasn't a lie. A dog can't lie by yawning.

That's the trouble with silence. You can hide a whole child in it, and no one will ever catch you.

---

Hazza' shared his bread, hard rounds of it baked in the ashes days ago, and boiled, strained water from his little brass pot. It only hummed a little. Theyab drank it with his eyes shut, braced, and nothing came.

"You can stay, if you like," Hazza' said, poking the fire with his broken sword. He said it lightly. It mattered a great deal. "Three days at least. The guest's right." He glanced at me. "Your stranger too."

He tossed me a piece anyway. I ate it. Dignity has limits.

Theyab lay down on the far side of the fire with his patched cloak pulled over him. And I watched him do a thing I had never once seen him do in fifteen years. He fell asleep with his knife lying in the sand beside him instead of in his hand. His right hand lay loosely on the sand instead, palm up, fingers half closed, as if waiting for someone small to take it.

Hazza' sat up a while longer, turning the gold ring and looking north. Then he lay down too.

I stayed awake. Someone has to.

The dune hummed. Around midnight, the wind changed, and a long sheet of sand let go along the crest, and the whole dune *boomed*, one deep shuddering note that went on and on, and for an hour afterwards the well beneath it sounded almost exactly like breathing. I sat with my back to the fire and watched the black water and did not let either of the boys go near it.

The stars turned. Suhail climbed out of the south, the great white star that rises at summer's end and breaks the heat. And low over the eastern dunes, the thin moon hung like a white thread, like the first stitch in a shroud that would take exactly one turn of the sky to sew.

I didn't count the nights, that first night. I should have. If I'd begun at the beginning I'd have known exactly how many they had, and I could tell you every one.

I began the next night instead.

I've never been able to stop. Some nights I still count them, and every time, there's one less than I remember.

---

*End of Chapter Eight*


---

# PART THREE: ONE TURN OF THE MOON


---

# Chapter Nine
## A Prince with a Broken Sword

> *A broken blade still cuts, if the hand is true. So does a broken promise.*
> — words cut above the door of an old swordsmith's forge in Nizwa, the rest of the line chiselled away

---

Night one. I counted it at dawn, which is cheating, but I'm the one telling this and I'll count how I like. You'll find the one telling the story always does.

Theyab woke to the sound of someone fighting nobody.

He came up out of his cloak with his knife already in his fist, the way he'd woken every morning of his life, and found Hazza' out on the open sand between the palms, moving through the grey light before sunrise. The broken sword flashed and cut the air in long looping patterns, wrist and shoulder and hip all turning together, as if the whole thing were a dance he'd been taught before he could walk.

Which, as it turned out, it was.

Theyab had learned to fight from bullies and falling walls. He had never seen fighting that was *beautiful*.

"You're staring," said Hazza', without stopping.

"Sorry."

"Don't be. I'm magnificent." He finished the pattern with the broken point a finger's width from a palm trunk, held it there, then lowered it. "Taught by the best swordsman in my father's guard, and then by every guard who wanted to knock a sheikh's son on his backside and call it a lesson. I have never lost a fight."

From inside Theyab's bag, muffled by the rag he kept it wrapped in, came a small clear *ting*. Old Bakhit's little brass bell. He'd kept it wrapped since the caravan, where no one could see it glint and ask.

And at the same moment, from beside the cold fire, came a growl.

That was me. I never mean to. When a man lies within reach of my nose, the hackles come up from my neck to my tail in a stiff ridge, and a sound comes out of my chest like a stone being dragged. It is the one part of me I have never been able to train.

Hazza' looked at the bag. He looked at me, with my lip lifted. He looked at Theyab.

"Your dog," he said, "is growling at me."

"He does that when people lie," said Theyab. "And that's a truth bell. It rings when someone lies to me."

Hazza' put his sword away with great dignity.

"Twice," he said. "I've lost twice. Once to one of the guards when I was nine, and once to a camel."

No bell. My hackles lay down smooth, and I put my chin back on my paws. The corner of Theyab's mouth twitched.

"A *camel*?"

"It was a very large camel, and it was a racing bull in its season, and I don't want to talk about it."

---

There was something wrong about that sword, and Theyab noticed it while Hazza' was picking sand out of the guard.

The hilt was wrapped in leather gone black with hand-sweat, and the pommel was a round brass cap that didn't quite match, as though made for a different sword.

"Why don't you mend it?" Theyab asked.

"Some things you don't mend."

It was true. My hackles didn't stir. It just wasn't an answer.

"The old smiths of my city made their hilts hollow," Hazza' said after a moment. He twisted the brass pommel off. Inside the grip was a narrow empty space, the length of a finger. "You're meant to keep a blessing in here. A holy man writes it very small on a scrap of paper, and you fold it tight and carry it into every fight."

"What's in yours?"

Hazza' tipped the hilt toward him. Empty. Dark in there, like a little well.

"I was never much good at asking for things," he said, and screwed the pommel back on.

Theyab nodded slowly. "Me neither."

It was a small thing. Two boys admitting a small thing to each other by a well. But I've learned to watch small things. They're usually where the big ones hide.

---

He told his story that night, by the fire, with the dune humming over them like a crowd that had come to listen.

Theyab didn't ask. That matters. If he asked, he'd be asked back. But somewhere in the long hot day something had loosened between them, and when the stars came out, Hazza' simply started talking.

"I'm from Ubar," he said.

Theyab laughed. Then he saw Hazza''s face, and stopped.

Every child in the sands knows that name. Mothers in Hilat al-Sarab used it on children who wandered: *Come home before dark, or Ubar will come up out of the sand and take your name.* The city of a thousand pillars, swallowed by the desert for its pride.

"Ubar's a story," Theyab said.

"It's a story with very thick walls," said Hazza'. He smiled crookedly at the fire. "The City of the Dead and Darkness. The sand never swallowed it. It's down in a great hollow in the deep sands where no caravan goes, and you can ride within a day of it and never see a tower. The walls are towers, all joined together, very high. And there are lamps on every wall, thousands, burning day and night, and they must never all go out. There are men whose whole lives are walking the walls with oil jars." He poked the fire. "And under the city there are tombs, deeper than wells. On quiet nights, if you lie with your ear on the palace floor, you can hear a sort of knocking down there. Very slow. The old men say it's the stone settling."

"Is it?"

"I was the sheikh's son. Nobody told me the truth about anything." He shrugged. "And on the walls of every lane, someone has painted the names of the dead. Back to the beginning. The painters go round touching them up every year, because the names fade." He paused. "They fade faster than they used to. That's what the old painters say. Some fade in a single night. You go to bed and your grandfather's on the wall, and you wake up and there's just a pale patch where he was. No one knows why."

"A city that won't let anyone be forgotten," he said. "Mostly."

He turned the heavy gold ring. The tower and the lamp caught the firelight.

"My father is Sheikh Mutlaq," he said. "I'm his eldest son. Or I was. It's complicated."

I lay perfectly still, hackles flat. Theyab looked at me, the way he'd begun to whenever anyone spoke. Then at the young man who scraped jinn-ash into a jar for a living. His mouth fell open.

"You're the sheikh's *son*."

"Was. Probably still am. No one's quite sure. It depends who you ask, and whether they've got a khanjar in their hand at the time." Hazza' shrugged. "Close your mouth. You'll get sand in it."

---

There was a boy called Suhail.

He worked in the sheikh's stables, Hazza' said, looking after the riding camels and the sheikh's white racing she-camels and the three old mares that nobody rode any more. He was maybe twelve, with ears that stuck out like a jerboa's and a laugh like a donkey with hiccups, and he knew the name of every animal in the stables and told each one *good morning* every day.

"His mother named him for the star," Hazza' said. "Born the week Suhail rises. She said he'd bring the cool. I used to hide in the stables. When lessons got too much. When my father got too much. Suhail never told. He'd hand me a brush and point at a camel."

One night Suhail was sent outside the walls on an errand, past the last lamp. No one would ever say who sent him. He came back grey.

"Not all the way," Hazza' said. "But half of him was gone. He'd stand there turning his hands over as if they belonged to someone else." His voice had gone flat and careful, like a man walking on a roof that might give. "And he'd smile, sometimes. That was the worst. Not his smile. A different one. As if someone had shown him what a smile was once and he'd copied it wrong. And he'd say *good morning*. Just that. *Good morning, good morning*, at midnight, to the wall."

Theyab's hand had gone to his chest. Behind my eyes I heard a man with an orange beard calling *couch the camels in a ring* across an empty plain.

"The law of Ubar says the emptied are to be carried out past the last lamp and left for the sands. Before it spreads. So I hid him."

"Where?"

"An old fodder loft over the stables. I brought him food. I sat with him every night. I read him the names off the walls, all the old names, thinking if he heard enough names he might find his own again." Hazza' swallowed. "Once, after about a month, he almost smiled. His own smile. I swear by my mother's grave he did. *Almost*."

The fire cracked. No one said anything.

"The Wazir found out," Hazza' said. "Muhaisen. My father's counsellor. He's never once raised his voice in my hearing. He has grey eyes, pale grey, like someone left them out in the sun too long." He picked at the cloth on his shoulder. "And he stood in my father's majlis, in front of every elder in Ubar, and said very gently that the sheikh's son had been hiding a jinn-ridden thing in the palace and doing sorcery on it. *Treason*. He said it so kindly, Theyab. As if it grieved him."

Grey eyes. A soft voice. I've known a few men with eyes like that. None of them were ever grieved by anything.

"Was it true?" Theyab asked.

"I was hiding him," said Hazza'. "The rest was a lie."

I didn't growl. I didn't growl for either half, because he believed both, and so did I.

"They sent guards that night. Six of them. I stood in the loft door." He touched the hilt of the broken sword lying beside him. "One had an iron-bossed shield, and I hit it wrong. I was trying to get past it to the one who'd caught Suhail's arm. The blade went in half. I kept fighting with what was left." He let out a breath that was almost a laugh. "I'm very good with what's left. I've had practice."

"And Suhail?"

The dune hummed.

"Muhaisen had him *taken away*," Hazza' said. "That's all anyone would tell me. I asked the stable hands, and they said, *What boy?* As if he was never there." He looked at his hands. "I've been looking for most of a year. Sometimes I wake up and I can't remember his face either. Just the ears. And the *good morning*."

"And your father—"

"Wouldn't look at me." Hazza''s voice didn't change. That was the worst of it. "Muhaisen did all the talking. My father sat there with his hand over his eyes. And then I was outside the gate with a broken sword and a camel and a bag of dates, and that was that."

He picked up a stick and drew in the sand. A small figure. A stick boy.

"My brother Zaid ran after me," he said. "He's ten. He got all the way to the outer gate in his bare feet, crying so hard he couldn't talk. He just grabbed my *thawb* and wouldn't let go." His mouth twisted. "They had to carry him back. Muhaisen carried him. Very gently. Zaid stopped crying the moment Muhaisen picked him up. Just stopped, all at once, like a lamp blown out." He rubbed the stick boy away with his palm. "I can still hear him. I can still hear him *stop*."

Theyab looked at the smeared place in the sand a long time.

Then, very quietly, because a story is owed for a story and Theyab always paid what he owed, he told Hazza' about Hilat al-Sarab.

The stones. The scar under his shoulder blade. Nadim, and the lane full of the emptied, and little Shibl going grey and coming back. The faces afterwards. The wadi. The caravan, and Jasir drinking the last water and spitting out the word. Every time somebody had used it.

When he finished, Hazza' said nothing for a while.

"They'll call you a monster, Theyab," he said at last. "They called me one too." He looked up from the fire, and the crooked smile came back, a little shaky at the edges. "Let's be monsters together, then."

I got up, and walked round the fire, and lay down between them, touching both.

And out past the palms, beyond the firelight, I heard something move in the sand. Not a jackal. Something slower. Something that had been lying under the dune listening to two boys say their secrets out loud in the dark, and had liked it.

I lay between them and the dark all night with my ears turned outward. Neither of them asked why.

---

Night four. That was the night of the scorpions.

I want to say first that it was not my fault.

I had found a fat yellow one under a flat stone, as long as Hazza''s hand, nipped the sting off the way I'd done for a thousand years, and crunched it by the fire with my mouth open. Nothing ruins a bearer's appetite faster, and a bearer with no appetite isn't thinking about anything worse. Theyab made a small sick noise, which was what I'd been going for.

Hazza', however, leaned in.

"I wonder what it tastes like."

"*No*," said Theyab.

"Why not? He eats them. He looks fine." He was already on his feet. "And I've eaten locusts fried in butter in the souk at Nizwa. Everybody eats locusts."

"Locusts aren't scorpions."

"Locusts are scorpions with better manners."

He came back a short while later with his own scorpion pinned under the flat of his broken sword, looking deeply pleased with himself. He pulled the sting off the way he'd watched me do it. The legs were still going.

"Hazza'," said Theyab.

"Don't be a coward. I once ate a sheep's eye at a wedding feast in Ibri. It looked back at me the entire time."

He put the scorpion in his mouth and crunched.

His face did several interesting things in a row. Then he swallowed, nodded slowly, and said, "Huh. Actually, that's not so—"

He was sick for a full day.

---

I've watched a great many people be ill. Plague in the coast towns. The fever off the *sabkha* that takes whole villages in a season. Hazza' was not the worst. But he was the loudest. He lay in the shade with his arms flung out, turning interesting shades of green, rolling over now and then to be sick, and announcing to the sky that he was dying.

"Tell Zaid," he croaked, "that I died bravely. In battle. Against a beast of terrible size."

"It was the length of your hand," said Theyab.

"Tell him it was *this* big." He tried to spread his arms and gave up halfway. "Theyab. Theyab. Are you there?"

"I'm here."

"I'm leaving you my sword. It's broken. Look after it. Don't let the dog near it. He'll bury it."

When he said that, something cold went down my back, like a door opening in an empty house. The desert has a terrible sense of humour, and it never forgets a line.

And Theyab laughed.

It wasn't a big laugh. It came out of him in a short surprised burst, like a sandgrouse startled off a waterhole. Then another, and then he had his hand over his mouth and his shoulders were shaking, and he couldn't stop.

Hazza' lifted his head off the sand. His face was green and his beard was full of grit.

"Is that—" he said. He looked at me, as if I could tell him. "Is he *laughing*? I'm dying and he's *laughing*?"

I thumped my tail on the sand. Twice. It is the closest a saluki comes to *yes, he is, and you deserve it*.

I'll tell you something now, and you can do what you like with it.

I had not heard Theyab laugh in eight years. Not since he was seven, and gave himself a name, and sat on the lip of Saqi's well saying it over and over under his breath until it made him giggle. After that, the village took the laughing out of him the way they took everything else. One stone at a time.

And here was this green, groaning, ridiculous sheikh's son, flat on his back in the sand at Bir al-Ghina', and he'd given it back without even trying.

I want you to hold on to that. I've told you how this ends, and there aren't many sounds like that one coming.

Theyab offered to help. He held out his hands, with the gold already stirring under the skin.

"Absolutely not," Hazza' moaned. "Every jackal for three days would come running, and I'd have to fight them lying down. And you'd go all strange in the eyes again. I'll be sick the honest way, thank you."

So Theyab sat with him all afternoon with water and a wet cloth, and every so often looked over at Hazza' groaning on the sand and started laughing all over again.

I didn't eat a scorpion in front of either of them for three days after that. I'd like that noted. It was a sacrifice. I know about those.

---

The lessons began on the sixth night.

It was Hazza''s idea. He'd been watching Theyab stare at the marks scratched into the stone lip of the oldest well, by some herdsman centuries ago, and finally he said, "You can't read, can you?"

Theyab's whole body went stiff. His hand went to his chest.

"No."

He said it to the fire. In Hilat al-Sarab almost no one could read, and Theyab had never once been allowed to sit with the boys at their lessons under the ghaf tree. And somewhere under his ribs he had already begun to dread the moment Hazza' would realise how far below him he was, and look at him differently, and go.

But Hazza' only smoothed a patch of sand flat with his palm and picked up a stick.

"Right," he said. "Come here."

He drew a shape. A little curved hook, like a bent finger, with a dot above it.

"We write from the right," he said. "Like this. That's *dhal*. It makes a *dh* sound, with your tongue between your teeth. Like the start of *dhi'b*. A wolf." To its left he drew three more. A little cup with two dots beneath. A tall straight stroke. A shallow boat with one dot underneath it. "*Ya*. *Alif*. *Ba*."

He sat back.

"That's your name," he said.

Theyab stared at it.

Four shapes in the sand, wobbly and crooked, because Hazza' wrote like a sheikh's son who'd always had a scribe to write for him. ذياب. *Theyab*. The only thing in the world that had ever been his, lying there outside him, where anyone could see it. Where anything could.

"Is it—" Theyab's voice cracked. "Is that like saying it? To the wind?"

"The wind can't read," said Hazza'.

I lifted my head off my paws and looked at the dunes. I'd seen the dunes write. I'd seen what they wrote back. I looked a long time, until Theyab followed my eyes, and went quiet.

He copied the four shapes, slowly, with his tongue between his teeth. Then again. Then again. He was still at it long after Hazza' had fallen asleep, by firelight and then by starlight, until the patch of sand in front of him was covered edge to edge with his own name.

In the morning the wind had smoothed it all away.

Nearly all.

I found it while the boys still slept. Round the lip of the oldest well, far from the fire, where the old stone was soft enough to scratch. ذياب. Cut deep, over and over, in a ring all the way round the well-mouth, in his crooked new hand. The scratches were fresh. There was blood in some of them, from a split fingernail.

When he woke, the nail of his right forefinger was torn to the quick. He looked at it, puzzled, and sucked it, and didn't remember. I scraped sand into the letters until they were filled, and sat a long time with my nose pointed down the black throat of the well, wondering which of us it had been talking to in the night.

He wrote it again that evening, by the fire. Awake.

---

It went both ways. That's what I want you to understand about the two of them. Nobody was rescuing anybody. Every night Hazza' taught Theyab a few more letters, and every day Theyab taught Hazza' how not to die.

He showed him how to spread a cloth over a scrape in the sand before dawn so the dew would drip into a cup. Which roots held water, and which plants held poison, like the milky sap of the calotropis that blinds you. How to smell a shamal a day off, like an old coin. How to read a dune's long back and steep face, so even on a starless night your feet know which way the wind has blown all year. And more quietly: how to listen for the hiss of the sand to stop altogether, which means something under it is listening too.

"And when the dunes shift at night," Theyab said, "you don't follow the sand. The sand lies. You follow the stars. Al-Jadi up there in the north, that doesn't move. Al-Thurayya, the little bunch, like grapes. And that one." He pointed low into the south, to the great white star just clearing the dunes. "That's Suhail. The old men say when Suhail rises the worst heat breaks. Keep him on your—"

He stopped. Hazza' was looking at the star, and his face had gone very still.

"Suhail," Hazza' said.

"The stable boy," said Theyab softly. "I'm sorry. I didn't—"

"No." Hazza' kept looking at it. "No. I like that he's up there. I like that you navigate by him." He was quiet a long time. "Nobody ever taught me which one he was. I had six tutors. Not one of them taught me a single thing that would keep me alive."

"Nobody ever taught me either," said Theyab. "I just didn't die."

They looked at each other over the fire. Then Hazza' began to laugh, and after a moment so did Theyab.

*Yet*, I thought. *You just haven't died yet.* I kept it behind my teeth with all the rest. Behind my teeth is the only place I have.

---

On the tenth night, Hazza' reached into his saddlebag and brought out a small clay pot with a wax seal, and two handfuls of dates he'd shaken down from the palms.

"Sidr honey," he said, breaking the wax. "Wild bees in the Hajar. Cost me two jackals' worth of ash. Don't tell anyone, it ruins my reputation as a dangerous exile."

Theyab went very still.

"There was a cook in my father's kitchen," Hazza' went on, not noticing. "Umm Shamma. Enormous woman. Arms like a pearl-diver. She used to leave honey cakes cooling on the windowsill, and Zaid and I stole them every day for ten years, and she pretended every single day not to know, and shouted at the kitchen boys for it. I think she baked extra." He smiled down at the pot. "I think she knew exactly."

Theyab told him then about the cloth bundles left outside his patched tent by Umm Hanan, who never once spoke to him in the street and never once missed a week. About the dates rolled in honey he always saved for the hardest days, as if sweetness could be stored like water against a drought. How, on the day the village stoned him out, she'd pushed bread into his hands in front of everyone, and that bread had kept him alive across the sands.

Hazza' listened. Then he dipped a date in the pot and held it out.

"Then let's have it on an easy day," he said. "For once. See what that's like."

Theyab took it.

They ate the whole pot and argued about which star was brightest, and I lay between them with my legs stuck out stiff the way a saluki lies when it is shamelessly content, and I didn't growl once all night. It was the best night. I've measured every night since against it.

I counted that one. I counted all of them. The thin moon had gone and left the sky dark for a few nights, and now it was coming back, a little fuller every evening, like a cup being filled. Like a pyre being built, one stick at a time.

When Theyab told Hazza' that Old Bakhit had sent him to find Ghidd al-Zujaj, the glass ruins, Hazza' didn't even pretend to think about it.

"I'll come," he said. "I know roughly where it lies. My grandfather's falconers used to talk about it. And frankly, you need someone with you who can read the signs."

"I can read *some* letters now."

"You can read your own name, the dog's name and the word *jamal*." Hazza' grinned. "Camel. The most useful word in any language. I'm coming."

Not one hair on my back moved. He meant it entirely.

I looked up at the moon, filling. I couldn't have said anything if I'd wanted to. Out past the palms, under the great dune, the thing that liked to listen shifted in the sand, and settled, and was patient.

---

*End of Chapter Nine*


---

# Chapter Ten
## The City of Glass

> *Here the first one said no, and the sand wept glass. Here the rest said yes, and no one wept at all.*
> — cut into the rock at Ghidd al-Zujaj in the old South Arabian letters, which no Bedu living can read

---

Night fourteen. We saw Ghidd al-Zujaj at sunset, which is the only proper way to see it, and the worst.

It lay in a flat basin between the dunes, and at first it looked like nothing: a low dark ridge of rock, a scatter of broken walls. Then the sun dropped behind it and the whole basin caught fire.

The ground was glass. Long branching veins of it, great frozen puddles, twisted pipes standing up out of the sand like roots dug up and left. Green as a bottle, black as oil, yellow as old honey, here and there a cloudy red like a bruise seen through skin. The Bedu find bits of it all over the deep sands, where lightning has struck and melted the sand where it stood. They call it *lightning's bones* and carry it for luck.

Here there was more than lightning could ever have made. The walls of the old town had melted where they stood, mud and stone run down like candle wax and set hard and shining. And when the sun went down behind it, every wall and pipe lit from inside, like a great many lamps standing in rows.

Like a great many small pyres.

Hazza' stopped walking.

"By my father's beard," he said softly.

Theyab said nothing. His hand was on his chest. He told me later that the mark felt as if something on its far side were pressing a palm against it, the way you'd lay your hand on a door to feel whether the room beyond was burning.

I lay down in the sand and put my chin on my paws and did not look at it.

---

It had taken us four days from Bir al-Ghina'. Theyab led by the stars, and Hazza' found the old waymarks: a cairn, a dead ghaf tree with a rag tied to it. They were good days. I want you to know that. Every evening they sat by a little fire while Hazza' drew letters in the sand and Theyab copied them.

He had a good many by then. His own name, and Hazza''s, and mine. *Ma'*, water. *Nar*, fire. *Jamal*, camel, which Hazza' maintained was the most useful word in any language. And on the twelfth night, with the fire burning down, Theyab had asked for the fire-words. What you call each thing a fire leaves behind.

"*Nar*," Hazza' had said, drawing. "The fire. *Jamr*, the coals. And when it's all gone, *ramad*. The ash." Four shapes, right to left. "*Ra*, with its little tail dropping under the line. *Mim*, like a knot. *Alif*, standing up. *Dal*, like a bent knee. That one's easy. You'll have it by morning."

Theyab copied it ten times. He didn't know yet why it mattered. Neither did Hazza'.

I did. I could have scuffed it out with my feet each time he wrote it. I didn't. I'm a great one for not stopping things.

---

Hazza' told the story on the last dune, the way his grandfather's falconers had told it to him, badly, the way people tell stories they half believe.

"There was an ordinary town here once. Wells, palms, a market, people quarrelling over the price of ghee. And a goatherd boy came down from the mountains carrying a light the One who made the sands had put in him. He was meant to give it away, up on the great dune, for everyone. He wouldn't. He stood on that rock there—" He nodded toward the dark ridge. "—and said *no*. And the light went *inward*. It burned so hot the sand melted. The whole town. Everyone in it. The sand wept glass." He looked down at the burning walls, and didn't sound like a man who thought it was a story any more.

I'll tell you what I couldn't tell either of them.

I'd stood on that ridge. I'd watched a small, poor, furious boy with a herding stick close his hands round the Light instead of opening them. I'd barked and barked his name, the only noise I had, and he'd looked straight at me and hadn't known who I was. Then the whole town below had screamed, all at once, and stopped, all at once, the way a scream stops when there is suddenly no air.

I had not been back in a very long time.

Theyab put his hand on my head. "Ghareeb?" he said. "You're shaking."

I got up and walked down the dune ahead of them, fast, so he wouldn't see my face. Dogs have faces. People forget.

---

You have to walk through the lanes to reach the rock. There's no other way in.

I tried not to look, so I'll tell you what Hazza' saw, because he looked. He couldn't help it.

The walls of Ghidd al-Zujaj don't have names painted on them, the way he said the walls of Ubar do. They have *people* in them.

Dark shapes inside the glazed mud, where the walls had run down over them and set before they could fall. A man with his arm over his face. A woman curled over something small. Two shapes in a doorway, holding each other. A dog across a threshold. Hundreds, like flies in honey, their shadows thrown out long across the lane so that we had to walk on them.

Hazza' stopped in front of one. A child, low down near the ground, small enough to have been four or five. Face pressed to the inside of the glass. Mouth open.

"Don't," said Theyab quietly, and took his arm, and pulled him on.

Hazza' went. But he kept looking back. And I heard him, under his breath, the way he'd told us he used to talk to Suhail in the fodder loft. *I'm sorry. I'm sorry. I'm sorry.* To each one as we passed. All the way down the lane.

That was Hazza'. I've never forgotten it.

I didn't look at the dog.

---

The carvings were on the rock at the heart of the town.

It was a long low ridge of dark sandstone curving round a hollow like a cupped hand. The glass lapped up to its foot and stopped; it was the only thing in the basin that hadn't melted. Sun and wind had burned its face black and shiny over thousands of years, and into that black skin, low down, someone had pecked the old pictures you see all over the Hajar: stick camels, an ibex, a hand. Faded almost to nothing.

Above them were the others.

There were eleven.

They ran in a long curve round the hollow, each taller than a man. Nobody living can carve like that. They were pecked into the black skin so finely that the pale stone showed through in every line of a face, every hair, as if each child had been pressed into the rock like a seal into hot wax.

Beneath each, newer by a thousand years, someone had cut a line of letters in the square, angular hand the first scribes used, before the letters learned to curve.

Hazza' had gone to look for the cistern. "Don't touch anything," he said, too brightly. "Especially you, Ghareeb."

So it was only Theyab and me in the hollow of the rock when he began to read.

---

He went slowly, lips moving, sounding out the square old shapes the way a child does, his finger tracing each letter just above the stone. The rock breathed cold onto his hand.

He couldn't read most of it. But he could pick out letters he knew, and every so often a whole word came clear for him like a coin turning up in the sand.

"*Haddad*," he said, in front of the third. "Smith. The… Smith."

And then he stopped, and frowned, and looked at the picture properly for the first time.

It was a smith's boy. Not a man. Broad in the shoulders from working the goatskin bellows, but a boy. Thirteen, perhaps. He held a hammer in both hands, and it was too big for him. His feet were bare.

Theyab moved on. In the fourth he found *twin*, and a word he didn't know. Two figures, almost the same, both children, one reaching a hand toward the other, who was turning away.

In the fifth he found *sheikh*. A boy again. A grown man's headrope slipping down over his small ears, a *bisht* too big for him trailing on the ground.

In the seventh, *milh*. Salt. A boy with salt crusted white in his hair and on his eyelashes like frost, the way it crusts on the salt-diggers of the sabkha, smaller than any of them. Nine, maybe. He was smiling. The kind of smile children put on for grown-ups who are watching them be brave.

In the eighth, a falcon lifting from a wrist so thin the bird's talons wrapped all the way round it.

Theyab turned and looked at me. His face had gone thin. "Ghareeb," he said. "They're all—"

I didn't move. I only looked at the next carving. *Go on.*

He stopped in front of the first for a long time.

A small boy with a herding stick. Turning away, out of the picture, face hidden. And all round him the rock was cracked, black and split and starred, as if something had struck it from inside. I didn't look at it. I looked at the ground between my feet.

"That's him," Theyab said. "The goatherd. Isn't it. The boy in my dream."

I looked at the ground.

He went on.

The ninth showed a boy walking away from a dune with his hands pressed over his eyes. A boy. But something in the tilt of his head, as if listening for something no one else could hear, made Theyab look at it a long while, and something went across his face that he didn't say. I didn't help him. I'll tell you about that one later. I promise.

The eleventh was clouded. The varnish was patchy, the pecking half done, as though the rock hadn't finished deciding. You could just make out a figure running. No taller than Theyab. A long line beside him that might have been a spear. Nothing else.

And then he went back to the beginning and looked at them all again, together. That was when he saw it.

---

It wasn't the words that told him first. It was the pictures.

In the first, the boy with the stick was turning away. In the ninth, the boy with his hands over his eyes was walking away. In the eleventh, whoever it was was running. But in the other eight, the picture was the same.

A dune. A very high dune, so high that its crest went up out of the carving into the black rock above. A full moon above it, enormous, a great pale disc of pecked stone filling half the sky. And on the top of that dune, a child with its arms open, and fire rising out of it like wings.

Eight times. Eight different children. A girl with her hair in long braids, the oldest of them, fifteen perhaps. The smith's boy with his hammer dropped at his feet. The little sheikh with his headrope fallen off. The boy of salt, so small the fire coming off him was taller than he was. Each on that same dune, under that same moon, arms open, burning.

And the rock had remembered their faces. That was what he couldn't look away from. In the carvings where the children turned away, you couldn't see their faces. In the eight that burned, you could. The rock had kept exactly how each of them looked, in that moment, with the fire coming up through them.

Not one of them looked like the hero of a tale told by the coffee hearth.

Every one of them looked like a child who has just found out that it hurts.

Theyab had seen that picture before.

He'd seen it every time he used the Light. With Shibl in his arms. On the caravan track. At Bir al-Ghina'. Every time a little longer, a little hotter.

He went back to the girl with the braids. His finger ran along the letters beneath her, past all the words he didn't know, to the very end, the far left, where one short word stood on its own. Four letters, squarer than Hazza''s, but he knew them.

*Ra. Mim. Alif. Dal.*

"*Ramad*," Theyab whispered.

He went to the next carving, the smith's boy. The last word was *ramad*.

The next. *Ramad*.

The next. *Ramad*. *Ramad*. *Ramad*. Eight times, eight carvings, eight children, the same word at the end of every line. Hazza' had told him it was an easy one.

He stood in the hollow of the black rock with the glass town burning down to embers behind him and the night coming on, and he understood.

It wasn't a picture of something that *might* happen.

The bearers didn't save everyone and go home. They didn't grow old and tell it by the fire. They were children, every one, and they climbed the great dune under the full moon and opened their arms and burned, every one who'd been brave enough, and the grey went away for a while, and they were *ramad*. Ash.

*They are remembered*, Old Bakhit had said, in his tent full of dried herbs and little bells, when Theyab had asked what happened after.

No bell had rung. It was true. The rock remembered them. Perfectly. Forever. Their faces at the worst moment of their lives, set hard for anyone to look at.

---

He didn't cry. I'd half expected him to.

He sat down at the foot of the rock, slowly, the way a very old man sits, and pulled his knees up to his chest, his hand flat over the feather mark.

Then he said, without looking at me, "You knew."

He wasn't asking. And he was watching me, out of the corner of his eye, the way he'd learned. My ears went back. My eyes slid away from his. A dog can't do much, but a dog can't hide that.

"And Bakhit knew."

I put my head down.

"They're all children," he said. "Ghareeb. They're all *children*. Why are they all children?"

I looked up out of the hollow at the first stars, and something rose in me I'd been swallowing for a very long time. Eleven times I had walked a child toward that dune, telling myself the Light just settled in the young, the way frost settles in low ground. I'll tell you what I think at the end. You won't like it.

The growl came up out of me before I could stop it. Not at him. At the sky. I stood on stiff legs with my hackles up and my lips drawn back, snarling at the stars like a mad thing, the way I'd snarl at a man who lied to me.

He flinched. Then he looked where I was looking.

He looked up at the eight burning children in the rock. In the dusk the pale lines of them seemed to shine faintly, like coals under ash.

And then he asked me.

He asked in a very small voice. It was the voice of a seven-year-old sitting on the lip of a well, saying his own name over and over so he wouldn't forget it.

"Ghareeb," he said. "Is there a way I don't have to die?"

---

I want to tell you about that moment properly. You know I can't abide liars.

I have walked beside eleven bearers. I have kept things from all of them, because I am a dog and can't tell anyone anything. But I had never once told one of them a thing that wasn't so. Not with my ears, my tail, my eyes. Not even Ghadban, when he begged me to.

And I looked at that boy on the ground with *ramad* written eight times around him under eight small burning faces. I thought: *He's fifteen. He's only just learned to laugh. He has a friend for the first time in his life, and that friend is somewhere in this town right now saying sorry to the dead in the walls, and he'll come back soon, and they'll eat together and argue about the stars.*

I thought: *Let him have that. A little while. Let him have a little longer before his face looks like theirs.*

So I lied.

I barked. Once. *Yes.* The bark he had taught me himself, on the second evening out of Hilat al-Sarab.

Then I went to him and wagged, my whole back end swinging, and pushed my head under his arm and leaned my whole weight into his chest the way I do when everything is all right, and looked up at him with my ears soft and my eyes warm.

*Yes*, all of me said. *Yes. There's a way. Don't be afraid.*

---

And my body let me.

I'd braced for it, even as I leaned into him: the hackles coming up along my spine on their own, the growl, the stone dragged over stone, the sound I keep for liars. Nothing came. My back lay flat as still water. For the first time in all my years, the thing in me that cannot abide a lie lay down and let me tell one. I lied with my whole body, and not one hair of it argued.

And somewhere in Theyab's bag, muffled in its rag, Old Bakhit's little brass bell gave one small clear note.

*Ting.*

It went up into the dark of the hollow and came back off the rock, again and again, smaller each time. *Ting. Ting. Ting.* As if all eleven of them were ringing it.

Theyab's hand was on my back. He felt the hackles lying flat under his palm, and he heard the bell. He'd been watching for both before I'd even moved. He had learned to read me better than any of them. He looked into my eyes, the amber one and the silver, and didn't say anything.

That was the worst. I'd been ready for him to push me off, or shout. But he only looked at me a long moment, and then he looked away, up at the eight burning children, and he nodded slightly, as if I'd confirmed something.

Which, of course, I had.

"Okay," he said quietly.

That was all.

He didn't push me away. He left his hand there, on my flat, lying back, for a long time.

Nothing I've ever borne hurt as much as that one lie, and that one small word he gave me back.

I'll be honest with you now. I don't know whether I lied to protect him, or to protect myself from watching his face if I'd let him see *no*.

Both, probably. People are rarely only one thing. Not even dogs. Especially not dogs who are only half of anything.

---

Hazza' came back with a full waterskin and glass-dust in his beard, talking before he was even into the hollow.

"There's a cistern under the east wall, it's full, it's disgusting, I *think* the thing in it is only a toad. And there are more of them down there. In the walls. A whole room of them, sitting in rows, like they were waiting for someone to—"

He stopped. He looked at Theyab on the ground, at me pressed against him with my ears down, and up, slowly, at the eight burning children in the rock.

"What happened?"

Lying wasn't open to Theyab. He told him all of it, the carvings and the vision and its smell, in a flat quiet voice.

He didn't tell him what I'd done. He left that out. I've never forgotten that he left that out.

Hazza' set down the waterskin very carefully. Then he walked to the first of the eight and read it out loud, slowly, frowning at the old letters. His voice came back off the rock, quieter, as if the hollow were reading along.

"*The Weaver's Daughter*," he read. "*Who wove the black tents of her people, and in her fifteenth year climbed the great dune under the full moon, and opened her hands, and the grey went away. And of her there remained only*—" His voice caught. "*Ramad.*"

He went to the next. *In his fourteenth year.* And the next. *In his twelfth year.* The boy of salt: *In his ninth year.* His voice got quieter and harder with each one. He read all eight. Then he read the first, the ninth, and the eleventh. *He refused.* *He refused.* *He refused.*

Then he stood in the middle of the hollow with his fists clenched at his sides.

"*Ninth year*," he said. "That one was nine. Theyab. That one was younger than Zaid."

"Hazza'—"

"*No*." He swung round. His eyes were too bright in the dusk. "No. Look at them. Look at them, they're *babies*. Three of them said no. Three of them *lived*."

"The first one became the grey," Theyab said quietly. "You told me. This whole town is what happened when he said no. The people in the walls."

"Then he said no *wrong*." Hazza''s voice rang off the rock. "Eight burned and three ran. That's two ways. Who says there are only two? Who *decided* that? Who decided it had to be *children*? A rock? A falconer's story?"

"The One who made the sands," said Theyab. "That's what Bakhit said. That's whose light it is."

Hazza' looked up out of the hollow at the open sky, at the stars, at the moon swelling over the dunes. He had the broken sword in his fist, though I don't think he knew he'd drawn it.

"Then I'll ask," he said. His voice shook. "I'll climb that dune myself and I'll stand on it and I'll ask. I mean it. I've already had one boy *taken away* from me because someone decided there was only one way things could go. I'm not standing here and letting it happen again. I'm *not*."

Every hair on my body lay flat as still water. He meant every word.

I'll tell you something. When he stood in that hollow and shouted *who decided*, I loved him for it. It was the first time in all my years that anyone had shouted it with me, instead of me snarling it alone at a sky that doesn't answer.

It didn't help. It never does.

Theyab looked at him a long time. And then he smiled. Small and tired and sad. But real.

"Okay," he said. "A third way."

He didn't believe it. But he'd seen how much Hazza' needed it, and he *would* look for one. That was true. I didn't growl. I pushed my nose into Hazza''s hand instead, and he rubbed my ears without seeming to know he was doing it.

---

They slept in the hollow of the rock that night, because it was the only place in the town out of the wind. Neither of them would sleep in the lanes, with the shadows.

Hazza' lay with his back to the rock and his broken sword across his chest, facing the way in. He didn't say much more. Once, in the dark, I heard him say, "Zaid would love this place," to nobody. Then, a long while later, so quietly I almost missed it: "No. He wouldn't." Then he was quiet.

Theyab didn't sleep for a long time.

He took out the broken golden feather Old Bakhit had given him and turned it over. It seemed to me that the break across its middle had closed. Only a little. Only the width of a hair. As if facing one hard truth had mended something in it. I've wondered since what it was mending itself *for*.

He didn't notice. He was looking at the twelfth place on the rock: past the clouded Runner, one more stretch of smooth black varnish. Empty.

Not yet.

And past it, the smooth black face went on, curving away into the dark. Room for a thirteenth. Room for a great many more. Whoever had chosen this rock had not been planning for twelve.

Theyab looked at the twelfth place a long time. Then he lay down beside Hazza' and closed his eyes.

I lay across the way in and kept watch. Above the rim of the hollow the moon hung half full. It was halfway to the dune.

Some time after midnight, he moved.

He didn't wake. But his right hand came out from under his cloak, and the forefinger, the one with the torn nail, reached out and began to write in the fine film of glass-dust on the ground. Slowly, carefully, right to left.

*Ra. Mim. Alif. Dal.*

Then again beside it. *Ramad.* And again. Moving in a slow ring around himself, the way he'd ringed the well at Bir al-Ghina', until he lay in the middle of a little circle of the word.

And then, in a hand that wasn't quite his, one more word. Very small. In letters Hazza' hadn't taught him yet. The same shapes the dunes had written at him that night on the ridge.

He couldn't read it now either. He was asleep. But his finger knew it.

I can read it. I'm a dog, and I can read every script these sands have ever been written in. I've had time.

I wiped it all away before dawn, with my forefeet and my tail, every letter. You'll learn what the dunes were spelling when he did, and not before. That much at least I'll let him have.

I counted the night. Night fourteen. I wished I had never learned to count. I wished, for the first time in a very long time, that I had never learned to read.

---

*End of Chapter Ten*


---

# Chapter Eleven
## Riders of the Storm

> *Ride with the shamal, never across it. And if the wind says your name, do not answer.*
> — what every Bedouin father tells his son before the first sandstorm, and again after the last

---

The eighteenth night since the thin moon at Bir al-Ghina'. The moon was fat and white and getting fatter, and I'd stopped looking at it.

We'd left Ghidd al-Zujaj three days before, walking east toward the oases. Hazza' had decided that if the carvings didn't know a third way, somebody alive might. "Every old man between here and Buraimi has his own version of every story," he said. "One of them has to be the one where the boy lives."

Nobody answered. The rock had shown us eleven bearers cut into stone, and every one of them was a child. You don't argue with a wall like that. You just stop sleeping well.

Theyab walked. He found water. When Hazza' made jokes he laughed at them, a little too quickly, like a man paying a debt.

I kept my distance. I'd told one lie in that carved place, without a single word, and it had used up whatever it is in a dog that does the work of talking. Theyab hadn't mentioned it once. He still let me sleep against his side. Somehow that was worse.

On the morning of the eighteenth day, Theyab stopped on top of a dune and lifted his face to the air.

"Storm," he said.

Hazza' sniffed too. "I don't smell anything."

"Like an old thaler. Like a coin someone's been holding under his tongue." Theyab was looking north-west, where the sky along the edge of the world had gone the yellow of an old bruise, the kind that never quite heals. "Shamal. It's coming fast." He frowned. "And something else."

I'd smelled it for an hour and hoped I was wrong. Smoke without fire. Ash without heat. Burning cloth a long way off, and under it the sweet, flat smell of a room where somebody has been dead for days and nobody has opened the door.

Al-Ghasaq. The grey.

From the dunes behind us came a snarl like tearing cloth.

---

They came over the ridge to the west in a long grey line.

Jinn-jackals first: eight, ten, more, running low, with darkness streaming off their backs like smoke off a wet fire and their eyes burning like coals blown on. They don't pant. A hunting dog notices that. They run and run and never breathe, because there is nothing inside them that needs to.

Behind the jackals, on camels, came men.

Racing camels, the thin-legged kind the sheikhs breed for wagers, running with their heads high and their mouths open, roaring. The riders sat them light and forward, as if they weighed nothing at all.

The riders wore grey robes. Their faces were smooth white masks with nothing on them: no eyes, no mouth, no nose. A blank white oval, like the inside of a skull scraped clean. Every mask was pointed the same way, at the same boy, the way a falcon's head points at the lure.

Each one had a blade at his hip that didn't shine. It was grey, the flat, swallowed grey of a sky with nothing behind it. Steel quenched in the Ifrit's ash.

The Nameless.

Twelve of them. Theyab counted under his breath, and I watched the colour go out of him number by number.

They were humming. No words, no tune, rising and falling with the camels' strides, and it came across the sand without getting any quieter, the way a sound does in a dream.

"Friends of yours?" said Hazza'.

"They hunt me."

"Of course they do." Hazza' drew his broken sword. "Twelve of them, ten jackals, two of us and one bad-tempered dog. I like those odds."

From somewhere in Theyab's pack, muffled, came a small *ting*.

Old Bakhit's bell. It does not ring for silence. It rings for liars.

"I don't like those odds," Hazza' admitted. "Run."

---

Nobody outruns a racing camel on foot. Not even me. By the time the boys had stumbled down into the next hollow, the first rider was cresting the dune behind them, his camel sliding down the slip-face on its haunches in a hiss of sand.

Hazza' turned, planted his feet, and waited.

The rider came at him with the grey blade swinging low. Hazza' stepped *toward* it, which is not what anyone sensible does. He ducked under the swing, caught the rider's sleeve, and *pulled*, and the masked man went over the camel's shoulder into the sand. Hazza' was up behind the hump before the beast had finished roaring about it.

The man he'd thrown didn't get up straight away. He lay on his back in the sand with that blank white face turned to the sky, and from behind the mask, in a young man's voice, he said quite clearly:

"*Tell my mother I'll be home before the coffee's cold.*"

Then he got up, like a puppet lifted by the back of its robe, and walked toward Theyab.

"Theyab!" Hazza' wrenched the camel's head round by its halter rope. "*Come on!*"

A second camel had come down the slope. Its rider was turning to slash at Hazza's back. Theyab ran at it, ducked the long yellow teeth, grabbed the rider's ankle with both hands, and hauled. The masked man fell without a sound. Theyab scrambled up into the saddle in his place.

The camel went mad underneath him, roaring and spinning, trying to bite his knee. He grabbed the saddle horn and the rope together and clamped his legs. I took three strides and jumped. The Bedouin carry salukis on camelback across deep sand, so our feet are fresh for the hunt; I've ridden that way for longer than anyone has been writing it down. I landed behind him, scrabbled, and wedged myself against his back, and he didn't even yell at me.

Hazza' was already racing away. Theyab's camel saw its companion go and decided, as camels do, that wherever that was looked better than here.

It ran.

---

The dunes went past in a blur. Behind us came the Nameless, a grey tide pouring over every ridge, and under the drum of padded feet, steady and unhurried, that humming. Ahead and to our right, the sky had gone black, and the shamal was coming down on us like a wall falling over.

"*Right!*" Hazza' shouted. "We go right, around it—"

"*No!*" Theyab's voice cracked. "*With it! Ride with it!*"

"*What?*"

"*It's coming out of the north-west! Turn across it and it'll knock you flat! Go where it's going!*"

I'll give Hazza' this. He'd spent most of a month learning from Theyab how not to die, and he didn't waste a breath. He hauled his camel's head round until the storm was at its tail, south and east, the way the wind was running. Theyab followed.

And the storm swallowed us.

---

The world went brown. Then it went dark.

Sand everywhere, roaring like the sea off the pearling banks, so thick Theyab couldn't see his own hands. He pulled his ghutra across his face and rode blind. I pressed myself flat against his back, my nose buried in the cloth of his shirt, and felt his heart going like a trapped bird's.

And in the wind, there were voices.

The old men say it's only the sand singing. But I know the difference between the sand singing and the sand *talking*. Dozens of voices, faint and close at our ears, worn smooth with repeating:

*Is that you? Is someone there? I can't see. I can't see. Where did everyone go?*

And then, under all of them, close enough that I felt Theyab go rigid:

*Theyab.*

Not shouted. Said. Gently. The way someone says your name when they've found you after a long time looking.
