Every hair along my spine stood up. When something lies, my hackles rise and a growl starts in my chest before I've decided anything at all, and that voice was a lie from end to end, *especially* the kindness in it. I growled into his back, low and long, so he'd feel it through his ribs.

He didn't answer. I felt him bite down on it.

Behind us, the Nameless tried to cut across.

I heard it happen. A camel bellowed as its rider turned it sideways to the wind, and the shamal caught it like a sail and rolled it, rider and saddle and all, away into the dark. Then another. Then a jinn-jackal howled, a howl like nothing I'd heard from them before, as the wind tore the shadow off it in strips and there turned out to be nothing underneath.

The riders didn't scream when they went. They just kept humming, right up until the storm took the sound away.

One rider was still with us, close behind, running in the lee of our camels. Theyab didn't see him until the grey blade came out of the brown, low and fast.

He twisted. It missed his leg and caught his forearm, a shallow cut along the old golden cracks.

But for one long heartbeat, Theyab couldn't remember what honey tasted like.

He knew he'd eaten it. But the sweetness itself was gone, cut out clean, and where it had been was a cold blank with smooth edges, like the place a tooth used to be.

Then it seeped back, slowly. Mostly. He gasped.

I don't think it all came back. It never does. The ash-quenched blades don't cut flesh so much as they cut *you*, and you heal the way a dune heals over a footprint: smooth on top, and something missing underneath that you'll never be able to point to.

Hazza's camel came round out of the storm, and the broken sword came down, and the rider behind us was gone. His mask spun away into the dark, still humming.

---

We came out of the worst of it into a place of stone.

Ruins, older than anything Theyab had ever dug in. A long road ran between rows of carved pillars, half sunk in sand, half fallen, like the ribs of something that had died on its back. There were faces cut into the pillars, and every one had had its eyes chiselled out. Carefully, all the way down the road.

The camels didn't like it.

Hazza's stopped so suddenly he went over its neck. Theyab's dropped to its knees and threw us both, and by the time Theyab had rolled to his feet both camels were gone into the brown, halter ropes flying. They'd rather the storm. Animals are wise about places.

"Well," said Hazza', spitting out sand. "That's gratitude."

Then the ground shook.

One of the great pillars beside him had been leaning for a thousand years. The shamal had been leaning on it for an hour. Now, with a long groan like the wall in the old ruins on the morning this story began, it gave up.

"*Hazza'!*"

He threw himself sideways. He almost made it.

The pillar came down across his legs with a sound like the end of the world. Hazza' didn't scream. He made a short, punched-out noise, as if every bit of air had been knocked out of him, and then he lay very still, face-down from the knees under a column of carved stone.

"I'm all right," he said into the sand. His voice was thin. "Nothing's broken. I think nothing's broken. It's just *very heavy*."

Theyab got his shoulder under the edge and heaved until the cracks on his arms flared, and the stone didn't so much as tremble. It weighed as much as a house. It weighed as much as a life.

I dug. It's what my kind does when something we love is under something. I tore at the sand beside Hazza's hip until my claws split, and the sand poured back in as fast as I threw it out.

From the storm behind us, out of the dark between the pillars, came a snarl like tearing cloth.

---

Six jackals. Four masked riders on foot, camels lost, grey blades drawn. They came out of the swirling brown slowly, because they could see there was no hurry. A boy who couldn't move a stone. A sheikh's son who couldn't move at all. A dog with bleeding feet.

The riders stopped in a line. They tilted their blank white faces, all four of them, to exactly the same angle at exactly the same moment.

"*Is that you?*" said one, in an old woman's voice.

"*Before the coffee's cold,*" said another.

"*Don't let it in,*" said the third. "*Don't let it in. Don't let it in.*"

The fourth said nothing. The fourth had a child's voice, I learned later, and had nothing left to say at all.

Theyab reached for the Light. It came grey and thin and sputtering, the way it had with the jackals at the well, because he wasn't thinking about anyone else now. Terror filled him to the brim and left no room for anything gold.

"Theyab," said Hazza' from the sand. "Go. *Go.* Ride the storm out. You know how. Leave me—"

"*No.*"

The first jackal sprang.

And I stopped being only a dog.

---

I'll tell you how it feels, because nobody has ever asked me. Nobody could.

It feels like drowning in reverse. Something cold and enormous that has been folded small inside me for a very long time begins to *unfold*, and it doesn't care what it breaks on the way out.

My silver eye goes first, the clouded one the Bedouin mutter about. It doesn't burn; it *opens*, wider than an eye should, and behind it there's no dog at all. Then the dark comes up out of the place where half of me used to be. It isn't a part of me. It's a part of *it*, a piece of the old curse I swallowed long ago, dragging someone out of it. It has always been patient.

My body didn't grow. That's the part that makes the men who've glimpsed it wake screaming years later. I stayed a saluki, lean and sand-coloured, ears flat to my skull, four bleeding feet planted in the sand.

It was my *shadow* that grew.

It poured out from under me the wrong way, toward the light instead of away from it, up the pillars, up the storm. Long, too long, a dog's shape stretched past anything a dog should be, ears like spearheads among the broken capitals, a jaw that opened and kept opening, row on row of teeth in it. It didn't lie on the ground the way a shadow does. It *stood*. The black that was there before anything was lit, pouring upward like smoke going back into a fire. And where its eyes should be, two coals.

And I could hear them. The ones inside the dark. Every one of the emptied the grey has ever eaten, all the last words of everyone who has ever been forgotten, rushing through me like wind through a ruined house. *Is someone there. Where did everyone go. Don't let it in.*

One voice under the others, quiet and familiar, saying a name I won't repeat.

The jackal that had sprung at Theyab met my shadow in mid-air and simply came apart, like fog walking into a wall.

I put my shoulder to the fallen pillar, a dog's shoulder, thin as a boy's wrist. I *pushed*, and the strength that came wasn't mine. It came up out of the dark through my legs, and the stone rose, the whole length of it, grinding off Hazza's legs the way you'd lift a stick off a sleeping child. I heard my own bones creak, and something under them that wasn't bone.

Hazza' dragged himself out on his elbows, and I heard him make a sound when he looked up past me and saw what was standing over him on the wall of the storm. It was not a brave sound. I don't blame him.

And then, because I was angry, and because the jinn part of me is always angry, I threw it.

It went end over end through the swirling sand and came down on the four masked men with a sound I won't describe to you. I'll only tell you that the humming stopped. All four voices at once, mid-note, the way a door cuts off a room.

The other jackals turned to run. I caught two. I didn't need my teeth. My shadow's mouth closed over them where they ran, and they went into the dark inside me like water into sand, and the dark was glad. That's the part I've never told anyone. *It was glad.*

Then it was quiet, except for the wind and my own breath, which was not a dog's breath. It frosted the sand at my feet.

I turned round. A thin old saluki standing in a ring of frost, and behind him and above him, rising off him like a tent pole holds up a tent, the shape of something with ember eyes and its long jaw still working on nothing. I looked at the boy I'd spent fifteen years protecting.

---

I've hidden that shape from every bearer I ever walked with. All eleven. Not one of them ever saw it.

I told myself it was to keep them from being afraid of me.

It was really to keep me from seeing their faces when they were.

Theyab's face, looking up at me in that storm, was the worst thing I've ever seen. And I have seen eleven children burn.

He didn't see me. Not the dog. All he saw was a jinn standing over Hazza' with its mouth full of teeth, the biggest thing of the grey he'd seen since the zawba'a took the caravan. And somewhere in the voices of the storm around us, one said his name. It wasn't mine. I have no words a man can hear, not even then. All he felt was fear, pure and total, filling him like water filling a well, all the way to the top and over.

"*Get away from him!*"

The Light came out of him like the end of the world.

It wasn't gold. It wasn't grey. It was both, twisted together, pouring out of his hands and his chest and his eyes in a raging white torrent, gold shot through with grey like smoke through fire. He didn't aim it. He couldn't have. It *burst* out of him, and it hit my shadow full in the chest, and my shadow was me.

Light and jinn don't mix. Nothing in the world burns the dark the way that Light does.

I howled.

Salukis are a quiet kind. I had never howled like that in all my years. It came out of me broken and many-throated, every voice inside me screaming at once, and went ringing off the pillars and up into the storm. The Light tore into the jinn half of me like fire into dry grass, and for one moment I felt the dark in me *afraid*, which I had never felt and hope never to feel again. It burned my silver eye. It burned down into the cold place where the dark lived, looking for the bottom, and it *hurt*. More than the curse did, the first time it took me. More than anything.

I fell.

And as I fell, the shadow fell with me, running back under my feet like water out of a broken jar, and the voices went with it, the quiet familiar one last of all. By the time I hit the sand there was only a dog, scorched and smoking down my left flank, my silver eye squeezed shut and weeping something that wasn't tears, and a shadow the ordinary size, lying the ordinary way.

The light went out of Theyab all at once. He stood there swaying. His arms were cracked gold from wrist to shoulder.

"Ghareeb?" he whispered.

---

The vision came the moment the Light went out, the way it always did now, like a debt being collected. Longer this time. Clearer. Closer.

The full moon, so near he could see the grey stains on its face like old thumbprints. The great dune of Tal Moreeb beneath his feet, its crest a knife-edge of sand. The fire climbing his arms, and this time he could *feel* it: not pain yet, but heat, rising, the way the air rises off a coffee-roasting pan just before you touch it. Somebody below him on the dune, shouting. And for the first time, the sand under his bare feet, cold as a grave, and how the cold and the heat met somewhere around his knees.

Then it let him go, and he was on his knees in the ruins, and he could still smell his own hair burning. He touched it. It wasn't. He touched it again anyway.

"Ghareeb," he said. "*Ghareeb.* I didn't know— I didn't— Ghareeb, I'm sorry, I'm so sorry—"

He crawled toward me with his hands out.

I turned my whole face to the sand, so he couldn't see the eye and I couldn't see his. If I'd had a voice for him, I'd have said: *don't look at me.*

I turned away from him for a long time after that.

---

The storm blew itself out by nightfall.

They made camp among the pillars, because Hazza' couldn't walk far; his legs were bruised black from the knee down, but by some mercy nothing was broken. Theyab built a fire from a dead calotropis and its dry pods, and turned the nearest stone face toward the dunes so its empty sockets wouldn't watch us. Then he gathered me into his lap, scorched side up, and curled himself around me, and I let him, because I had no strength left to get up and go.

He kept saying he was sorry into my fur until the words stopped sounding like words, and even asleep his hands kept checking that I was still there.

I lay in his arms with my good eye half open, and I watched Hazza'.

Hazza' didn't sleep either.

He waited until Theyab's breathing had gone slow, then limped to the fallen pillar to see if any of them were alive. Hazza' was like that, even with them.

One of them was. Barely.

He lay apart from the others, half under the stone. His mask had cracked down the middle, and Hazza' had to pull at it. It had grown to his face at the edges, the way bark grows over a nail driven into a ghaf tree. Under it was a face thin and grey and frightened. Not much older than Hazza'.

His lips moved. Even I couldn't hear. Hazza' bent down close. Whatever he said, it took all he had, and then his lips stopped, and his eyes stayed open on the moon.

Hazza' knelt there a long while. Then he reached into the dead man's robe and drew something out.

A letter. Folded small and sealed with grey wax.

He held it up to the moon and went still. He'd seen that seal before, at the bottom of the order that sent him out of Ubar with half a sword. The personal seal of the Wazir Muhaisen.

He broke it and read.

I'll tell you what it said, because you'll need to know, and because Theyab couldn't have read it then even if he'd seen it. It was signed *The Voice*. Soft, careful handwriting, every letter perfectly formed, like the hand of someone who has never once been in a hurry. It gave orders. On the Night of Lamps, when every lamp in Ubar was lit for the lost bearers, Sheikh Mutlaq was to die.

Hazza' read it twice. His hand was shaking.

Then he folded it very small. He twisted the brass pommel off the hilt of his broken sword and slid the letter into the hollow where, in the old swords of Ubar, a smith would roll up a written blessing for the man who carried it. He screwed the pommel back on, tight.

He came back to the fire and sat beside us with the sword across his knees. He looked at Theyab asleep, the gold still glowing faintly up both arms. He looked at me, and I didn't pretend to be asleep, and he didn't pretend he hadn't seen what had stood over him that afternoon.

He didn't wake Theyab. He didn't tell him.

I think he meant to. I think he wanted Theyab, for one more night, to carry only one terrible thing instead of two.

We were a fine pair, the sheikh's son and I. Two keepers of secrets, sitting up in the dark among blind stone faces, watching over the same sleeping boy.

Above us the moon hung nearly full, white and patient, like an eye that has decided not to blink.

---

*End of Chapter Eleven*


---

# Chapter Twelve
## The Night of the Full Moon

> *Under the badr, every promise is weighed. And what is found wanting is taken.*
> — an old saying of the oasis people, spoken over water at weddings and at graves

---

I made no sound at all before the full moon. Not a whine, not a growl, not a sigh through the nose.

I limped behind the two of them with my coat scorched brown down my left side and my silver eye swollen shut. The burn wept a thin black fluid that smoked when it touched the sand. I didn't eat much. At night I slept away from the fire, and every night Theyab looked over at me before he lay down, and every night I turned my face away.

He tried to say sorry. Once he left me the best part of a roasted sandgrouse and sat with his back to me so I could eat unwatched. I ate it after he'd fallen asleep. I want that on the record.

I wasn't angry with him. You need to understand that, because he didn't. I wasn't angry at all.

I was ashamed. He had seen what stands behind me. He had heard the voices that live in me. And there is no word for how that feels, in any language I've learned, except that it's the opposite of being remembered.

---

Hazza' was different too, after the storm.

He still joked, still complained about his feet and the general unfairness of sand. He still drew letters every evening, and Theyab could read whole short lines now, right to left, his finger following the way Hazza' had shown him. One night Hazza' wrote three words in big letters beside the fire, *Theyab and Hazza': monsters*, and made him read them aloud, and they both laughed, and for a moment the dark beyond the firelight felt a long way off.

Remember those words. I'm sorry. I have to ask you to remember them.

But Hazza' had started touching the pommel of his sword, resting his thumb on the brass cap the way Theyab touched his feather mark. And when he thought no one was looking, he'd stand on a dune and stare west toward Ubar, the way you stare at a house where you know someone is waiting for you in the dark.

"We're changing direction," he announced on the fourth morning. "West. I need to go home."

Theyab stared at him. "You were exiled."

"I know. I was there."

"They'll kill you."

"They'll *try*." Hazza's grin came and went too fast, like a lamp swinging past a window. "I need to see Zaid. I need to make sure he's all right."

My hackles stayed flat. That was true. Every word.

But Theyab had learned to watch the spaces between words, too. He looked at Hazza' a long moment. He knew there was something more. And because Theyab had never in his life asked anyone for something they didn't want to give, he didn't ask.

I knew what it was. I'd watched it go into the hilt. I couldn't have told him if I'd wanted to, and I didn't try.

---

Every night, the moon got fuller.

Theyab stopped looking at it. Then he started looking at it all the time. He'd lie on his back by the fire with his hand on his chest, staring up at that white face as it swelled a little more each evening, and I knew what he was seeing. The dune. The fire. The sound of somebody shouting a name at the bottom of the world.

He'd started waking in the night and patting his arms and hair the way you pat out sparks. Once I saw him hold his hand an inch above the coals and keep it there, his face blank, as if he were trying to learn the feeling in advance. Hazza' knocked his wrist away and said nothing, and Theyab said nothing, and they didn't speak of it.

He knew Tal Moreeb was nowhere near. But every full moon would bring it one month closer, and this was the first one since he'd read the word *ash* at the end of eight lines on a carved rock.

On the twenty-ninth night, it rose full.

It came up out of the eastern dunes huge and white and close, exactly as in his vision. The desert went silver, and nothing moved in it, not a jerboa, not a breath of wind. The desert was holding still the way a hare does when the falcon's shadow passes over.

It was the brightest night Theyab had ever seen, and he sat by the fire and couldn't stop shaking.

One month. Thin moon to badr, and the dark between. That's how long they'd had.

I knew it. I'd counted. Neither of them knew.

---

It started, as the worst things do, with someone trying to help.

"Come to Ubar with me," Hazza' said.

He'd been quiet a long time, sitting across the fire turning his signet ring round and round on his finger. Now he leaned forward, and his face in the firelight was serious in a way Theyab had hardly ever seen it.

"I mean it. Under the city there are the tombs, and down among the tombs there's the old library. Every book anyone in Ubar ever wrote. Every story. Every name. Hundreds of years of them, Theyab, rooms and rooms, all the way down into the dark. If there's a third way written anywhere in this desert, it's there. You can read now. Not fast. But we could look together."

"Hazza'—"

"I can get you in. I know a way through the kitchens. Umm Shamma would hide an army if I asked her nicely and brought back the honey jars I stole."

"I can't go to Ubar."

"Why not?"

"Because it's not *there*." Theyab's voice cracked. "It's not in a book. You read them. On the rock. You read them out loud to me. Eight of them burned, and three of them ran, and the ones who ran—" He pointed east, at nothing, at the empty silver desert. "Look what happened. One of them *is* the Ifrit's now. Its mouth. That's the third way, Hazza'. That's the only other way there is. You become the thing in the storm."

"You don't *know* that."

"I've *felt* it! Every time I use the Light I'm standing on that dune and I can feel the fire coming up my legs, I can *smell* it, I wake up and I can still smell it—" He stopped. He pressed his hand flat over his chest. "It isn't a story anymore. It's going to happen. It's *written*."

"Then we unwrite it."

"You can't unwrite the *sand*!"

The words went out across the silver dunes and the desert swallowed them whole and gave nothing back.

Hazza' looked away first. He looked down at his hands. When he spoke again, his voice had gone quiet.

"Promise me something," he said.

Theyab didn't answer.

"Promise me you'll never give up." Hazza' looked up. His eyes were very bright. "Whatever happens. Whatever you see in your visions. Whatever the rocks say. You keep going. You don't just *lie down* and let it take you. Promise me."

Theyab looked at him across the fire, under the full moon.

He could not lie. You know that. So when he said it, he said it knowing that the feather on his chest would have burned like a coal if he hadn't meant it with everything he had.

"I promise," said Theyab.

It didn't burn.

And I, lying in the dark with my face turned away, felt the hair along my back lie down flat and smooth as a stroked cat's. I lifted my head. I couldn't help it. A true promise does that to me, the way a lie does the opposite.

And somewhere out in the silver dark, a long way off, something answered. Not a jackal. Not a voice. A single soft sound, like a heavy book being closed.

Neither of them heard it. I did. I've never known what it was. I've been afraid for a very long time that I do.

---

If they'd stopped there, this would be a different story.

They didn't stop there.

"Good," said Hazza'. He let out a long breath, almost a laugh. "Good. Then come to Ubar."

"That's not—"

"You just promised."

"I promised not to give up. Going to the dune isn't giving up." Theyab's voice was shaking. "Don't you see? It's the *opposite*. The ones who gave up are the ones who ran. If I keep going, Hazza', *that's* where I'm going. That's what not giving up *is*, for me. It's walking into the fire with my eyes open."

Hazza' stood up so fast he nearly kicked over the coffee pot.

"No," he said. "No. I'm not doing this again. I'm not standing by while someone I—" He bit it off. "I'm not letting them take you. I'll drag you to Ubar by your ankles. I'll lock you in the tombs. I'll find the third way myself and read it to you through the door. I am *not* losing anybody else."

"You can't save me."

"Watch me."

And then Theyab said it.

He didn't plan it. I know that, because I know him. He was frightened, so frightened, and tired past the point where tiredness has a bottom, and the moon hung full and white above him like an eye that wouldn't close. Hazza' was standing over him promising things nobody could promise. And it hurt so much to be loved like that, when he knew exactly how it ended, that something in him reached for the sharpest true thing it could find, and threw it.

"You can't save me," he said. "You couldn't even save Suhail."

---

The fire cracked.

Hazza' didn't move. He didn't shout. His face didn't even change, at first. He stood there with the full moon behind him, looking down at Theyab, and something went out of his eyes. It was like watching a lamp go out on a high wall in Ubar, one of the ones that must never all go out, and then the next one along, and the next.

My hackles didn't stir. I wish they had. But it was true, every word, and nothing in the world cuts like the truth said at the wrong moment for the wrong reason.

Theyab saw his face. He saw what he'd done.

He got up. He didn't say sorry. He couldn't make his mouth do it. He turned round and walked away from the fire, out into the silver dark.

"Theyab," said Hazza' behind him.

Just his name. Once. Not loud.

Theyab didn't turn round.

I've thought about that moment more than any other in this story. More than the dune, more than the fire. A boy walking away across moonlit sand, and another boy standing alone by a small fire, saying a name once into the wind.

You know the old saying. *Never say your name to the wind.*

Nobody ever told Hazza' what happens when you say someone else's.

Theyab meant to come back. You should know that. He meant to walk until he'd stopped shaking, then come back and make it right. There'd be time in the morning. That's the oldest lie there is, and the feather never burns for it, because nobody knows it's a lie until afterwards.

He walked away and left Hazza' alone.

---

And where was I?

I'll tell you the first part.

I'd smelled jinn-jackals that evening, before the argument started. A long way off, downwind, but coming, drawn by the Light still glowing in the cracks on Theyab's arms. I'd been lying a little way from the fire with my scorched side to the cold sand, my nose working. When Theyab walked off into the dark, I got up and went the other way.

I went hunting.

A saluki runs down what runs. I let the jinn half out. It hurt worse than it ever had, the burned side tearing open as the shadow came up out of it, and the voices inside were louder that night, as if the full moon had pulled them up near the surface the way it pulls the tide over the coastal flats. But there were jackals in the dunes and the boy I loved was alone out there, and that is the only use I've ever found for the thing I carry.

I hunted them across the silver sand under the full moon, for most of the night. My shadow ran ahead of me, longer than the dunes.

That's all I'm going to tell you about where I was. Don't ask me any more. Not yet.

I said, *don't ask*.

---

Theyab didn't go far. He climbed a high dune a long way off and sat at the top, knees pulled up to his chest, the moon pouring white light down on him until he cast almost no shadow at all. He could still see the fire, very small, a single orange point down in the hollow where he'd left it.

He said sorry to it. Out loud, over and over, practising.

*I'm sorry. I didn't mean it. You were right to try. You were right to try to save him. You were right to try to save me.*

Some time after the middle of the night, he looked down, and the little orange point had gone out.

He told me later he'd thought nothing of it. Fires burn down. He said it twice, and the second time his mark burned, faintly, and he had to start over. *I told myself it was nothing.* That one was true.

Near dawn, with the moon sinking into the western dunes and the sky going the grey of old bone, he fell asleep sitting up.

He didn't dream. No visions, no warnings. He felt nothing at all. Not a whisper in the sand. Not a single bell.

The sky was very quiet that night. I've never forgiven it for that. It's a long list, the things I haven't forgiven the sky for. Most of them are quiet nights.

---

He woke when the sun touched his face. He scrambled up and ran down the dune, sliding and stumbling, the words ready in his mouth. *I'm sorry. You were right to try.*

He came over the last rise and stopped.

There were camels in the hollow. Five of them, kneeling and grumbling, with slabs of grey salt from the coastal sabkha roped to their sides. And there were men, five travellers in dusty white thobes, standing in a ring around the cold black circle of the fire.

Five grown Bedouin and not one of them talking. One had his hand over his mouth.

They were looking down at something on the sand.

Theyab walked toward them. He didn't feel his legs moving. The morning was very bright and very still, and the only sound was a saker falcon on one man's wrist, screaming and screaming under its hood, the way animals scream at something they can smell and can't see.

Hazza' lay on his back beside the dead fire, one arm flung out, his broken sword in the sand by his open hand.

He was grey.

Not the grey of the emptied. Something worse. His skin had gone the colour of old ash, every part of it, his lips, his eyelids, the beds of his nails. And running all through the grey, across his face and down his throat and along both arms, were fine bright lines of *gold*. Thin glowing cracks, like the glaze on a broken coffee cup. They were still glowing. Faintly. Fading even as Theyab watched, one by one, the way the last sparks go in a fire nobody is feeding.

His eyes were open, looking up at the morning sky. They had gone pale. Not white. Pale, like a window someone has breathed on.

His mouth was a little open too, as though he'd been about to say something. As though someone had come up to the fire in the dark, and he'd looked up, and started to say a name.

The sand around him was smooth. Perfectly smooth, the way the wind leaves it, for ten paces in every direction. No struggle. No tracks. Not even a beetle's. As if the desert had tidied up after itself.

And beside the fire, untouched, not a grain out of place, the words he'd written in the sand that evening, before the argument, and never rubbed out. Theyab hadn't noticed they were still there. He noticed now.

*Theyab and Hazza': monsters.*

Except the first word had been smoothed away. Just that one. The rest were perfect.

---

One of the travellers saw Theyab first.

An older man with a beard gone yellow at the edges. He backed away a step, then another.

They looked at Theyab. They looked at his arms, at the golden cracks still glowing faintly from wrist to shoulder.

"That's him," said the young man with the falcon. His voice was high and thin. "The sheikh's son. The exiled one. The son of Mutlaq of Ubar. Look at the jaw, the scar. They said at Buraimi he was out here selling himself for a sword—"

"And *that*," said the old man, staring at Theyab, "is the glowing boy."

"From the caravan," the old man said. "They were talking about him at the last well. The one the sand-devil didn't eat. A man walked out of the sand alive and he's telling everyone from here to the coast." He was backing toward his camel. "*Monster*, he said. And here he is."

"I didn't," said Theyab.

"I didn't do this."

The feather mark didn't burn. He felt it not burn. It was true, every word. And it didn't matter at all, because these men had never heard of a boy who couldn't lie, and if they had, they wouldn't have believed it.

There were other true things, too. They came up in him one after another, like water rising in a well that's about to drown you.

*I left him.*

*I said the worst thing I could think of, and I left him alone in the dark, and I watched his fire go out and I told myself it was nothing.*

"I left him," said Theyab. "I walked away. I left him alone."

The feather didn't burn for that either.

The travellers stared at him. And then one of them, I never learned which, said it: the word that would follow Theyab across the whole desert, carried on falcons' legs and in traders' mouths and around every coffee hearth from Liwa to the Hajar, faster than any racing camel could run.

"*Prince-Killer.*"

They ran.

They had the camels up and away in moments, salt slabs swinging, the falcon still screaming. East, toward the oases. Toward everyone.

*Prince-Killer. Prince-Killer. The glowing boy killed the sheikh's son.*

---

Theyab knelt in the sand beside Hazza'.

He didn't touch him at first. He knelt there with his hands open on his knees and the gold fading in Hazza's face, crack by crack, until there were only a few lines left glowing at the corners of his eyes, like the last of something trying to get out.

Then, very slowly, he reached out to close Hazza's eyes.

They didn't want to close. The lids were stiff and cold as wax and slid back open, a little, both times. The third time he held his fingers there and counted, the way you count through a pain, and when he let go they stayed. Mostly. A thin pale line of eye still showed beneath each lid, watching nothing.

He wanted to heal him. Of course he did. His hands were already burning. He laid them on Hazza's chest and called the Light, and it came, gold and desperate, more gold than it had ever come for anyone. It poured into Hazza' and went looking.

It found nothing. Not a dark place, not a hollow like one of the emptied, where something had been scooped out. *Nothing.* As if there had never been anyone in there. The Light went down and down and touched no bottom, and came back to him cold.

He'd never seen it fail before.

And then, because it always did now, the vision came. Not of Hazza'. There was no past or future in Hazza' to see. Only his own: the full moon, the dune, the fire climbing. But this time, for one breath, he wasn't alone in it. Somebody was standing on the sand below him, just at the edge of the light, very still, looking up. He couldn't see the face.

Then it was gone, and he was kneeling in the morning with his hands on a cold chest.

He took Hazza's hand. It was cold. The fingers were grey and cracked with gold, and on the right hand, on the finger where the heavy signet ring had always been, there was nothing. Only a band of paler skin where it had sat for years. The ring he never took off.

He looked at his own arms, at the cracks. Then at the cracks on Hazza's face. The same gold. He'd been asleep. He'd been far away. He'd felt nothing. But the Light had burst out of him once already when he didn't mean it to, and nearly burned a dog out of the world.

He thought of the little orange point going out.

*Did I do this?*

He would carry that question for a long time. I'm sorry to tell you that. I could have taken it from him, if I'd had a tongue for words, or the courage to find another way. I'm sorry to tell you that, too.

---

The broken sword was lying in the sand by Hazza's open hand.

Theyab picked it up.

It was lighter than he'd expected. Half a blade, dark leather on the grip, a brass pommel that didn't quite match. It was cold. Everything in that hollow was cold, though the sun was well up by then. He held it across his knees the way Hazza' used to hold it by the fire at night.

He didn't know what was inside it. Not yet.

The desert people bury their dead the same day, before the sun is high. Everyone knows that. Even a scavenger boy nobody ever invited to a funeral knows that. So when he could make his arms work, Theyab dug. With his hands, and with the broken sword, a shallow scrape in the hollow beside the fire, the way the poorest are buried, no shroud but the cloak Hazza' already wore. It wasn't deep enough. It couldn't be. The sand kept running back in.

He laid Hazza' in it and pushed the sand over him with his forearms. Then he sat beside it with the sword in his lap while the sun climbed and the shadows of the dunes went short.

Then the wind came, and began, gently, the way it does, to finish the work. A little over the place where the face was. A little more. A ridge of fine sand drifting across the hollow, smoothing it, until you couldn't have said where the grave began.

Theyab watched it happen and didn't stop it. He didn't cry. Not then. That came later.

He just sat, and held on.

---

I was already on the dune above the hollow when he came over the rise that morning. I'd been there before the travellers came.

Don't ask me how long.

I lay at the top of the slope, a long way off, with my scorched coat and my one good eye, and I didn't go any closer. My hackles lay flat. My tail lay in the sand behind me. Not a growl in me. Not a sound. Nobody down there was saying anything that needed me.

Theyab didn't look up. I'm not sure he knew I was there.

I could have gone down to him and let him hold me instead of a cold broken sword. I could have done what every hunter has seen a good saluki do: walked, and kept looking back over my shoulder until he followed. I could have shown him a track. One track. With a face at the end of it.

I didn't do anything.

I'm very good at that. I've told you before. It's becoming a theme.

It's the one I'm least proud of. It's the one that still wakes me, when I sleep, which isn't often. In the dream I'm always on that dune, and the fire is always going out, and I am always, always a moment too far away.

That's all. That's all you get. Turn the page.

---

*End of Chapter Twelve*


---

# PART FOUR: THE NAMELESS ONES


---

# Chapter Thirteen
## Nameless Again

> *The loneliest grave is the one no one visits. The second loneliest is the one that visits you.*
> — a saying of the old women who wash the dead, in the villages under the Hajar

---

For four days, he didn't say a word.

He walked. That was all. In the cold before dawn, in the hammering noon sun that kills goats where they stand, at night under a moon already beginning to thin, as if something had taken a bite out of it. He let the dunes choose the direction. If the sand was writing that week, it was writing for a boy who had stopped reading.

Hazza's sword was tied across his back, wrapped in his ghutra. It knocked against his spine with every step, and every time it did he flinched, and every time he flinched he kept walking. It had a rhythm to it after a while. Like a knock on a door. Like someone behind him, tapping his shoulder, wanting to be let back in.

He'd buried Hazza' the way the poor are buried, in a scrape, with no stone, and the wind had finished it. Then he'd walked away with the travellers' word chasing him the way stones had once chased him out of Hilat al-Sarab.

*Prince-Killer.*

He'd been called a lot of things. Nameless. Laqeet. Monster. Now he had a new one, and the worst part, the part that sat in his chest like a swallowed stone, was that he couldn't be sure it was wrong.

I followed a dune behind. I'd have walked closer, but I wasn't walking well.

---

You remember what his light did to my jinn half in the storm.

There was a patch on my left side, below the silver eye, where the coat had come away. What was underneath wasn't dog. It was dark and faintly smoking, like a coal dropped in water that hadn't decided whether to go out. Sometimes, at night, when it was very quiet, I could hear it whispering. Not to me. To itself. The same three words, over and over, in a voice I knew.

It hadn't healed. I'd stopped expecting it to.

The night of the full moon had made it worse. I'll say that much and no more.

And my shadow, since the storm, lay a hand's width longer than the sun said it should. A goatherd we passed stared at it, then drove his goats the long way round.

So I limped. And he walked. And neither of us looked at my shadow.

---

On the second day he found water: a wet finger to the wind, a hollow where a desert gourd trailed its green ropes and its bitter yellow fruit, digging until the sand went dark. I dug beside him. I wasn't going to stop being useful just because I'd stopped being forgiven.

At night he hardly slept. He sat at the top of whatever dune he'd stopped on, and with one finger he wrote in the sand.

The same word, every night. Four letters, right to left, slow and careful, the way you write a thing you've only just learned, tongue between his teeth. *Ha*, round as a knot. *Zay*, a little hook with its one dot riding on top. *Alif*, standing straight as a tent pole. *Ayn*, the open mouth at the end. Hazza' had made him practise it fifty times by the fire, laughing every time he forgot the dot on the *zay*. *Without the dot it's a different letter, Theyab. You've written a word that isn't me. It's my name. Have some respect.* He never forgot the dot anymore.

Hazza'.

And every morning the dunes had moved in the night, and the word was gone.

---

On the third morning, the word was still there.

Not where he'd written it. He'd written it on the crest, where he sat. This one was at the bottom of the slope, fifty paces down, in a smooth bowl of sand where the wind hadn't reached. Four letters. The dot on the *zay*. Pressed in deep with one finger, the way he pressed.

"Did you write this?" he said. Not to me, really. To the air.

I hadn't. I have paws. And I'd been beside him all night, and I'd have sworn on every bearer I ever buried that he never moved.

But there were his own footprints, going down to it from where we'd slept, and none coming back. I put my nose to them. They smelled of him. Only of him. And very faintly, under that, of cold ash.

He rubbed it out with his foot, carefully, until the sand was smooth. Then he looked at his finger for a while, as if it belonged to somebody else.

That night he didn't write anything at all.

---

On the third night he tried to lie.

He was sitting with his knees up and the sword across his lap, and he said, so quietly I nearly missed it, "It wasn't my fault."

My hackles came up before I'd decided anything. A growl started in my chest, low as distant thunder.

His face twisted. His hand flew to his chest. The feather mark flared under his shirt, hot and sharp, and he doubled over and pressed his palm against it until the light went out and there was a smell in the air of scorched cotton.

Most people get to tell themselves small kind lies in the dark. *There was nothing I could have done.* Lies like that are how people get up in the morning. Theyab never got any. The truth was all he had: he had walked away, and watched the fire go out, and called it nothing.

"The sand remembers," he whispered afterwards. The old words.

The mark didn't burn. It wasn't a lie. My hackles lay down.

But I think he'd started to hear it the other way round. Not *may the sand remember you*, like a blessing. *The sand remembers*, like a threat.

I limped up the slope and lay down beside him, close, and pushed my long nose under his hand until his palm was over my skull.

It's all a dog has. We can't say *it wasn't your fault.* We can only stay there, and let you feel our hair lying flat.

He didn't look at me.

"Don't," he said.

I kept my head under his hand. *Feel my back,* I thought at him. *I'm not lying to you.*

"You were like that at Ghidd al-Zujaj too," he said. "Right up until you weren't."

I had nothing for that. One small, merciful lie in fifteen years, told with my whole body and not a single word, and it had cost me every true thing I'd tell him afterwards. Remember that, if you ever think a lie is cheap.

He was quiet a while. Then, without looking at me: "Where were you?"

"That night," he said. "After I walked away. You weren't with him. You weren't with me. Where were you?"

I lifted my head out from under his hand.

I'm not going to tell you what I nearly did, or where I was. Not yet. It's my story, so I get to choose. That's the only power a storyteller has: to make you wait in the dark.

I put my head down on my paws.

He nodded slowly, as though I'd answered. Then he lay down with his back to me and the broken sword in his arms, and I lay beside him until Suhail went down behind the dunes and the stars went pale, and the burned place in my side whispered its three words to itself, and neither of us made a sound at all.

---

There was a twist of waxed cloth at the bottom of his bag. Three dates rolled in honey, the last of the ones he'd carried all the way from Hilat al-Sarab, gone sticky and dark.

On the fourth evening he took them out and held them in his palm for a long time.

Then he did something I didn't understand until later. He put one to his lips, just touched it there, and closed his eyes, and I watched his face go hunting for something. Searching, the way a tongue searches for a missing tooth.

He was trying to remember what honey tasted like. The ash-blade had taken it in the storm, and it had come back, mostly. *Mostly*. He was checking.

I don't know what he found. He wrapped them up again without eating any. Maybe he was saving them. Or else he was afraid to taste them and find nothing there at all.

---

The fourth night was the one I'd been afraid of.

He stopped at the bottom of a long slope instead of the top. In fifteen years he had always slept high. Now he walked down into the cold trough between two dunes, where the wind poured sand down the slip-face in hissing curtains, like something being poured over a body.

And he lay down.

On his back, sword on his chest, arms folded over it, looking up at the stars. He let the sand come.

It came softly. A trickle over his feet. A drift against his side. The dunes were moving the way they move every night, and he had lain down in the path of one, and he was very still, and he was watching the Pleiades with an expression I'd seen before on other faces, a long time ago. Relief. That's the word. It's the most frightening expression a living face can make.

I barked. Salukis hardly bark; we were bred to run down gazelle without a sound. I barked anyway, sharp and ugly, right in his face, and shoved my nose under his jaw.

He didn't answer.

The sand was over his ankles now. His knees. It was spilling into the folds of his shirt, into the hollow of his throat.

The dead go into the sand the same day. That's what desert people do. He'd scraped a grave for Hazza' too shallow to keep the wind out. So he was giving the sand the next best thing.

I got my teeth in his sleeve and pulled with everything I had, which that night was less than a puppy has. My burned side split and smoked and I yelped, and his arm didn't move.

I could have let it out. You're thinking it. I was thinking it. My shadow could have flung that dune aside in three strokes.

He'd seen that shadow once, and his light had nearly burned it out of the world. To wake with that thing standing over him in the dark might be what finished him.

So I stood there in the sliding sand, shaking, a thin burned dog barking at a boy who wouldn't listen, while the desert buried him an inch at a time.

And the light came.

---

It wasn't his light. That's what I knew first.

His light starts at the mark and pours outward. This came from the slope beside him, from nowhere at all. It was soft and gold, and it didn't burn. It glowed the way a lamp glows in the doorway of a tent when someone is waiting up for you.

And it was cold. That's what nobody puts in the songs. The sand around it frosted. I watched the frost spread out from it in fine white feathers, and my breath came out of me in a cloud, and every hair on my body stood up at once, and then, one by one, lay down again.

I didn't see anyone in it. I only saw the light on Theyab's face, and his lips move.

Hazza' was sitting on the slope beside him, cross-legged, the way he sat by the fire when he was about to say something annoying. Light came through the edges of him, like a cloth hung in front of the sun.

He was grey. Theyab told me that, and then he wouldn't tell me any more about it for a long time. Grey, like the hollow that morning. And the golden cracks were still in him, running across his face and down his throat, and they were glowing again. Brighter than they'd been. As if the thing that had been trying to get out of them had nearly made it.

His hand was bare. No ring. Theyab noticed that. He couldn't stop noticing it.

"You look terrible," said Hazza'. "That's weird."

"You're lying in a hole," said Hazza'. "Letting a dune sit on you. Do you know how stupid that looks from where I am? Very. It looks very stupid. Even the dog thinks so. Look at him."

"I left you," Theyab whispered.

Hazza' stopped smiling.

"Yes," he said. "You did."

"I left you," Theyab said again, "and you died."

"I know. I was there." Hazza' leaned forward. He laid his hand on Theyab's shoulder, and it was *warm*. Theyab swore to me it was warm, warm as a living hand, warm as the sun on a cold winter morning, though the frost was spreading out from him across the sand the whole time. "Get up anyway."

"Who did it?" Theyab said. "Hazza'. Who—"

Hazza's face went still. He opened his mouth, and for a moment nothing came out of it but the smell of cold ash, and his eyes went somewhere past Theyab's shoulder, into the dark, and stayed there. As if someone were standing there. As if he'd seen them before.

"I can't," he said, and for the first time he sounded young, and frightened, and seventeen. "I don't think I'm allowed. I think there's a lot I'm not allowed." His grip tightened. "Get up, Theyab."

"I can't."

"Yes, you can. You're just choosing not to. It's different." His eyes were very bright, and very pale, and they didn't blink. Not once, the whole time. "Never give up, Theyab. You promised."

Theyab shut his eyes.

He had. Under the full moon, on the last night, Hazza' had said *Promise me you'll never give up*, and he had said *I promise*. And he could not lie. Not even lying in the sand with a dune on his chest.

He had promised. So it was true. So he had to make it true.

When he opened his eyes, the slope was dark and empty, and there was only a dog standing knee-deep in the sand beside him, ears up, every hair along his back lying flat and smooth as combed silk.

I hadn't seen anything. I'll swear to that.

But I hadn't growled once, the whole time that light was on the slope. And I growl at mirages. I growl at anything pretending to be what it isn't.

And the frost was still there on the slope, in the shape of someone sitting cross-legged. It stayed there until the sun came up and took it.

---

He got up.

He dug himself out with his hands, the way he'd once dug himself out of collapsed rooms in the old ruins, and climbed the slope on hands and knees, dragging the sword.

Then he looked at me. Actually looked, for the first time in four days.

"Did you see him?" he said.

I looked back at him. I didn't wag. I didn't lower my head. I just looked, and he read it, the way the bearers always half read me.

"No," he said slowly. "But you didn't growl."

I lay down at his feet with my chin on his ankle. My back was flat.

He looked at it a long moment. "He was warm," he said. "He was warm, Ghareeb. And everything around him was freezing." He swallowed. "Is that how it's supposed to be?"

I've walked with eleven bearers. I've seen a great many things come back to the people who loved them. Some of them were what they looked like.

I didn't know. I couldn't have told him if I had. I put my head down and closed my good eye, and he let it be an answer.

He looked away over the dunes, but he didn't turn his back on me. That night he slept with me curled against his side, one hand on the sword and one on my ribs, and twice I felt him wake and look at the slope where the frost had been.

---

This next part is where I went wrong.

In the morning he walked on, and I couldn't keep up.

The dune had cost me more than I'd thought. By mid-morning I'd folded up in the thin shade of a calotropis with my legs not listening, and he was four dunes ahead. I told myself I'd catch up when the heat broke.

Which is why I was a long way off, lying on a ridge, when the lamps came.

You don't see lamps lit in daylight. That's what made me lift my head. A string of camels coming over the eastern dunes, with brass lamps hung from their saddles still burning in the full morning sun, the way the lamps burn on the walls of Ubar. Water skins so fat they sloshed.

They came straight to him. Not near him. *To* him, as if they'd known exactly which dune a tired boy would be standing on.

The lead camel knelt. A man climbed down.

He was old, his beard silver and combed long over a bisht the deep blue of the sky just after sunset. His face was soft and kind and lined, someone's grandfather. His voice was low and gentle, the kind that makes you lean in.

And his eyes were grey.

Faded grey, flat and pale, like cloth left in the sun for a hundred years. Like the eyes of the emptied, before the white sets in.

The hair along my spine stood straight up, and the burned place in my side stopped whispering. All at once. Just went silent, the way the crickets go silent when something walks past in the dark.

Behind the old man, another figure climbed down: the biggest man I had ever seen. Shaved head under a plain cloth. Arms like the trunks of date palms. A face that didn't look as though it had ever made an expression it meant.

"Take the boy's bag," the old man said softly. "Can't you see he can barely stand?"

The giant lifted the bag gently from Theyab's shoulder and carried it back to the baggage camel and tied it there, among the water skins.

Remember that. I didn't. I was watching faces. I should have been watching the bag. The bag with Old Bakhit's bell in it.

---

"You are the boy who glows," the old man said.

Theyab didn't answer. His hand had gone to the hilt of the broken sword on his back.

"My name is Muhaisen," said the old man. "I am the wazir of Ubar. I serve Sheikh Mutlaq." A pause. "You'll have heard my name. From him."

He had. Hazza' had told him the whole story by the fire: the stable boy Suhail he'd hidden and tried to cure, the broken sword, and the soft old voice of the wazir calling it treason, calling it jinn-work and sorcery, until his father believed it.

"Yes," said Muhaisen. "I named him traitor." He bowed his silver head. "I was wrong. I've spent a month crossing this desert to tell him so. And I came too late."

He reached into his bisht and held something out on his open palm, and I saw Theyab's whole body go still.

A ring. Heavy gold, worn smooth, the tower-seal of Ubar cut into its face.

Hazza's signet ring. The one he never took off.

"He gave me this," Muhaisen said gently.

On the ridge, my hackles lifted.

I *think* they lifted. I'm nearly sure. But my fur had been standing since I'd seen those eyes, and a growl that's already in you can't rise any higher. I was a long way off, and in pain, and afraid. All of that is true. None of it is an excuse. I've had a very long time to try to make it one.

"When?" said Theyab. His voice cracked on it.

"On the night of the full moon," said Muhaisen. "I found him at last. By his little fire. We spoke. He forgave me, I think, or began to. And he gave me his ring and said, *If anything happens to me, find the boy who glows. Look after him.*" The old man's voice shook, very slightly, in exactly the right place. "I didn't understand what he meant. Not until morning."

There was no bell. Old Bakhit's bell was in a patched bag tied to a camel twenty paces away, among the water skins, and it made no sound. I've wondered since whether a bell can be smothered the way a voice can.

Theyab reached out, slowly, the way you reach for something that might vanish. Muhaisen let him take the ring. It was warm from the old man's robe. Theyab closed his fingers over it, and his face crumpled.

"Did you kill him?" Muhaisen asked quietly. Almost kindly. "They're saying you did. They're saying it in every majlis from here to the coast."

Theyab's eyes were streaming. He could have said *no*, and it would have been true.

But the boy could not lie, and in his heart, the truth was something else.

"I left him," he said.

Muhaisen nodded slowly. "I know," he said. "So did I, once, a long time ago. Someone I loved." His grey eyes were wet. "Everyone leaves someone, child. It's what we do afterwards that matters."

He held out his hand. After a moment, Theyab gave the ring back. Muhaisen tucked it inside his robe, carefully, close to his heart.

"It should go to his father," he said. "And his brother. I'll see that it does. But you need food, and shade, and sleep. There's an oasis town two days east, Al-Nakhlatain. I have a house there. Come with me. You'll be my guest, and a guest is sacred for three days, and longer if I say so." A pause, gentle as a hand on the back of the neck. "And when you're stronger, I'll tell you about your father."

Theyab's head came up.

"I knew him," said Muhaisen. "Long ago. He carried what you carry. I can tell you who he was. If you want to know."

---

By the time I got there, Theyab was already climbing onto a camel.

I came down the slope in a sort of controlled fall, my side smoking. Muhaisen's soft grey eyes found me, and he smiled.

"Ah," he said. "The old saluki. Of course."

There was no surprise in it at all. He looked at the burned place on my side, the dark showing through, and then, slowly, down at my shadow lying a hand's width too long on the sand. He looked at it the way you'd look at a door you already had the key to.

I stood at the camel's feet with every hair on my back on end and my lips drawn off my teeth, and I growled. Not loud. But long, and low, the growl every shepherd knows means *there is a wolf on the far side of this fold.* I looked up at the boy: one hand on the halter rope, the other pressed flat over his mark.

I could have done a great many things. Bitten the old man's ankle. Stood in front of the camel until someone had to kill me to get it past. I've had a long time to make a list.

I growled. That was all.

Theyab looked down at me. He looked tired beyond anything a boy of fifteen should be. "You growl at everyone," he said.

That was true too. Mostly.

"And you didn't growl once," he said, more quietly. "At the carvings. Remember?"

He turned the camel's head east and followed the old man's lamps over the dune.

I stood there in the sand a long moment. Then I followed. What else was I going to do? I'd been following him for fifteen years.

The giant walked at the back of the line. He didn't look round once. But at the top of the dune he stopped, and he waited, very patiently, until I'd passed him, so that he was behind me all the way.

I didn't know then what it would cost, that one small, cowardly, honest growl, and all the things I didn't do after it. I know now. I'll tell you. Not yet.

The burned place in my side didn't whisper again all that day. Not once.

It was listening.

And above us the moon was thinning, night by night, like a face turning slowly away, and I had started counting again.

---

*End of Chapter Thirteen*


---

# Chapter Fourteen
## The Ringing of the Bell

> *The bell does not lie, but it can be too late. A bell rung too late is only a funeral.*
> — a saying of the old healers on the edge of the sands

---

The town was called Al-Nakhlatain, the Two Palms, and it was the kind of place you could love if nobody in it was trying to kill you.

It sat where the gravel plain runs up to the first red dunes, around a spring so old the stones of its well-head had been worn into the shape of hands. Two date palms stood over it, older than anyone's grandfather's grandfather, leaning toward each other like a pair of old men sharing a secret. There were thousands of palms in the gardens below, fed by a falaj that ran out from the spring in a stone channel. But only those two counted. The people said the water would last as long as the second palm stood, so nobody ever climbed them. There was a mud-brick watchtower, a market under palm-frond shades, a dyer's yard hung with indigo cloth, and a hundred flat-roofed houses. No walls. Just water, and people who'd decided to stay near it.

Muhaisen's house stood on the edge of the market. A carved teak door brought up from the coast, a courtyard with a lime tree, and a branch of the falaj running right through it in a plaster channel. In the desert, that is about as close to showing off as you can get.

"Dogs stay in the yard," said the steward at the gate, looking down at me.

Any Bedouin will tell you a saluki isn't a dog, not the way that word is meant. We sleep in the tents. I looked at the steward with my clouded silver eye, and let him look back at it for a while.

He went pale, the way people do the first time, and looked at Muhaisen.

Muhaisen smiled his soft smile. "The saluki may go where he likes," he said. "Outside."

So I slept on the roof.

---

They gave Theyab the top room. A real mattress with a real blanket, a basin of clean water, a window that looked out over the two palms. He stood in the doorway looking at it the way you'd look at a meal you suspected was a trap.

Then he took off the broken sword and laid it on the bed, and his bag beside it. And for the first time since the full moon, he slept through a whole night without the sword in his arms.

I watched him through the window from the roof's edge, my burned side pressed against the warm mud bricks.

I didn't sleep.

The servants in that house didn't speak. They moved through the rooms with lamps and basins and folded cloths, and they never said a word, not to Muhaisen, not to each other. When one passed below me in the courtyard I saw her lips moving under the edge of her face-covering, very slightly, the same small shape over and over, like someone saying a name to keep from losing it.

I told myself it was a prayer. I've told myself a lot of things.

---

You have to understand how good Muhaisen was at it.

Most liars are bad at it. They talk too much. They hurry. They sweat, and my kind can smell it. Muhaisen never hurried, and he never sweated. He wanted you to feel he had all the time in the world, the way you feel the weight of a warm camel-hair blanket.

On the first evening there was coffee, poured three times from a brass dallah with a beak like a bird's, the way you honour a guest. And there were dates on the tray. Dates rolled in honey, on a blue glazed plate.

Theyab stopped in the doorway and stared.

"Hazza' told me you liked them," said Muhaisen gently. "Sit. Eat."

The bell was upstairs, in the bag on the bed. I was on the roof with my ear to a crack in the shutter, and every hair along my back came up, and the growl came up into my throat and I had to swallow it like a stone. Nobody saw.

Theyab sat. He ate one date, slowly, eyes shut, and I watched him go searching for the sweetness the ash-blade had taken in the storm, and find it, and his face broke open with relief. He didn't touch the twist of waxed cloth in his pocket. He ate Muhaisen's dates instead, and his eyes filled, and Muhaisen pretended not to notice, which was the kindest thing anyone had done for him in days.

That's how it's done. Not with chains. With honey, and coffee poured three times, and a real bed, and an old man who knows exactly when to look away.

---

Muhaisen wrote letters in the evenings.

He sat at a low table by the lamp with a reed pen and a little stone seal he wore on a cord around his neck. When each letter was done he folded it, dripped grey wax onto the fold, and pressed the seal in with his thumb. Every night a saker falcon flew out of the window with a letter tied to its leg, and just before it went, it turned its head and looked at Theyab with one black eye.

On the second evening Theyab sat across from him and watched the pen move.

"*Water,*" he said quietly. "That word. *Ma'.* Upside down from here. It says *water*."

The pen stopped.

Muhaisen looked up. For a moment, only a moment, something moved behind those faded grey eyes, quick and cold as a fish turning in the dark of a falaj.

"You can read," he said.

"A little. Hazza' taught me."

"Did he." Muhaisen set down the pen. "He was a patient teacher?"

Theyab almost smiled, the first I'd seen since the full moon. "No. He was terrible. He laughed every time I wrote a *kaf*. He said they looked like camels kneeling down the wrong way."

"Then you must be a very good student," said Muhaisen, and went back to his letter, and folded it, and sealed it.

I remember the seal. The grey wax, and the little curling mark it left. Some part of me must have known. The part of me that always knows things a moment too late.

---

On the third evening he talked about his son.

"I had a son once," said Muhaisen.

He said it to the lamp, not to Theyab.

"And a brother. A long time ago, before I came to the sheikh's court. There was a fever that year in the town where I was born, down on the coast. It came through like the shamal. My brother first. Then my boy." He turned his little coffee cup slowly in his long fingers. "He was six. He used to sit on my knee while I wrote, and try to catch the pen."

On the roof, my back lay flat as poured water. Every word was true.

That's the thing about truly dangerous liars. They hardly ever lie. They tell you the truth, and the truth is sad and real, and it opens you like a key in a lock. Then, once the door is open, they walk in and close it softly behind them.

"I'm sorry," said Theyab.

"So am I." Muhaisen smiled, tired and gentle. "I spent a long time trying to forget them. It doesn't work, you know. Not the way you'd hope." He looked at the lamp a moment longer. "Not unless you're willing to forget everything else as well."

Theyab was quiet. Then he said, "Tell me about my father."

"He carried what you carry. The Light. Before you. The carvings have a name for him. You've seen them, I think. At Ghidd al-Zujaj."

Theyab's hand went to his chest.

"*The One Who Ran*," he whispered.

"The One Who Ran. He was afraid of what the Light would ask of him. So he ran." A pause. "He's alive, Theyab."

"Where?" His voice cracked. "Where is he?"

"When you're stronger," said Muhaisen, and reached across the tray and laid a hand over Theyab's, very lightly. It was a dry hand, cool, and it stayed a moment too long. "I promise. When you're stronger, I'll take you to him."

My hackles twitched, then didn't. I could never tell, with him. That frightened me most of all.

He trusted him.

For the first time since Hazza', Theyab trusted someone.

---

That night he climbed up to the roof.

He sat down beside me with his knees pulled up, looking out over the two palms and the dark dunes beyond. The moon was a half now, and shrinking.

"He's alive," Theyab said at last. "My father."

I put my chin on his knee.

"He said he'd take me to him."

He looked down at me. "You don't believe him."

My back stayed exactly as it was. No hackles. No growl. Nothing, because I didn't *know*, and a dog can't say *I don't know* any more than he can say anything else.

"Ghareeb."

I got up. I went to the top of the stairs and looked back at him, and then down toward his room, and back at him, the way a hunting dog does when the hare's in a hole and the man's too slow to see it.

He frowned. "What?"

"I'm not playing games, Ghareeb." Sharper. "If you know something, *show* me. If he's lying, show me what about."

I could have stood under the old man's window and growled until the whole town came out with lamps. And then he'd have asked *why*, with his eyes, the way the bearers ask, and I'd have had to show him where I'd been on the night of the full moon, and what had been standing behind me. I wasn't ready. Not with him. Not even with you, yet. And I had no proof but a dog's growl, and my growl had gone silent once, at Ghidd al-Zujaj, when it should have spoken.

I lay back down.

Theyab looked at me a long time. Then he made a small, tired sound that wasn't quite a laugh.

"Where were you?" he said. "That night."

I put my head on my paws.

"That's what I thought," he said, and went back down the stairs.

---

I went down later, when the house was asleep.

Along the top of the courtyard wall, and in at Theyab's window. He was asleep on his face with one arm off the mattress.

His bag was on the floor by the bed. I put my nose in it. Dates and the old smell of Hilat al-Sarab, smoke and goats and poverty. And at the bottom, wrapped in a rag, a small brass bell no bigger than a thumb, on its cord of braided camel hair.

I took it in my mouth, carefully, so it didn't sound. Then I laid it on the mattress beside his face, an inch from his nose, where it would be the first thing he saw.

---

In the morning, he wore the bell.

He tied it to his belt by its camel-hair cord, under the fold of his shirt. A small brass bell no bigger than a thumb.

I went down into the market to find something to eat.

---

That's where I saw Nadim.

I almost didn't know him. The boy I'd last seen in Hilat al-Sarab had been broad and loud and well-fed, the well-keeper's son. This one was thin as a stick. Rags. Feet bound in strips of cloth gone black. He stood at the old well under the two palms with his hands cupped, begging for water, and nobody gave him any. They looked at his face and stepped round him.

There was a grey streak down one side of it. Not the old one, from the emptied thing in the lane. A new one, running from his temple into his hair, and the hair there had gone grey too, all the way to the roots, as if someone had laid a cold thumb on him and pressed. When he blinked, the eye on that side was a little slow to follow.

I went very still in the shade of a palm-frond awning.

He didn't see me. He was watching the house with the teak door the way a starving dog watches a butcher's stall. When the market got busy around noon he slipped off down a narrow lane between the dyer's yard and the garden wall.

I followed him. Every footprint he left was full of his fear.

The lane ended in a little dead square full of broken water jars, where the dyer's indigo cloths hung over the wall like curtains in a room where someone has died.

Someone was waiting there.

Grey robe. Smooth white mask.

I flattened myself behind a cracked jar and stopped breathing.

---

"Well?" said the mask. Its voice was flat and dry, like sand poured from a cup. It didn't seem to come from behind the mask. It seemed to come from all around the little square at once.

"He's here," said Nadim. His voice shook. "You were right. The house with the carved door. The top room, over the palms. He sleeps by the window. He's got a broken sword. He keeps it on the bed."

My back stayed flat. True. All of it.

"You're certain it's him."

"I'd know him anywhere." Nadim swallowed. "I've known him all my life."

"Good. Tonight, then."

"And Shibl?" Nadim took a step forward. "You *promised*. When you found us in the desert, you said you'd keep him safe from the grey. You said if I found the glowing boy, you'd give my brother back."

"When we have the bearer," said the mask, "the child goes free."

Every hair on my body stood up at once.

A growl came up out of me before I could stop it, just a breath of it against the clay of the jar. I wanted to scream at him, *Look at me!* But he wasn't looking at me. He was looking at that blank white oval as if it were the only door left in the world.

"Everyone's gone grey," he said. His voice cracked in half. "Do you understand? All of them. My father sits by the well all day with the bucket rope in his hands, and he says—" He stopped. He made himself go on. "He says, *Draw it up slow, boys, there's plenty.* That's all. Over and over. That's what he was saying to us the morning it came. He doesn't know my name. He says it to me like I'm a stranger. Sheikh Mazyad walks round and round the square in the dark. And Ghanim—" He laughed, a terrible little sound. "Ghanim gave you his camels. Did you know that? Every thaler he'd ever hoarded, every rupee, the whole chest from under his floor. For a safe road out. He gave it all to you, and you took it, and then you took *him*." He pressed his hands over his face. "I took Shibl and I ran, and we walked for days, and then you came, and—" He stopped again. "He's seven. He's only seven."

"Tonight," said the mask.

It didn't turn to go. It just stood there with its blank face pointed at Nadim, perfectly still, until Nadim backed out of the square himself. And when I looked back, after Nadim had gone, the mask was still standing there, facing the empty place where he had been.

Then, very slowly, it turned its face toward the water jar I was hiding behind.

It didn't come over. It didn't need to. It only tilted its head, the way the emptied do, as if it had heard a sound it almost remembered. A growl, maybe. A dog it used to know.

---

Why would the Nameless need a starving boy to tell them where Theyab slept, when Muhaisen had chosen the room himself? I understood later. A man like Muhaisen never lets his own hands be seen. If the bearer lived long enough to wonder how the masks had found him, it would be Nadim's face he remembered. The boy who'd thrown the first stone. Not the kind old man with the dates.

It was a clever plan. It would have worked.

Except for a small brass bell, no bigger than a thumb, that a dog had carried in his mouth and laid by a sleeping boy's face.

---

I ran.

The teak door was shut.

I found a place where the garden wall was low, and jumped. I hit the top on my chest and scrabbled over, and came down hard on the burn. By the time I'd got up and found my way round to the courtyard, the sun was going down red behind the palms, and the whole yard was the colour of the inside of an eyelid.

The giant was standing by the falaj channel.

He turned his head and looked at me. That was all. He stood between me and the door, and when I tried to go round him, he was there. When I tried the other way, he was there too. He never seemed to move. He was simply always where I was going.

I was still standing there, shaking, my shadow trembling long across the plaster, deciding, when I heard the sound upstairs.

---

They were sitting at supper. That was the strange thing, Theyab told me later. Such an ordinary evening.

Then footsteps on the stairs. Many of them, soft and quick and perfectly in step.

And under them, faint, a hum. No tune. No words.

The door opened.

Grey robes. White masks. Five, then eight, filling the doorway and spilling into the room, and the lamps all leaned away from them, every flame bending the same way, as if a cold wind had come in with them. In their hands were blades, long and thin, the colour of old ash, and they drank the lamplight instead of reflecting it.

The smell came up through the floor to me in the courtyard. Burning cloth. That sweet scorched something. And under it, out of place, a thread of rose attar, the cheap heavy kind traders splash on to cover the smell of their camels.

I knew that attar. I'd smelled it every week for fifteen years, over a trading counter in Hilat al-Sarab, while a big man with a thumb on his brass scale told a scavenger boy his copper was worth half what it was.

Theyab was on his feet. The tray went over. His hand went to his belt for the knife, and the knife wasn't there. It was upstairs in his bag, with the broken sword, on the bed by the window, exactly where Nadim had said it would be.

And Muhaisen rose.

He rose smoothly from his cushion, the old man in blue, and stepped between Theyab and the masks. He put one arm out behind him, shielding the boy, the way a father shields a child.

"Stay behind me, child," he said. His voice was low and calm and full of love. "You are my guest. You're safe under my roof. No one will harm you while I live."

And the bell rang.

---

It wasn't loud. That's what I always remember. I heard it from the courtyard, through the shutters: one clear note, high and sweet as a drop of water falling into a well. A tiny sound.

It filled the whole room.

The masks stopped humming. All of them, at once.

Theyab went completely still.

Muhaisen turned his head. The soft face didn't change at all. But the eyes did. They moved down to the bell and rested there, and something in them went flat and cold and very, very tired, like a man who has been wearing a heavy cloak for a long time and has finally been allowed to put it down.

"Ah," he said quietly. "Bakhit."

Theyab's lips were white. "The ring," he whispered. "Did he give you the ring?"

"Of course he did," said Muhaisen.

The bell rang again. Louder. And it didn't stop shivering after, the way a bell does. It kept trembling on its cord, humming to itself, as if it had more to say and couldn't find the note.

Then it all came at once, each piece a small stone striking his back. The bag on the camel. The dates on the plate. *Hazza' told me you liked them.* The ring that had been warm from Muhaisen's robe. The guest-right, the coffee poured three times.

*He gave me this.*

"No," said Theyab. It came out of him like something tearing. "*No.*"

Muhaisen sighed. He lowered his arm.

"I should have searched you myself," he said, and stepped aside.

---

The masks came in.

Theyab fought. I want you to know that. No knife, no sword, no friend, and he fought anyway: fists, the brass tray, a lamp hurled in a burning arc that sent two masks reeling back with their robes alight. They didn't scream as they burned. They just patted at the flames, calmly, and came on.

One of them had a little brass trader's scale hanging from his belt on a cord, the pans swinging empty. He was big under the robe. He walked a little heavy on the left foot, the way a man does who's spent thirty years standing behind a counter. And as he came at Theyab, from behind the blank white face, in a voice I knew, he said:

"*Short weight, boy. Short weight. That's all it's worth.*"

Over and over. The last thing he'd said before they burned his name. Probably to someone else, at the end, bargaining for his road out with the last thing he had. Probably he'd thought he was winning.

Theyab didn't know him. He'd never once looked that man in the face; you don't, when you're the boy being cheated. But I knew. And I'll tell you what I felt, because I promised to tell you the truth: it wasn't glad. I'd have liked it to be. It was only cold.

And the light came.

But it came *grey*.

Thin and dim and sputtering, the colour of fear and fury. It crawled out of his fists like smoke and did nothing at all. A mask stepped in close and laid an ash-quenched blade flat against his forearm, almost tenderly.

Theyab screamed.

It didn't cut. There was no blood. But where the blade touched him, the grey light went *into* it, drawn out like water into dry sand, and something left him. A memory. He'd tell me later he never knew which. Only that it had been warm, and now there was a cold place with smooth edges where it used to be, and that he sometimes caught himself reaching for it, in the dark, the way you reach for a step that isn't there.

That was when I came through the shutters.

I don't remember how I got past the giant. Then I was in the room in a spray of broken wood, going for the nearest mask the way a saluki takes a gazelle, by the hind leg.

I got one ankle. That's all. It was cold. Cold as a cistern in winter, and soft, like something kept too long out of the light. Then a hand like the side of a house closed round my middle, right over the burn, and the giant lifted me, and I made a sound I'm not proud of, and he threw me.

I hit the wall. The world went white, then grey, then very far away.

---

The last thing Theyab saw, before the sack came down over his head, was the doorway.

Someone was standing in it. Behind the masks, in the shadow at the top of the stairs. A thin boy in rags, feet wrapped in cloth, a grey streak down his face.

Nadim.

They looked at each other across the wrecked room. The boy who'd thrown the first stone, and the boy it had hit.

"They've got Shibl," Nadim said. His voice was barely there. "I'm sorry. Theyab, I'm sorry."

He had never once, in fifteen years, called him by his name. I don't think he'd even known it, until the masks told him.

The bell on Theyab's belt hung silent. Every word was true.

Then the sack came down, and it smelled of burned cloth and something sweeter underneath, and the ash-blades came with it, and the room went dark.

---

I woke alone on the floor of a room that smelled of lamp oil and spilled coffee and lentils.

On the floor near the hearth lay a snapped length of camel-hair cord, and beside it, on its side, a small brass bell.
