I picked it up in my teeth, gently, the way I'd carried it the night before, cord and all, and hid it in the soft of my mouth where no mask would think to look. It was still faintly warm. And very faintly, so faintly I might have imagined it, it was still trembling.

I didn't know where they'd taken him. I didn't know whether there'd be anything left of him when I found him. I only knew that the bell had rung, and rung too late, and that I had been standing in a courtyard when it did, with the truth in me, and no way to say it, and no courage to show it.

Nadim told me, many years later, sitting by a fire with his hand on my head the way the bearers used to, that he never forgave himself for that night.

Neither did I.

---

*End of Chapter Fourteen*


---

# Chapter Fifteen
## The Buried Ship

> *A ship in the desert is a coffin waiting for the sea. What does it hold while it waits?*
> — a riddle the caravan children tell, which has no answer, and which they are told not to tell after dark

---

I'm going to tell you this part the way he told it to me.

He told it once, in the caves on Jebel Hafeet, by a fire that kept going out, in a flat, quiet voice I never heard him use again. He looked at his hands the whole time. I spent most of this part under a dead ship with sand in my mouth.

---

It took me a night and a day to find it.

I followed the camels. And under the camel-smell, the cold, empty smell of ash-blades. And another, the closer I got: burning cloth, and hair, and something sweet and scorched beneath them, like honey left too long on the coals.

I carried Old Bakhit's bell in my teeth the whole way. A saluki has a soft mouth. I didn't let it ring once.

At dusk on the second day, under a moon thinned almost to nothing, a white rind, like the edge of an eye rolled back, I came over a long ridge and saw it.

A ship.

Below me lay a sabkha, a salt flat, dead and white and cracked into plates, stretching off toward a sea that hadn't been there in a thousand years. You find shells in the sand out there, a day's ride from any shore. And at the edge of that dead white flat, between two great dunes, half swallowed, lay a dhow.

A vast old hull, longer than all of Hilat al-Sarab, sunk on its side like a drowned animal, its tall stern rearing out of the sand, its ribs pale through the broken planks. The whole thing leaned toward the sabkha, as if it were still trying to crawl back to the sea.

And inside it, through every crack and gap, lamps burned.

I crept down the slope in the dark, round to the side away from the sabkha, where the sand had drifted up against the buried belly of the ship. I put the bell down in a hollow and covered it.

And I dug.

It's what my kind does. I dug down along the curve of the hull under the sand, where the wood was soft and rotten and the salt had eaten it like worms eat a date, and I didn't stop until I could put my ear to the planks and hear, very close, a boy breathing.

---

He woke up chained.

A great beam ran down the middle of the hold, and from an iron ring in it ran a grey chain to a cuff on each of his wrists. When the light in his chest tried to rise, the chain drank it, with a faint sound like a child sucking at a cut finger.

The planks were covered in writing.

Names. Hundreds of them, scratched into the old wood with nails and buckles and fingernails, as high as a man could reach. Every one had been scraped over afterwards until only a pale smear was left, like a word rubbed out of the sand. He couldn't read a single one.

In the far corner, tossed in a heap like rubbish, were his bag and Hazza's broken sword.

---

They brought him up on the first night, for the burning.

The upper deck lay tilted under the stars, one edge running down into the sand like a ramp. The Nameless stood in a circle on it. Fifty of them, perhaps more, grey robes, smooth white masks, silent as the emptied. In the middle stood an iron brazier, and in it a fire burned.

To one side, in a line, stood the children.

There were eleven. The youngest perhaps five, the oldest perhaps twelve. Little grey shifts. Shaved heads. They stood very straight and still, the way children stand when they've learned that moving gets you hurt.

They weren't crying. Not one. Not a sniff. Frightened children cry. These had been frightened so long that crying had been worn out of them, the way the stones of an old well-head get worn into the shape of hands.

Behind them, on a row of pegs along the rail, hung eleven small white masks. Waiting.

The Nameless brought Theyab into the circle and forced him to his knees.

Then three young men stepped forward. They wore grey, but no masks. Each held a strip of white cotton, and on each, in charcoal, a word was written.

A name.

The circle began to chant: the wordless hum from the storm, fifty throats rising and falling like one breath. Then it flattened into words, fifty voices in one:

"*Names are chains. Memory is pain. Forget, and be free.*"

The first young man held his strip of cloth over the brazier. He said the name on it, once, out loud. His own name. He said it the way you'd say goodbye to someone at the edge of the camp, before a long journey.

He let it fall.

The cloth caught and curled, and as it burned he tried to say his name again. Then his lips stopped halfway through the shape of it and stayed open, puzzled, like a man who has walked into a tent and forgotten why. The smell came off the brazier: scorched cotton, and that sweet burnt something underneath.

A brother stepped up behind each of them and pressed a white mask to his face, and Theyab saw its edges *settle*, softening against the skin like wax near a flame. One of the young men raised a hand and felt for the edge, and couldn't find one. He lowered his hand.

Then the children swept the ash into a sack. The smallest hummed the chant while she swept, perfectly.

Three men had walked into the circle. Three nobodies walked out.

Theyab told me it was the worst thing he ever saw. Worse than the choosing, he said, was how *relieved* they looked.

---

Then a figure stepped into the firelight, and the circle bowed.

Grey robe, white mask, like all the others. But when it spoke, Theyab knew the voice before the first word was done.

"Hello again, child."

He crouched in front of Theyab and held out a strip of white cotton and a stick of charcoal.

"Write it," he said.

Theyab looked at the cloth.

"You can now, can't you?" said the Voice softly. "He taught you. Write it, and give it to the fire, and all of this stops. The grief. The guilt. That walk under the full moon, every time you close your eyes. You saw their faces. Tell me they weren't at peace."

The charcoal hung in front of Theyab's face.

He thought of Hazza' drawing letters in the sand with a stick, right to left. *Dhal first. Like an old man's bent back, with a dot riding on it. Then ya, a little bowl with two dots underneath, like footprints. Alif, the tent pole. And ba, a boat with one dot under it, sinking. That's you. A sinking boat.* Laughing. Always laughing.

Theyab spat on the cloth.

The Voice looked down at the wet spot a moment. Then he folded the cloth carefully and tucked it into his robe, close to his heart, where he kept the ring.

"I must go back to Ubar," he said, rising. "The Night of Lamps is close." He turned to the circle. "Drain him slowly. Make him remember. When he is empty enough to forget his own name, he will give it to us himself. They always do."

---

They put him back on his chain in the hold, and the draining began.

They came in pairs, every few hours: one with a lamp, one with an ash-blade.

An ash-blade doesn't cut. It lies flat against you, cold as the bottom of a well, and pulls, and the Light comes out of you in thin gold threads and runs into the steel and goes grey.

And with each thread, a memory came.

Never a good one. The good ones they took. The bad ones they *played*. Theyab was six again, running with a stone in his back. Or kneeling with Shibl's hand in his while Nadim screamed *Monster!*

Or he was on the dune under the full moon.

Over and over: turning away, walking, watching the orange point of the fire go out. And each time, the memory changed a little. By the second day he couldn't tell which version was true. In the version he saw by then, he'd heard Hazza' scream.

He hadn't. I know that much. One day I'll tell you how.

I couldn't tell him. I lay under the hull an arm's length from him, hearing every sound he made and making none, digging a little further each night, waiting for the one moment that would work. That's what I told myself. True things can still be cowardly.

---

On the second night, they let the emptied in.

Dozens of them were penned in the lower hold, so close to my tunnel I could smell them through the wood. The Nameless *kept* them, the way a sheikh keeps hawks. And the emptied are drawn to the Light the way moths are drawn to a lamp, with the same ending.

They were all muttering their own last words, a soft dry babble like water in a falaj. *Mind the step. Mind the step.* *Is that you?* *Bring the goats in, the shamal's coming.* *Tell her I said—tell her I said—tell her—*

Then, all at once, they stopped.

Then they spoke.

Every mouth, together. One voice out of thirty throats, not a syllable apart. Not their voices. Not any voice they'd ever had.

"*Little light.*"

Theyab went rigid on his chain.

Ghadban. The shepherd boy who said no. And under him, behind him, wearing him the way the masks wore the faces underneath them, the thing that had been sealed beneath Ubar since before Ubar had a name.

"*Little nameless light,*" said the emptied. Thirty jaws moving as one. "*They threw stones at you. They called you monster. They drove you into the sand to die. And you'd burn for them? Why? No one remembers the bearers. Ask the dog. Ask him how many he's buried. Ask him if anyone sings their names at the coffee hearth.*"

"*Ask him why it's always children.*"

Theyab shut his eyes.

"*Ask him who it is that's fed,*" the voice went on, gentle, almost sorry, "*every time one of you burns. Ask him what's on the other side of the Light. He's seen it. He's been inside it. He knows.*"

Here is something I've never told anyone. Under the hull, I didn't growl. I lay there with my lips off my teeth, waiting for the growl, and it didn't come. Not for *ask him why it's always children*. Not for *ask him who is fed*.

"*Let it go,*" said thirty voices. "*Let me hold it for you. It's so heavy, little light. I know. I carried it too.*"

Then the voice went softer. Almost shy. All thirty faces turned down, toward the floor of the hold, toward the sand where I lay.

"*Hello, Ghareeb,*" it said. "*I know you're down there. You always did listen at the tent wall.*"

The burned place in my side said its three words louder. In his voice. It had always been his voice.

I won't tell you what that voice sounded like long ages ago, when it was a shepherd boy's in the Hajar, whistling me in from the wadi at dusk. That's mine. I'll only tell you that I didn't go to it, and that it was the hardest thing I have ever not done.

---

The children came at dawn, with water.

One of them stopped in front of Theyab and stared.

He was seven. His head was shaved, his shift too big. And his eyes were brown, clear brown, not faded at all. Not yet.

"You're the monster," said Shibl.

"Nadim said you weren't," Shibl whispered. "After. He said he was wrong." Something moved in his face, deep down, like a fish rising in murky water. His lip shook. Just once. "Is Nadim coming?"

Theyab opened his mouth. And closed it.

He wanted to say *yes. I promise.* But Theyab couldn't lie, and he didn't know.

"I'll try," he said. It was all he had. "I'll try. I swear I will."

Shibl nodded, as if *I'll try* from someone who couldn't lie was worth more than *I promise* from anyone else.

Then the fish sank, and his face smoothed over. He hummed two notes of the chant under his breath, without seeming to know he'd done it, and followed the others up the ladder.

---

That night, alone on the chain, Theyab reached for his name and couldn't find it.

The blades had been at him all day. He was so cold he couldn't feel his hands. And he thought, the way you think of something just to make sure it's still there, *My name is*—

And there was nothing.

A blank, smooth place, like the face of a mask. He could feel the edges of where his name should be, the way a tongue feels a lost tooth. He reached for the village, Umm Hanan's bread warm in his hands, and they were greying at the edges like cloth left in the sun.

Worse: it didn't hurt. It felt like setting something down. He understood, then, the young men's faces at the brazier.

*Write it,* the Voice had said. *They always do.*

His finger moved in the sand and salt-dust on the floor of the hold. Searching. He didn't know what for.

"That's not even a letter," said someone.

The hold was full of gold.

---

Hazza' was sitting on the beam above him, swinging his legs.

Under the planks, every hair on my back lay down at once.

"I spend a whole month teaching you," Hazza' said, "and you give up on the first letter. I'm insulted."

"*Dhal*," said Hazza'. He leaned down and pointed at the dust. His finger left no mark. "The old man's bent back, with his dot. Go on. Right to left. You always forget which way."

Theyab's finger moved. A bend. A dot.

"*Ya.* The little bowl, two dots underneath." Hazza' was smiling now. "*Alif.* The tent pole. And *ba*. The boat with one dot, sinking. Go on. Say it."

"*Theyab,*" whispered Theyab.

It came back all at once, like water into a dry well. A small boy alone at the edge of a village, deciding he'd be called a word that meant *wolves*, because the wolves were the only ones out there who'd never once thrown a stone.

"There you are," said Hazza' softly.

His hand came down on Theyab's shoulder. Warm. Warm as the sun, though the frost was creeping out across the boards from where he sat, over the scraped-out names.

"You promised," he said. "Never give up." Then, quieter, with a grin that was almost the old grin: "Besides. There are eleven children up there who are forgetting their names too. Somebody's going to have to teach them. I'd do it, but I'm a bit busy being dead."

Theyab grabbed at his sleeve. His fingers went through it into cold. "What's it *like*? Hazza'. Where you are. Is it—"

Hazza's smile stayed exactly where it was. But his eyes went past Theyab again, to the dark at the end of the hold, the way they had on the dune. As if someone were standing there. As if someone were always standing there, wherever he went now.

"Crowded," he said.

Then the gold went out, and Theyab was alone in the dark, with four letters drawn in the dust beside his hand.

He didn't wipe them away.

In the morning, when the lamp came down the ladder, there were eight letters in the dust, not four. Under his own name, in his own hand, right to left, pressed in deep with one finger, the dot on the *zay* exactly where it belonged:

Hazza'.

He didn't remember writing it. He never did.

---

The guard who came at midnight on the third night was alone, and he was humming.

Theyab knew the tune before he knew the man: the old song of the 'Anqa, the firebird that burns and rises from its own ash, the song he'd heard in his memory at Bir al-Ghina'.

The masked figure came down the ladder with a lamp and two ash-quenched blades crossed on his back, sat a little way off, and went on humming as if he didn't know he was doing it.

The Seventh. Always hunting him. Always, somehow, missing.

Theyab started to hum too.

It just came out of him: the next line, the one the masked man hadn't reached yet.

The humming stopped.

"Where did you learn it?" Theyab whispered. "Who taught it to you?"

No answer. I never once, in all that time, heard him speak.

He stood and drew one of the grey blades. *Drain him slowly.* He knelt by the chain and laid the blade flat against Theyab's arm.

And Theyab's hand closed around his wrist.

He wasn't thinking about himself. That's the only reason it worked. He was thinking of eleven children who had stopped crying, and a boy asking whether his brother was coming.

The light came up out of his chest like the sun coming over the dunes.

*Gold.*

It ran down his arm and into the ash-blade and *through* it, faster than the blade could drink. Up the masked man's wrist. Up his arm. Into him.

And they both saw it.

---

A goat-hair tent, at night. A shamal howling outside, the walls bowing in and out like something breathing.

A small boy, five years old, sitting on a woven rug, very serious. In his arms, held carefully, the way he'd been shown, a newborn wrapped in grey cloth woven all over with strange symbols. The baby had one tiny fist round the boy's finger and wouldn't let go.

Somewhere above them, a man's voice was singing. Low and warm. The firebird, burning and rising.

Then the tent wall tore open, and there were white masks in the storm.

---

The vision broke.

So did the mask.

I heard it from under the hull: a clean crack, like ice splitting on a winter water-skin. A line ran down the white face from brow to chin, with gold shining through. Along the crack the mask had grown *into* him, fine pale threads that bled, very slightly, grey.

And Theyab saw what he always saw: the full moon, Tal Moreeb, the fire. Clearer. He could feel the heat on his shins now, his skin tightening. And there were people in it. A man on his knees in the sand, begging. A figure in a cracked white mask, standing very still.

The Seventh tore free and scrambled backwards, one hand clamped over the crack, making a small broken wordless sound, like a child woken from a nightmare who can't find the tent flap.

Then his blade flashed once. I can't tell you whether he meant to do it. I don't think he knew himself. But the grey chain parted between Theyab's wrists like a cut thread.

And the Seventh fled. Up the ladder, through the hatch, out into the night. Humming again, I swear it, as he went. Broken, out of tune. But humming.

---

He was across the hold before the hatch stopped swinging, the broken sword in his hand and his bag over his shoulder.

He went up the ladder to the children.

They were in a narrow room in the bow, where the hull curved in toward the stem-post, awake. Eleven small faces turned toward the boy in the doorway.

None of them moved. Two were wearing their masks already.

"Up," he said. "All of you. Now. Hold hands. Don't let go."

He went to the nearest masked child and worked his fingers under the rim, gently, the way you'd work out a thorn from a camel's foot, saying anything, *you've got a name, you've got a name*, and it came away with a soft sound like a lip unsticking. Under it was a girl's face: blank, then puzzled, then all at once crumpling.

She cried.

Shibl moved first after that. He got up and took the smallest girl's hand. Then the next child. Then the next. The second mask came off easier.

That's when the shouting started.

Running feet. Grey lamps swinging. Masks poured toward the bow, humming louder and louder. Theyab backed the children into the corner with half a sword and no way out, and the light in his chest flickered grey.

And the floor under them sagged.

---

That was me.

Three nights under the hull with Ghadban's voice in my ears. Three nights of digging along the keel and up under the bow, where the salt had eaten the wood soft as old bread, until I could hear the smallest child's heart.

When I felt the light go gold above me, I knew.

I let it out.

It came up through the burn like smoke through a crack, and all the voices came with it, his the loudest, glad, *there you are, there you are*. My shadow went up from under me, and there was no room in the tunnel for it, so it went up *through* the sand and the planks, long and wrong, a dog's shape the size of a ship's mast, ember eyes, my mouth full of teeth I didn't remember growing.

And I set my shoulders against the underside of that rotten hull, a thin old saluki's shoulders, and I *pulled it down*.

Not lifted. You can't lift a buried ship. I've heard it told that way since, around coffee hearths from Liwa to Ibri, and it's a good story, and it's not what happened. What happened was worse. I dug my claws into the keel-planks above me and pulled, with a strength that came up out of the dark through my legs, and the whole buried section of the bow came away. Wood that hadn't moved since the sea left tore loose like wet cloth. Sand poured in after it in a roaring rush. The ribs cracked one after another, and the deck behind the children's room folded down onto itself with a sound like the whole desert sighing, and the masks running along it went down with it, humming.

And the floor of the children's room fell away into my tunnel, and my tunnel ran out under the hull to the open dune.

I held the rest. The corner of the bow that hadn't fallen yet, the sagging stem-post, the planks over the children's heads, all of it leaning down onto me, every scraped-out name in that ship, on a dog's back.

Through the hole in the broken floor, Theyab looked down at me.

He'd seen this shadow once before, and burned it.

He wasn't afraid now. I saw his face. I'll never forget his face.

"*Ghareeb,*" he said.

That was all. My name. Not *monster*. My name.

And all the voices inside me went quiet. Every one. Even his.

"*Hold it,*" said Theyab.

I barked. Once. It came out of me like a rockslide, and it was not polite.

He laughed. In the middle of all that, he laughed. Then he was down through the hole with eleven children holding hands behind him, sliding down the tunnel past me in the dark, Shibl last, the smallest girl on Theyab's back, crying, still crying, all the way down and out onto the cold open sand under the stars.

When the last bare foot touched the dune outside, I let go.

The bow came down into the hole with a roar of splitting timber, and the ship settled deeper into its dune, groaning, and the sand closed over the broken end of it like water closing over a stone.

---

I don't remember my shadow coming back. I remember lying in the sand at the mouth of the tunnel, only a dog, with my side on fire and my legs not working.

I remember hands. His hands, picking me up. Holding me against his chest, right over the mark, where it was warm.

"I've got you," he said.

I wriggled until he let me down, and dragged myself three steps to a hollow, and dug, and came back with it in my soft mouth, and put it in his palm.

A small brass bell, no bigger than a thumb, on its snapped camel-hair cord.

He looked at it. He looked at me. And behind us, over the ridge, came the sound I'd been dreading: the howl of jinn-jackals, the roar and drum of racing camels, and under it all, the chant. No words. A hundred voices, none of them breathing.

Theyab knotted the cord and tied the bell to his belt. He hoisted me over his shoulders, long legs dangling, the way a herdsman carries a lamb. He took Shibl's hand.

Behind him, eleven children were holding on to one another in the dark at the edge of a dead white sabkha. Some were crying. Some weren't. Two still hummed, under their breath, and didn't know they were doing it.

He'd come back for them. Nobody had asked him to. I remembered that, later, when it mattered.

And he ran.

---

*End of Chapter Fifteen*


---

# Chapter Sixteen
## The Rider Unveiled

> *Every rider in the dark is someone's father. Pray to the One who made the sands that he is yours.*
> — from a lullaby of the herders under Jebel Hafeet, sung only to children who will not sleep

---

Have you ever tried to cross the sands at night with eleven children who have forgotten how to cry?

Don't.

Crying children are loud, but loud is a mercy. Loud means they still want something. These ones ran in silence, a grey huddle of shaved heads, and when one of them fell he didn't call out. He lay with his face pressed into the sand until somebody bigger picked him up, and then he ran again, and his eyes never changed. Every so often, all together, they hummed. Two notes. No words. The chant from the dhow's hold. They didn't know they were doing it.

And Theyab ran in front of them with a broken sword in one hand and Shibl's hand in the other and a half-dead saluki slung across his shoulders the way a shepherd carries a lamb, my legs hanging down his chest, and he didn't stop once.

I could feel his heart through my ribs. It was going like something trying to get out.

Behind us, the jackals were getting closer.

---

You'll remember the jinn-jackals. Lean shapes the colour of nothing, with eyes like coals blown on, and no smell. That is the worst of it, for a dog. A real jackal stinks of carrion and old piss, and you know it's coming from three dunes off. A jinn-jackal smells of nothing at all, the way a cold room smells of nothing, and the first you know of it is the cold.

They track the bearer's Light the way a jackal tracks a wounded gazelle. Theyab's Light was leaking out of him through the golden cracks on his arms, and on that night, with the moon pared down to a fingernail, he must have shone like an open wound.

They were calling to each other. Six voices. Then nine. One of them wasn't howling. One of them, off to the left, was saying *Theyab* in a small wet voice, over and over, the way a child says a word it has only just learned. The wind had carried his name a long way since that night on the dune above Hilat al-Sarab. Things had been practising it.

We came over a ridge, and the far face fell away too steep to run. The children stopped in a huddle at the top. Then, all at once, they turned and looked back the way we had come. Calmly. Like people waiting for a guest they had been told would arrive.

That frightened me more than the jackals did.

Below us, in the trough we'd just crossed, eyes were opening in the dark. Pairs of them, red as coals, spreading out in a ring.

"Behind me," Theyab said to the children. His voice was very calm. "Stay together. Don't look at their eyes."

He set Shibl's hand into the hand of the oldest boy, who looked at it as if he didn't know whose it was. Then he slid me off his shoulders onto the sand, gently, and lifted the broken sword.

I got my front end up and growled at the dark. He looked down at me. "I know," he said. "Too many."

My hackles lay flat. It was true. He knew.

He stood between the dark and the children anyway, and the Light came up in him. Not grey. It came up gold, because he had stopped thinking about himself at all, and I have learned to be very afraid of that particular gold. It's the colour they go near the end.

The first jackal leapt.

---

He cut it out of the air.

Half a blade, the way Hazza' had taught him. The gold ran down his arm into the steel, and when the sword went through the jackal it came apart, and for a moment, before it became ash, I saw what was inside it. Faces. Small grey faces pressed against the inside of the shadow, like children against a window. Then sparks, and nothing.

The second knocked him flat. Its jaws were a finger's width from his face. Its breath frosted his eyelashes. He jammed the hilt between its teeth, light burst from his palm, and it shrieked in a voice that was almost a word and was gone.

Then there were four at once.

He fought. He was bleeding from his cheek, the golden cracks were spreading up his arms like a map of wadis after rain, and he was getting slower. I lay in the sand trying to make my legs remember how to be legs, and I watched him get slower.

The fourth jackal came over his shoulder, straight at the children.

The children didn't move. One of the little ones smiled at it.

And a spear came out of the dark.

---

It took the jackal in mid-leap and pinned it to the dune, and the thing burst into ash that fell on the children's upturned faces like grey snow.

Hoofbeats, soft, the padding thud of a camel at full stretch. A pale she-camel, almost white in the dark, like something that had been dead a while and hadn't been told. On her back a tall figure in grey wrappings, his face veiled to the eyes, leaning low from the saddle to wrench his spear free as he passed.

The Veiled Rider. Twice before he'd come out of the dark for Theyab, and never a word, never a face. This was the third time.

He went through the jackals like a scythe through dry grass. The spear went left and right, the pale camel spun on the spot, and jackals burst into ash wherever he went. Theyab fought beside him with gold flaring off the broken sword. The Nameless camel-riders came over the far ridge, took one look, and wheeled away, their white masks turning to watch us as they went, all at the same angle.

Then it was quiet. Not one of the children was crying. Theyab was on his knees, gasping, grey flakes settling in his hair.

The Rider couched his camel and slid down. He stood looking at the boy. Then, slowly, as though his hands belonged to someone else, he unwound the veil.

---

He was a man of perhaps forty with the face of someone left out in the shamal for a hundred years. Lean, burned dark, beard cropped short and shot with grey, cheeks fallen in. He had the look of a man who has slept alone in cold places for so long that he has started to forget what other people's voices sound like.

And his eyes were light. Too light for a man of the sands. Grey-gold, like a dune at first light.

Theyab's eyes.

I watched the understanding come into Theyab's face a piece at a time, like the sun coming up over a dune, and burn everything it touched.

"Who are you?" he whispered.

The man's mouth moved. Nothing came out. He tried again.

"I'm your father," he said.

The little brass bell on Theyab's belt hung perfectly still. And I, lying in the sand at his side, felt every hair along my spine lie down as smooth as oiled leather. Not a growl in me. The man looked at me as though I'd drawn a knife.

---

Theyab didn't move for a long time.

"Three times," he said at last.

"More than that." The man's voice was rough, like a door that hasn't been opened in years and doesn't want to be. "There were nights you never woke."

"You were *there*." Theyab's voice cracked down the middle. "All this time, watching, and you never—" His chest was heaving. "Why did you leave me? In the sand? Why did you *leave* me there?"

The man went down on his knees in front of him.

"To protect you," he said. "They were hunting us both."

The bell stayed silent. My hackles stayed down.

And that was when Theyab broke.

I'd seen him cry before. Not like this. This came from a place under the floor of him, like the tombs under a city. Fifteen years of it. Every stone. Every *nameless*. Every night in the patched tent at the edge of the village wondering who had thrown him away, and whether they had been right to.

He hit the man once in the chest with his fist. There wasn't any strength in it.

Then he fell forward, and his father caught him.

They knelt in the ash, the boy sobbing into the grey wrappings and the man holding him so tightly I thought something would crack, crying without a sound into his son's sun-bleached hair. Up on the ridge the eleven children stood in a row and watched with no expression at all, and hummed their two notes, and I dragged myself between them and that embrace without quite knowing why.

---

His name was Sharood.

I knew it already. I'd run beside him once, when he was young and had a feather on his chest and was so afraid of Tal Moreeb that he couldn't say the name of the dune aloud. He woke screaming most nights that year. He told me, the way bearers tell me things, lying with his face in my neck, that he could smell his own hair burning in his sleep. He was the eleventh. The one the carvings call *the Runner*. At Ghidd al-Zujaj the rock keeps him as a boy of sixteen, legs pumping, scratched into stone by a hand nobody remembers. It always keeps them as children. Have you noticed that yet? You will.

When he finally looked up from his son, his eyes found mine. The amber one first, then the silver.

"Still alive," he said. "Old dog."

I looked at him. The bearers all say they understand me. He had been a bearer. He understood me well enough. *Still running*, I said, in the only way I have, which is with my eyes and my silence, *Runner*.

He looked away first.

Neither of us did anything else. Anything else would have taken all night, and some of it would have been teeth. I'd known who the Rider was from the first spear in the dark, the night of the stones. I'd known his smell under the camel-smell. And I'd done nothing to let the boy know.

I'm noticing a pattern. I expect you are too. Hold on to it. It gets worse.

---

The caves were a night's walk north and east, where the sand ran out against Jebel Hafeet.

A long pale ridge of limestone heaved up out of the plain by Al Ain like the back of something sleeping, the colour of old bone. At its foot lie the old stone beehive tombs the herders say were built by people before people, each with its little black door. The children hummed a little louder going past them.

The dunes can't reach the mountain. That's why he went there. The sand rolls up to the rock every night and falls back, and it can't write on stone. I went down to the foot of the cliff once, while the others slept, and watched the sand come up against the rock in the dark. It scratched. Long, patient strokes. Like something trying to write with its fingernails.

The caves were high, reached by a goat path that switched back and forth across the cliff. Inside: a small fire of ghaf wood, goat hides, spears along the walls, and dozens of leather sacks hanging from pegs, each one full of the grey ash of the jinn-things he'd spent fifteen years hunting. When the fire popped, the sacks shifted on their pegs, all together, as if the ash inside had turned over in its sleep. I kept one ear on them all night.

The children lay down on the hides in a heap and closed their eyes at the same moment. Theyab counted them twice. Then he sat staring at his own left forearm.

There was writing on it. Scratched into the skin, faint and pink, in the crooked letters Hazza' had taught him in the sand: *DON'T FORGET HIS EYES.*

"I don't remember doing it," he said. He looked at it a long time. "Whose eyes?"

I put my head on his knee. It's what a dog has instead of an answer. I think the grey blades in the dhow had already taken the colour of them, and some part of him had felt it going and tried to hold on with a knife, and then forgotten that too.

Sharood sat down by the sleeping children and, very softly, without seeming to notice, began to sing. A low, warm voice. The old song the herders sing about the 'anqa, the fire-bird that burns and rises from its own ash.

The children's humming stopped. Every one of them, at once.

Theyab went completely still. It was the voice from Bir al-Ghina', the man in the water singing over a small boy's hand. He had been hearing it, somewhere under the floor of himself, his whole life.

---

Sharood told him everything that night. I'll keep it short. Some of it isn't mine to give away.

He had carried the Light before Theyab. He had been afraid. Not of the pain, though there is pain, and he described it, and Theyab listened with his hand pressed to his chest. He was afraid of being gone.

"And something else," he said, looking into the fire. "When you get close to Tal Moreeb, you start to hear it. Not the Ifrit, son. Something on the other side of the Light. It's hungry too, and it's been hungry a long time." He rubbed his face. "I was sixteen. I didn't want to be eaten. So I ran."

My hackles did nothing at all. They didn't rise and they didn't settle. They stood half up, like the fur of a dog who hears a sound from a direction that doesn't exist. Sharood looked at the ridge of my back, and then at my eyes, and neither of us moved.

Because he ran, the grey grew. Years later he came down from the mountain and married, and was happy for a while. Theyab asked about his mother.

"Another night," said Sharood, and his face closed like a tent flap with a wind leaning on it. "I swear it. Not tonight."

The bell didn't ring. He meant it. There wasn't time.

On the night Theyab was born, the Nameless came out of a sandstorm. They wanted the Light, and they knew it had passed into the newborn. Sharood fought. He got the baby out. But his first son, a boy of five, was dragged into the storm by grey hands, and the last thing Sharood heard was the boy's screaming stop. Not fade. Stop. As if someone had closed a lid.

He searched for weeks. He believed the boy was dead. And he knew that as long as the baby was with him, they would find them both. So he carried his son across the sands to a poor village of palm-frond huts at the edge of Liwa, laid him down in the middle of a storm, wrapped in grey cloth, and walked away without looking back, because if he'd looked back he would never have stopped.

"The storm covered my tracks," he said. "That was the desert, keeping you. I had to believe that." He watched the fire. "Every night since, I've wondered whether it was keeping you safe, or keeping you for later. The way you keep a lamb for the feast."

Theyab was quiet a long while.

"Your son," he said. "The one they took. What was his name?"

Sharood closed his eyes.

"Saqr," he said.

And Theyab told him.

---

The guard in the hold. The fire-bird song, hummed in the dark. The Light running up the grey blade. A five-year-old boy in a goat-hair tent, holding a newborn wrapped in grey. The mask cracking down the middle, one pale eye staring out of the crack.

"He's alive," Theyab said. "He's one of them. The Seventh. He's been hunting me since the start." He swallowed. "And he keeps letting me go. He doesn't know why."

Sharood didn't move. I watched the words go into him and keep going, the way a stone dropped into an old well keeps going after you've stopped hearing it.

Then he stood, walked to the mouth of the cave, and went down on his knees on the cold ledge above the plain.

I have heard a great many terrible sounds. The sound Sharood made on that ledge was worse than any of them, because it went on, and then it turned into something that wasn't a sound at all, just his whole body heaving in silence. Fifteen years. His son alive the whole time, his name burned, his face taken, taught to steal other people's children. And his father hiding in a mountain with sacks of ash, telling himself stories.

Theyab didn't go to him. Forgiveness doesn't work like that. Not quickly.

He turned to me instead.

"Did you know?"

I could have looked away. I could have got up and gone to sniff at the sacks, the way a dog does when the people get loud. I'd had a great deal of practice.

I didn't look away.

He knew what that meant. They always do.

Something went across his face that I am still, in the long nights, trying to forget.

"You knew he was my *brother*."

I held his eyes. Amber and silver.

He turned his back on me and didn't speak again that night. I lay outside the circle of the fire, where a dog is put when he has done something wrong, and it was the right place for me.

I'd given him the truth about that one. There was another truth I was still lying on, a heavier one, with blood under it. One out of two. You decide whether that counts.

---

In the morning we gave the children away.

In a fold of the plain at the foot of the mountain, where the warm springs come up out of the rock and the ground goes green for a stone's throw, the Al Bu Ghaf were camped, as they were every year when the nights began to cool. Their headman was broad and grey-bearded, with a voice like gravel in a pan. He gave us coffee first, because you give a guest coffee before you ask him anything, even a guest who comes down off the mountain with a dog and eleven shaved children. Then he looked at the shaved heads for a long time.

"The Nameless?" he said.

"Not anymore," said Theyab.

The children were standing in a row, the way they'd been taught, humming. The headman's wife came out of the nearest tent, saw them, and went back in.

"We'll keep them," he said at last. "Three days is a guest's right. These ones can have more than three days. Until someone comes for them, or the grey lifts." He lowered his voice. "We'll keep a lamp burning where they sleep. And we'll hobble their ankles at night, like young camels, the first while. The ones who come out of that ship, sometimes they walk."

Shibl held on to Theyab's waist a long time.

"Will you find Nadim?" he said into Theyab's shirt.

Theyab couldn't lie. He didn't know.

"If I see him, I'll tell him where you are. I'll tell him you were brave."

That was true. Shibl let go.

The smallest child, a boy of five, tugged Theyab's sleeve. "I don't know my name," he whispered. "They burned it. I watched. It smelled like hair."

Theyab knelt in front of him.

"Then you'll find it," he said. "Or you'll make a new one. I did."

The boy looked at him with no expression at all.

Theyab put two fingers in his mouth and whistled. Not loud. A herder's whistle, the high rising one the camel boys use, that cuts through wind. Every camel in the ring lifted its head.

"When I was little," Theyab said, "I didn't have anybody. So at night I used to whistle, out on the dunes. So I'd know I was still there." He took the boy's small cold hand and folded two fingers of it and put them to the boy's lips. "Like that. Blow."

A wet hiss. A thin squeak. Then, on the fifth try, a real whistle, small and high and wobbling, and the boy's eyes changed. Not much. A crack of something in them, like the first crack in a mask.

"If you're ever lost," said Theyab, "whistle. Somebody might come."

As we climbed back toward the caves I could hear him behind us, all the way to the first switchback, whistling. Thin and wobbling and stubborn, at the plain, at the sky. So he'd know he was still there.

My hackles never rose once. I lay that down in the place I keep such things, and didn't know yet what I was keeping it for.

---

That evening Sharood gave him two things.

The first was a pendant on a leather cord: a knot of old silver, black with age, a loop that turned back through itself and never ended.

"Every bearer has worn one," said Sharood. "I took it off the night I ran. It's yours."

Theyab put it on. It lay below the feather mark, and it was warm, the way a stone is warm when someone has been holding it. Nobody had been holding it.

The second was a small, battered compass in a dented brass case, the kind the pearling captains buy from the Indian traders on the coast.

"It doesn't point north," said Sharood. "It points toward whatever you're running from. For fifteen years it pointed at Tal Moreeb. I stopped opening it. I could feel it pointing through the case, like a finger in my back."

Theyab opened it. The needle spun, wobbled, and settled. Not toward the sands. Not toward Liwa, where the highest dune in the world waited for the full moon.

It pointed at the broken sword, lying on the hides beside the fire.

---

He'd carried that sword every step since the night of the *badr* and never once really looked at it, because looking at it meant looking at a body in the morning sand, grey and cracked with gold.

He turned it over now. Half a blade, notched and dull. A plain leather grip. A round brass pommel.

The pommel was loose. Gritty, as if it had been unscrewed and screwed back in a hurry, in the dark, by someone with shaking hands. He twisted it, and twisted again, and it came away, and the hilt was hollow.

Inside was a roll of paper, tied with thread.

A broken seal of grey wax, pressed with a little curling mark we both knew, because we had watched an old man press that same seal every evening in Al-Nakhlatain by the lamp, with the hooded falcon on its perch not moving.

Wazir Muhaisen's seal.

I smelled it before he'd unrolled it. Rosewater and grey ash. I growled, low, before I knew I was doing it, and my hackles went up so stiff they hurt.

Theyab read it himself. Slowly. Out loud, the way Hazza' had taught him, right to left, his finger under each word.

"*To the Brothers,*" he read. "*On the Night of Lamps, when every lamp in Ubar is lit, the Sheikh will die. Be ready on the stairs of the fort. When he falls, put out the lamps. Then the city. The eldest son is gone. The youngest will come to me. Speak no names.*"

His finger stopped at the bottom.

"*The Voice.*"

---

The fire cracked. On their pegs, the sacks of ash stirred. Down at the foot of the cliff, the sand went on scratching.

"He knew," Theyab said at last. "Hazza'. He was carrying this. Why didn't he tell me?"

"Perhaps he meant to," said Sharood. "Perhaps he ran out of time."

I put my nose on my paws and looked at the fire. Let me keep it a little longer. It's the only thing I have that's heavier than I am.

"Hazza' taught me to read," Theyab said.

It came out of him like something breaking open. As if every evening at Bir al-Ghina', every letter written backwards in the sand, every time Hazza' laughed and rubbed it out with his heel and made him try again, had been leading to this cave and this paper.

As if it had all been for something.

I wanted to believe that with him. It frightened me too. Because when everything in a life starts lining up to lead somewhere, little one, you should ask who drew the line.

Later I watched him unwrap Old Bakhit's broken golden feather. The break had nearly closed, mending a little more every day as he walked toward the end, like flesh closing over a thorn. I hated it.

He slid the letter back into the hilt and screwed the pommel tight. Then he stood.

"I'm going to Ubar," he said.

Sharood was on his feet. "Ubar isn't on any map. Nobody finds it who isn't dead or carrying the dead. And every gate it has is waiting for the Prince-Killer. You'll walk straight into Muhaisen's hands."

"Probably," said Theyab.

The bell didn't ring.

"The Sheikh has to see this. Hazza's little brother is in that fort. And Hazza' died carrying it." He picked up the sword. "I promised him I'd never give up."

Sharood looked at him a long moment. Then he put a hand on his son's shoulder.

Theyab let it stay. He didn't lean into it. He didn't call him father. But he didn't step away.

It's not nothing. In the sands you learn to count the things that are not nothing. You count them the way the people of Ubar count their lamp oil: by the drop, knowing exactly how little is left.

---

*End of Chapter Sixteen*


---

# PART FIVE: THE END IS THE BEGINNING


---

# Chapter Seventeen
## The Walls of Ubar

> *Behind every wall a lamp; behind every lamp a fear; beneath every street a knock. Do not answer it.*
> — a saying of the lamp-keepers of Ubar

---

Ubar isn't on any map.

Every child on this coast has heard of it. The city of tall towers that grew too proud and was swallowed by the sand in a single night. The old men tell it at the fire with their coffee going cold, as a story about pride.

They have it wrong in one particular. Ubar wasn't swallowed. It was *kept*. It stands where it always stood, deep in the red sands south of Liwa, where the dunes rise like mountains and nothing lives that doesn't have to. You don't find it. It lets you in. The dead find it easily enough: for as long as anyone remembers, the dead of the whole desert have been carried there on slow camels, wrapped in white, by guides who don't speak about the road.

We had a broken sword, a letter, a compass that pointed backwards, and a dog. None of us knew the way.

---

On the twelfth day south the wind began to carry a smell I didn't like. Lamp smoke, old and oily, ten thousand wicks burning at once, and under it something sweet and closed, like dates left too long in a shut jar.

I put my nose up and whined.

"You smell it," said Sharood. "Close is as far as I've ever got. You can smell Ubar. You never see it."

Then I smelled something else. Camels. Myrrh, the kind they pack the dead in. And a man.

He came over the next ridge on foot, leading three camels, and across each saddle lay a long white bundle. A young man, thin, sun-black, his face wrapped against the sand. He stopped when he saw us. He looked at Theyab a long time, and pulled the cloth down.

The left side of his face was paler than the right, a faint ashy patch from temple to jaw where the grey had licked him once and never quite let go. I knew that smell. I'd lain across his lap once, in the wreck of a caravan, after the zawba'a went through it.

"You," said Jasir.

Theyab didn't move.

"You gave me your water," Jasir said. "The last of it. I was dying in the sand and you put your hands on my face, and there was light, and then you put your waterskin in my mouth, and I called you—" His jaw worked. "I called you a monster. In front of everyone that was left."

"I remember," said Theyab.

"So do I. Every day." Jasir looked at the bundles. "Abu Kharbash is dead. Nobody hires a man from a caravan the whirlwind ate. Bad luck sticks. So now I guide the dead to Ubar. The dead don't care about luck." He looked at the broken sword. "Every coffee house from Buraimi to the coast says you killed the Sheikh's son. There's a thousand thalers on you."

"I didn't kill him."

I sat by Theyab's leg with my hackles as flat as a wet cat's. Jasir looked at me, and at the boy.

"Your water tasted of goat," he said at last, and touched the old waterskin hanging from his saddle, a knot of faded red wool still tied round its neck. "It was the best thing I ever drank." He turned his lead camel. "You can't use the gates. Come with the dead. The dead go in by the old way."

---

The old way was a *falaj*: one of the ancient water channels cut through the rock underground, with the men who dug it a thousand years dead. Ubar's runs under the sand from a spring nobody has seen, and the dead go in along it.

From the top of the last dune, before we went down, I saw the city.

It lay in a bowl in the sand as if a great hand had pressed it there. Walls of dark stone, eight towers taller than any fort on the coast, and every tower lit from foot to crown with lamps, so many that the smoke hung over the city like a bruise. The walls didn't reflect the last of the sun. They drank it. And all around, pressed against the edge of the lamplight, sat the grey. Patient as a dog outside a butcher's door. I know how that dog feels. I didn't like seeing it from the outside.

Sharood stood by the pale camel, not saying things. It ran in the family.

"I'll stay out here," he said. "The Nameless won't come through the gates like honest men. I'll see them first. And Theyab. Come back out to me. After. Swear it."

The boy was quiet a long time. He *couldn't* swear it. He'd started waking at night beating at his own chest, at flames that weren't there. If he'd sworn, the mark would have burned him like a coal.

"I'll try," he said.

My hackles lay flat. It was completely true. It was nothing like enough.

Sharood rode away along the hollow between the dunes, and the wind smoothed out his tracks behind him, the way it does for things the desert means to keep.

---

Jasir walked us in himself, down a ramp of worn stone into the dark, between posts carved so long ago the carving had worn to a suggestion: a hand, an eye, something that might have been a lamp.

The water was knee-deep on the men and considerably more on me. Salukis are made for running down gazelles across open sand in the sunlight. No saluki in the history of the world was made for wading through the dark under a city of the dead. I waded anyway, with my tail held up out of it on principle.

Theyab's mark glowed through his wet shirt and gave us just enough light. That was a pity, because then we could see the walls.

The falaj ran through the tombs.

The rock on either side was cut into shelves going up into the dark and on into the dark, and on every shelf lay a long white shape, wrapped and still. Above each, a name painted in red ochre. Up in the city, Jasir whispered, there were painters who went over the names day and night. Down here the names were left to do what names in Ubar do.

"Which is what?" said Theyab.

"Go," said Jasir.

Most were nearly gone. The ghost of a letter. A smear like a thumbprint.

And the tombs breathed.

Not a draught. The air went *in*, all of it, down the length of the tunnel, slow and steady, pulling at the feathering on my ears. A long pause. Then it came back out, warm, smelling of old linen and myrrh and something sweet underneath, like fruit gone soft. The water rose a finger's width against my chest on the in-breath and sank on the out. The camels' eyes rolled white. Jasir talked to them in the low steady voice camel men use, and they went on.

Theyab read some of the names. A boy who's only just learned to read reads everything.

"*Son of*," he whispered. "*Little daughter of*— *Beloved wife of*—" As he said *beloved*, the last red letters slid down the stone like a drop of water and were gone, and the name went with them before he could read it. "Did I do that?"

"No," said Jasir. "They do that by themselves."

I sniffed where the name had been. It smelled of nothing at all. The jackal-smell. Something on the other side of the stone had drunk it.

Where the channel bent there was an alcove with eleven shelves in it. Small ones. Child-length. Empty. Above them no names, only eleven painted feathers, gone the colour of dried blood.

Theyab stood in the water a long time.

"Ghareeb," he said. "Who paints the names of the bearers?"

I couldn't have answered. I'll tell you, though, because you'll need it. Nobody does. Nobody ever has. The desert remembers the *bearers*. Not the children.

Then, from under us, under the water and the stone, came a knock.

Three knocks. Slow. Patient. The way you'd knock on the door of someone you knew was home.

The tombs drew in a long breath and held it.

The knocking didn't come again. After a while the air went out, warm and sweet, and Jasir walked on much faster, and Theyab didn't read any more names.

---

Where the falaj split in three, Jasir stopped.

"I take the dead up the left way, to the washing house," he whispered. "They search everyone who comes out of the ground at the top. I don't know any other way. I'm sorry. I'll go up slow. I'll talk a lot."

He touched Theyab's arm, once, where the golden cracks were, and pulled his hand back fast. Then he led his dead up into the dark, and we were alone with two black tunnels and the breathing.

That was when I smelled the boy.

He was sitting up on a tomb shelf among the wrapped dead, so still I'd taken him for one of them. Fourteen or so, in what had been a stable lad's tunic. Thin as a lamp-wick. His skin was the grey of the emptied, but not all of it: patches of brown were left on his hands and one cheek, like the last colour on a cloth left out in the sun. His eyes were white, except for a thin ring of dark round the edge of each, like the rim of a well.

Under the grave-smell he smelled of camels and straw. He'd been down here a long time and the stable hadn't quite washed off him.

"Prince Hazza' says hide," he said, in a flat, pleasant voice. "Prince Hazza' says hide."

Theyab went white under the gold.

"Suhail," he said.

Hazza' had told him the story at Bir al-Ghina'. The stable boy the grey had taken. The boy he'd hidden in the fodder loft over the stables and tried to cure with honey and talking and stubbornness. The guards. The sword that broke defending him. And Wazir Muhaisen, after, smoothing his robes: *The boy has been taken away. For everyone's safety.*

Taken away. Down. Into the tombs, alive, where nobody would go looking. Where the knocking was.

Theyab climbed onto the shelf and put his hands on the boy's face. I told him with my teeth that it was a poor idea. He didn't listen.

The Light came, gold, and the grey didn't go. It flinched back a finger's width from the boy's mouth and stopped there. Too much of him was eaten, too long ago.

Theyab came back gasping, and I knew from his face what he'd seen: a stable full of hay and sunlight, a laughing young man with dark eyes pushing a frightened boy behind the feed sacks, *Here, quickly, hide, I won't let them.* And his own death, a breath longer than last time.

But the dark rings in Suhail's eyes were wider.

He looked at the hilt over Theyab's shoulder.

"He hid me," he said. Not flat. A boy's voice, rusty, surprised. "He hid me."

Then the grey came back over his mouth, and he climbed down into the water and walked away down the right-hand tunnel. We followed. What else was there?

He took us by ways no one living had used in a hundred years. Through a gap behind a fallen tomb-door. Along a ledge above black water. Up a channel so narrow Theyab went sideways and I went on my belly, until the air was warm and smelled, impossibly, of bread.

At the end were slippery steps and a wooden hatch.

Suhail stopped at the bottom. For one moment the dark rings were very wide.

"He used to go up there," he said. "For the sweet things. He always brought me one."

Then the white came back, and he turned down into the dark saying *hide, hide*, and the tombs breathed his voice in.

"I'll come back for you," Theyab said into the dark.

The bell on his belt gave the faintest shiver. He heard it. He didn't say it again.

He pushed the hatch up, and through the gap came bread, onions, cardamom, saffron and hot date syrup. He climbed out into a storeroom of rice sacks and hanging dried lemons, and found himself face to face with a cleaver.

---

The woman holding it was the largest person I had seen in some centuries.

Nearly as tall as Sharood and twice as wide, in a black abaya dusted with flour, the gold burqa of the old coast women across her nose and brows, and forearms like a well-digger's. She held the cleaver at the height of a head coming out of the hatch, the way you would if you'd been waiting.

"Back down," she said. Her voice could have stripped the gilt off a coffee pot. "I heard the knocking. You've a count of three to show me you're breathing on your own—"

Then she saw the hilt of the sword. The cleaver dropped a hand's width.

"Where," she said, in a completely different voice, "did you get that?"

Theyab stood dripping on her clean floor and told her the truth, because it was the only thing he had ever known how to do.

"It was Hazza's. He was my friend. I didn't kill him."

I sat down on a sack of rice and let her look at me. Every hair flat. Not a sound.

"He growls when someone lies," said Theyab, a little apologetically.

"Then he'd growl himself hoarse in this fort," she said. "And he's wet on my rice." Then her hand went up under the burqa to her mouth, and her eyes went wide, and then wet. The cleaver rang on the stone.

"I knew it," she whispered. "I *knew* it. I told them, and nobody listens to the cook."

She was Umm Shamma. She'd run the fort's kitchens for thirty years and half-raised the Sheikh's two sons, because the Sheikh was busy being a sheikh and somebody had to make sure the boys ate. She sat Theyab on a crate and put rice and lentils and a plate of her honey cakes in front of him, little fried rounds of dough shining with sidr honey. She put a bowl of goat's milk on the floor for me. I want that recorded.

"That boy," she said, pointing a floury finger at the hilt as if Hazza' were inside it, "stole honey cakes from my kitchen for ten years. Came up out of that hatch smelling of the dead every time, and thought I didn't know. Half of them went out to the stables, to that poor stable boy. I knew that too." Her chin trembled. "A boy like that doesn't kill anyone. And neither do you. Eat."

Theyab ate slowly, the way he'd eaten Umm Hanan's bundles, as if somebody might take it away.

"Suhail's still down there," he said. "He said Hazza' always brought him one."

Umm Shamma turned to stir a pot that didn't need stirring, and her big shoulders shook. When she turned back she was already tying up a cloth of bread and dates, and she set it by the hatch.

"I'll go down tonight," she said. "I'm not frightened of the dead. It's the living in this fort I'm frightened of."

My hackles stayed flat.

---

She hid us behind the oil jars in the back storeroom, great clay things taller than Theyab.

"Don't knock them. That's three months' light for this wing. People here have been killed for less oil than you could carry in your mouth."

At the inner gates, she said, the oil-sellers measured it out with brass spoons, a thumb at a time, to silent grey-faced queues. A thaler for a cupful. In Ubar they don't swear by their fathers' graves. They swear by their oil. Because if the last lamp in the city ever goes out, the dark outside the walls will walk in, and what knocks under the streets will come up.

The Sheikh did not eat, she told us, as if that were the worst of it. Since Hazza's death the only man he listened to was his wazir.

"Muhaisen," said Theyab. She spat into the cooking fire.

"Grey eyes. Soft voice. He's brought the Sheikh Hazza's ring. Said he took it from the Prince-Killer's own hand. Proof, he called it." She looked at Theyab sideways. "The Sheikh wears it on a chain now. Over his heart. And they painted Hazza's name on the wall of the fort court, under the traitors, and the painters aren't allowed to touch it up. So every day there's less of him. And the little one, Zaid, sits in front of it every sunset with a pot of ochre he stole from the painters and goes over the letters with his finger."

Then she asked what I'd been dreading.

"How did he die? Our Hazza'. Did it hurt him?"

Theyab didn't know, and it had been cutting him every night for a month.

"I wasn't there," he said. "I should have been."

My hackles lay flat. It was true. I looked at the floor.

Umm Shamma looked at me. She'd spent thirty years knowing which kitchen boy had broken a jar by the set of his shoulders. She looked at the set of mine. She didn't ask. You can't ask a dog.

I was grateful. I was ashamed of being grateful.

I'll tell you about that night soon, little one. Let me be a coward a little longer.

---

Zaid came down after midnight, bare feet on the back stairs, lifting the lid off the honey-cake jar exactly the way someone else had taught him.

Theyab stood up behind the jars. His mark glowed.

The boy froze. He was ten, small for it, with his brother's dark eyes and none of his swagger, and red ochre dried under every fingernail.

"That's Hazza's," he whispered.

"Yes."

"They said you killed him."

"I didn't."

I padded out and stood in front of him, every hair flat, and put my nose into his ochre-stained palm. He didn't pull it away.

"He knows when people lie," said Theyab. "Hazza' said he was the rudest dog in the Empty Quarter. He said he once ate a scorpion to impress him and was sick for a whole day, and Ghareeb watched and wagged."

That was generous about the wagging. I had thumped my tail twice, that was all. The mark didn't burn, so I suppose it counts. I let it go. Zaid made a small cracked sound, and it took me a moment to understand it was a laugh.

Then he sat down on the floor and cried, and Theyab sat beside him, shoulders touching, the way you share warmth at a fire, and I lay down across both their feet.

"His name's going," Zaid said into his knees. "I painted it three times today and by evening the last letter was gone again. Is that what happens? If nobody remembers you hard enough?"

Under the floor, very faintly, something knocked three times. The boy didn't even flinch. He'd grown up with it.

Theyab took out the letter. Zaid read it by the light of the mark, and I watched his face change halfway down.

"The Night of Lamps is tomorrow," he said. "The moon will be full. My father sits in the majlis in the morning. Anyone can come. I could take you in."

---

The majlis at the heart of the fort had no windows. It was lit only by lamps, hundreds of them, hanging on long chains from palm-trunk beams so high and black with soot that the lamps looked like stars in a sky with no moon. Every sound went up into the shadows and came back softer and slightly wrong, a word you hadn't said, a cough a beat late, as though the dark up there were listening and practising.

Along the walls the men of Ubar sat on cushions with their coffee cups, each with a little lamp of his own burning on the carpet beside him. At the far end, on a black stone bench spread with a camel-hair rug, sat the saddest man I have ever seen.

Sheikh Mutlaq had been tall and broad once. You could see the shape of it, the way you see the shape of a house after the roof has gone. A heavy gold ring hung on a thin chain at his neck, and every few breaths his hand drifted up to touch it.

One step below him sat Wazir Muhaisen. Silver beard, smooth face, robes the colour of sand, rosewater. His faded grey eyes found Theyab the instant the boy stepped out from behind Zaid.

He smiled. A small smile. The smile of a man who sets a trap and comes back in the morning to find the fox in it, exactly where he'd pictured it.

"The Prince-Killer!" someone shouted, and then everyone was shouting. Rifles came up, old Martinis and a matchlock older than the fort. A merchant shrieked *Monster!* and I saw the gold on Theyab's skin flicker grey.

I stood in front of him with my lips back. A saluki with his teeth out is not a small thing, even an old one.

"*Stop!*" Zaid had planted himself in front of us both with his arms out, shaking. "Father, he's come to your majlis. He's under your roof. You have to *listen*."

The Sheikh looked at his son a long time. Then he lifted one tired hand, and the rifles went down.

Theyab read the letter aloud. Every word. His voice didn't shake. Hazza' had made him practise until it didn't.

When he finished, I could hear the lamps hissing, and far under the floor, the slow breathing of the tombs.

"That is Wazir Muhaisen's seal," said Theyab. "Hazza' took this from one of the Nameless and hid it in his sword. They killed him before he could bring it home."

And Muhaisen sighed, gently, like a teacher whose favourite pupil has made a small mistake.

"My Sheikh will remember I reported my seal stolen on the night of the young sheikh's exile, and had a new one cut in Muscat. Every man in this majlis heard me." He spread his soft hands. "A forgery in a broken sword, carried by the boy who killed him, on the eve of the Night of Lamps. It is clever. And my Sheikh already wears the proof of what this boy took from Hazza's body."

Long before Hazza' found that letter, Muhaisen had made sure that if it was ever found, it would look like a lie.

My hackles went up from my neck to my tail. I growled, low and long, the way I'd growl at a viper in a tent.

"Did you kill Hazza'?" said Theyab. It came out of him the way the Light did, without permission.

Muhaisen looked down at him, soft and sad. "I loved that boy," he said, "as if he were my own son."

From Theyab's belt, Old Bakhit's little brass truth bell rang.

In that hall it sounded like a stone dropped down a well. And up in the dark above the lamps the hall rang it back, faint and wrong, a dozen tiny bells that weren't there. My growl went on under it.

"A Bedouin trinket," said Muhaisen, without looking. "Bells ring when boys shake them, my Sheikh. And dogs growl at strangers."

"He didn't shake it," said Zaid.

"I didn't kill your son," Theyab said to the Sheikh, very low. "I left him alone that night. We argued, and I walked away, and when I came back he was dead. That's the worst thing I've ever done. But I didn't kill him."

My growl stopped as if cut with a knife. Every hair on me lay down.

Some of the men along the walls had hunted with salukis all their lives. I saw them look at me, and understand, the way men understand their own dogs. I saw them look at the wazir.

The Sheikh wavered, like a dune shifting at night. Believing us meant believing he'd exiled his son for a lie and worn his ring over his heart because the man who killed him had handed it over. No father can look at that straight on. Not all at once.

"Hold the boy," Sheikh Mutlaq said at last. "In the east tower. He is under my roof; no one touches him. After the Night of Lamps I will hear this again. From both of you." He looked at Muhaisen. "Both."

"Of course, my Sheikh," said Muhaisen. "Shall we cancel the Night of Lamps? For safety?"

The Sheikh closed his eyes. "No. The people need their lamps."

As the guards took Theyab's arms, I saw the wazir's face when he thought no one was looking.

He was still smiling.

---

The tower room was small and round, with one window facing east. They took the sword and the bell. Nobody had worked out how you arrest a saluki. I lay down across the doorway, and the guard looked at me and decided to leave it at that.

The sun went down. All over the city, bolts slid home one after another like a great slow clock. Then the whispering began from every rooftop: the people of Ubar reading the names of their dead off the walls before the paint could fade further. *Son of. Daughter of. Beloved.* And under it, all across the city, so faint you'd think it was your own heart, the knocking. Three. Pause. Three. From under every street at once.

"They're answering," Theyab said at the window. "Something down there's answering the names."

My hackles didn't move. He saw them not move.

A soft sound at the door. Not a guard. Guards stamp.

Muhaisen's face appeared at the little barred window in the wood. In the dark his eyes looked almost white, like the emptied.

"Thank you," he said gently, "for coming. I've wondered for a month how to bring you inside these walls on the Night of Lamps, under the full moon. And you came by yourself, up through the dead, with a letter in your hand." He sounded almost fond. "The desert really does write in the sand. It wants you here as much as I do. Ask yourself why, bearer."

Then, softer still: "Tomorrow night, the lamps go out."

I lay with my nose a hand's width from his slippers and every hair on my body flat. Not a growl. Theyab looked down at me, and I saw him understand. Everything Muhaisen had said was true. That was what made it so terrible.

The slippers went away down the stair.

Theyab went to the window, and I put my forepaws on the sill beside him. Below, the city lay under its smoke, ten thousand small flames holding back the dark, each one bought a thumb at a time. And on the roof of the tower opposite, at the edge of the lamplight, a figure in a grey robe crouched with two grey blades across his knees. His mask was a smooth white face with a crack running down the middle, brow to chin.

He was looking up at our window.

And very faintly, under the whispering of the names and the knocking beneath them, so faint I might have been the only creature in the world with ears good enough to hear it, he was humming.

---

*End of Chapter Seventeen*


---

# Chapter Eighteen
## The Night of Lamps

> *On the Night of Lamps, every lamp is a name. When the lamps go out, so do the names.*
> — painted above the gate of the Sheikh's court, Ubar; repainted daily

---

Once a year, on the night the moon comes up perfectly round, the whole of the sands lights lamps for the lost bearers.

In the villages of Liwa it's a few clay lamps on doorsteps. Everyone goes to sleep feeling vaguely blessed and slightly relieved, the way you feel after a burial that wasn't yours.

In Ubar, every family carried one extra lamp through the streets at dusk, filled with oil saved for a year, a thumb at a time, and set it in the wall niches where the names of their dead were painted, so that for one night the names glowed and nothing could drink them. And on the great black stairs of the fort, eleven brass lamps with panes of coloured glass were set out in a row. One for each bearer who had come before.

I stood with my forepaws on the tower sill and watched an old lamp-keeper light them, one by one.

The Shepherd. The Weaver's Daughter. The Smith. The Twin Who Stayed. The Last Sheikh. The Sand Singer. The Boy of Salt. The Falconer. The Blind One. The Dreamer. The Runner.

I knew every one of them. I knew which had wet themselves on the last night, and which had cried for their mothers. The Boy of Salt was nine. Not one soul down there could tell you who they were lighting a lamp for, or how old, or what they'd smelled like burning.

Behind me Theyab sat against the wall with his eyes shut. The mark glowed through his shirt, brighter than I'd ever seen it, as though it knew what night it was and was looking forward to it.

"Eleven," he said. "There should be another one. Tomorrow."

I came and put my chin on his foot.

---

Before the moon was up I saw the dust.

Out on the dunes to the east, in the last red of the light, camels at the run, a dozen strung out. I put my nose to the window slit. Goat-hair tents. Coffee smoke. Ghaf wood. Children.

The Al Bu Ghaf.

They came down the last dune to the east gate in a spray of sand, men shouting hoarse up at the wall, and among them a small high voice shouting the same thing over and over, and a small shaved head in front of the headman's saddle.

The gate didn't open. Men on the wall laughed and pointed rifles down. Raiders, they thought, or beggars, or Nameless, with that shaved child.

But in the lane just inside the gate, doors opened. A man and a woman came out and stood listening to the shouting from beyond the wall. Then they went back in, took their family's lamp out of its niche and in with them, and barred the door. The house beside theirs did the same. Then the next.

I found out later what the voice was shouting. Three nights before, the eleven children had sat up in the Al Bu Ghaf tents all at once, in the dark, and said in the flat voice of the hold: *The lamps go out on the night of the full moon. Ubar first.* They'd heard it through the dhow's planks. They hadn't known they remembered it. The headman had looked at those eleven faces for one night and then saddled every camel he had.

The camels wheeled away round the wall, south, toward where Sharood was waiting.

The moon cleared the dunes.

---

The first lamp went out just after it rose.

One tiny flame on the western wall, and then it simply wasn't there. Then the one beside it. A gap of darkness opened along the wall, spreading, like a black thread drawn slowly through gold cloth.

Down in the city, the whispering of the names stopped. Ten thousand faces turned west.

In the silence, from under every street, came the knocking.

Not three knocks. Not patient. Hundreds of fists, all at once, hammering on the underside of the city like people trapped under the ice of a well.

Then somebody screamed, and every lamp on the southern wall went out together.

There were shapes on the walls. Grey robes, white masks. The Nameless walked the battlements in a line, and as they passed each lamp they cupped a hand over it, almost tenderly, the way you'd close a dead man's eyes, and it died. They were humming the wordless chant from the dhow.

And where the light died, the grey came in.

It poured over the walls like sea fog at dawn and slid down into the streets. Where it touched the wall niches, the names didn't fade. They *ran*. The red ochre went liquid and slid down the stone in long streaks, a hundred years of names bleeding into the gutters, and the grey lapped them up as they went.

Then the iron grates over the tomb shafts began to lift.

All over the city, slowly, pushed from below. Out of them came hands. Then arms. Then figures, grey and white-eyed, wrapped in rotted linen that trailed behind them. They had been down there a long time, knocking, waiting to be let in.

The people of Ubar saw them climbing out of the ground in grave-wrappings and thought their dead were rising.

Some of them were right.

---

There had been two guards on the stair. I'd heard nothing from them for some time. Not a step. Not a breath. The bolt slid back.

The Seventh stood in the doorway.

Through the crack in his mask I could see a strip of his real face: the edge of one pale eye, a line of cheek so white it had never seen the sun. He had Hazza's sword in one hand and Old Bakhit's bell in the other.

He held them out.

I didn't growl. A man in a Nameless mask, smelling of the hold and the grey blades, and every hair on my back lay flat, because there was nothing in him to be false.

"Saqr," said Theyab.

The figure flinched as if struck. The sword and the bell clattered to the floor. His hands came up to his mask and gripped its edges and pulled. I saw the skin of his jaw stretch where the rim met it. It didn't come away. It had grown there. He let go, shaking, with a sound like a hurt dog. I know that sound. I've made it.

He hummed four notes. Not the chant. The beginning of the fire-bird song.

Then he turned and ran.

Theyab picked up the sword, and tied the bell back on his belt by its camel-hair cord. "He's still in there," he said.

I licked his hand. I'd known since the first night the Seventh let him go. I put that on the pile with the rest.

---

The streets were full of grey.

Most of the lamps were out. The ones still burning lay smashed on the stones, oil spreading in burning pools, and people knelt beside them scooping the burning oil up in their bare hands to save it, screaming as they did it, and not stopping.

And everywhere, the emptied.

They moved through the crowds slowly, reaching out, touching. At every touch, someone stopped running and stood with their eyes going white and lost. Then the newly emptied began to speak, each saying the last thing they had said before the touch, over and over, in a flat, pleasant voice, smiling with faces that didn't know how to smile anymore.

*Bar the door. Bar the door.*

*Mother, I'm here. Mother, I'm here.*

*Where's the oil, where's the oil.*

At the corner of the oil market a merchant's stall was burning. The great jars had cracked in the heat, and the oil ran down the lane in a gold river, and the man who owned it was standing in the middle of it.

I knew his kind by the smell before I saw his face. Musk and stale tobacco, and the sour sweat of a man counting money he hasn't earned. Ghanim had smelled like that under his rose attar, over his crooked scales in Hilat al-Sarab.

This one was an oil-seller of Ubar, one of the brass-spoon men from the inner gates.

He'd hoarded twenty camel-loads of oil for the one night of the year the city would pay anything. His camels were gone, led off loaded through the east lanes by grey robes. His strongbox lay open and empty at his feet. His ledger was burning. And he stood in his own oil with the hem of his robe smouldering and his eyes gone white, holding up one finger and smiling.

"Two thalers the thumb," he said pleasantly, to no one. "Two thalers the thumb."

That was the last thing he'd said before the hand touched him. He'd been naming a price.

The awning went up with a roar, and when the smoke cleared the grey had carried him off down the lane with the others, still holding up his finger. I didn't growl. Two thalers the thumb was exactly what he'd meant to charge.

---

At a wall niche in the next lane a girl of six stood alone. One of the emptied had touched her cheek, and the grey was spreading across her face like a stain through cloth. She was looking up at the wall where her family's names had been. There was nothing on it now but red streaks running down into the gutter.

"Whose names were those?" she said to no one. "I can't… whose were they?"

Then she said it again, in exactly the same voice. The grey had reached the corner of her mouth.

Theyab was on his knees in front of her before I could get between them. I barked, once, hard: *every jackal in this city will come.*
