"Yes," said Theyab, and put his hands on her face.

The Light came gold. It always did, by then, when it was for someone else. The grey shrank back from it like frost from the sun. Her eyes cleared. On the wall behind her a single name glowed faint and gold where the ochre had run. Her grandmother's. Then it faded. But the girl remembered it. I watched her remember.

And Theyab saw. An old woman's henna'd hands braiding her hair. A grown woman reading names at this same wall.

And then his own death, the way it always came now. Not a picture. A place he was *in*.

The full moon, close enough to touch. The cold sand of Tal Moreeb under his bare feet. And the heat, starting in his chest, under the mark, the way a coal starts in kindling. His skin splitting along the golden lines. Hair, cloth, and something under them that was himself. A small boy's hand slipping out of his. His father on his knees. Grey hands plunging into the gold. Letters moving across the dunes, a word he could almost read. And behind the letters, at the very edge of the world, something watching the fire the way a man watches a coffee pot come to the boil.

He came back screaming.

He'd never done that before. He knelt on the stones beating at his own chest, at flames that weren't there, and I pushed my head into him and licked his face, over and over, the way you lick a pup that won't wake, until he stopped. The cloth over his mark had scorched brown in the shape of a feather.

The girl's mother dragged her away into the dark without a word.

"I saw a word," Theyab said, raw. "In the sand. And something past it."

Across the lane three jackals had come out of the grey with their coal eyes on him, and the one in front was saying *Theyab, Theyab* in a little wet child's voice.

---

They never reached us.

A spear took the first through the chest, and it burst into black sand. The pale camel came down the lane like a ghost with Sharood on her back, his veil torn half away. And behind him, filling the lane, the hard brown faces of the Al Bu Ghaf, their old rifles going off like dry palm-ribs in a fire, the headman bellowing like one of his own bulls.

Sharood was always going to come. Fifteen years late, but always.

"*The fort!*" he shouted. "They're inside! Go!"

The third jackal sprang at Theyab's throat.

It met me instead.

I don't like to talk about the other shape. But it happened in that lane, and I didn't care who saw. The light of the burning oil threw my shadow up the wall, and the shadow didn't stay the size of a dog. It swelled up the wall and over the roofs, long and lean and wrong, a saluki the height of a watchtower with a silver coal for an eye. And I moved inside it with a strength no dog has ever had or should. I took the jackal out of the air and shook it once, and it came apart like smoke. It tasted like home.

That's the part I hate. Not the size. The joy. The silver half of me that is jinn howled with joy.

The people in the lane screamed at me the way they'd screamed at the things in the grates. An old Al Bu Ghaf rider covered his eyes and asked the One who made the sands to protect him from me. I didn't blame him.

The old burn on my left flank, where Theyab's Light had caught me in the storm, tore open.

Behind us, the grey was rolling down the lane like a wave full of smiling faces. I planted myself across the lane in front of it with my back to him, and growled. In that shape, my growl rattles shutters. He understood. The bearers always do.

He wasn't afraid of me. He hadn't been for a long time. I hadn't noticed when it changed.

"Don't die," he said.

I'm a great deal harder to kill than you, I thought at him. That's sort of the whole problem.

He ran.

---

The fort stairs were black stone, wide enough for twenty men abreast, rising in one long climb from the court to the carved doors of the majlis. Of the eleven brass lamps along them, seven had gone out. The other four threw coloured light across the steps: red, then green, then gold.

In the court below, Hazza's name was gone from the wall. The stone was perfectly clean. And in front of it, on his knees, a smashed pot of ochre beside him, was Zaid, painting his brother back onto bare stone with his fingers. He didn't look round.

He didn't see the jackal.

It came out from under the stairs on its belly, the way a jackal stalks a kid goat at the edge of the herd. No smell. No sound. Its coal eyes were fixed on the back of the boy's neck. Theyab was a whole court away, and I was a lane behind him holding back a wave.

And from the top of the court wall, someone whistled.

A herder's whistle. High and rising, thin, wobbling, stubborn. The kind the camel boys use, that cuts through wind.

Zaid's head came up. He turned, saw the jackal a body's length from him already gathering to spring, flung the ochre in its face, and ran. Up the stairs, two at a time, like a hare. The jackal's jaws closed on the place his neck had been.

Theyab cut it in half from behind before it could turn.

On top of the court wall, against the moon, stood a small shaved figure with two fingers in its mouth. Five years old. No name. He'd been told to stay with the camels. He had climbed the wall instead. Somebody was lost, and he had whistled, and somebody had come.

He was still whistling when the Al Bu Ghaf headman reached up and hauled him down by the ankles.

Remember that whistle. I do.

---

The Eldest let Zaid run past him through the great doors.

He stood at the top of the stairs with his feet planted wide and a grey blade as long as Theyab was tall. The largest man I have ever seen, and I had recently met Umm Shamma. Tonight he wore the mask, smooth and white and perfect. In all his years at Muhaisen's side he had never once been heard to speak. He watched the small boy run past him into the dark of the majlis and didn't lift a finger.

The most frightening thing I saw that night. The boy wasn't escaping. He was being *let in*.

The Eldest came down the stairs. Theyab met him halfway.

The first blow would have taken Theyab's head if he hadn't dropped flat. It struck the stone and threw sparks. The second came straight down. Theyab rolled, and the edge skimmed his shoulder.

It didn't cut skin. Grey blades don't need to. But the Light dimmed where it touched, and Hazza's face was gone.

Not blurred. Gone. He knew he'd had a friend, more important than anyone. He reached for the face and found a blank, smooth space, as smooth as the mask above him. He reached for the eyes, for *DON'T FORGET HIS EYES*, and there was nothing to not forget. And he could feel himself beginning not to mind.

Then, out of the grey, a voice, laughing at him by a fire a long way back.

*A short blade means you have to get close. Most men are afraid to get close. So be the one who isn't.*

Hazza' had said that on the third night at Bir al-Ghina', showing off. Theyab had rolled his eyes. He remembered rolling his eyes. And from that one small, stupid memory the rest came back like water into a footprint: the scarred jaw, the crooked grin, the dark eyes always laughing. *Dark.* His eyes were dark. *There* you are.

Theyab got up.

The Eldest swung wide to keep him off. Theyab went *in*. Under the swing, so close the long blade was useless, so close he could hear the man breathing behind the mask, quick and shallow, like someone very frightened.

He raised Hazza's broken sword, and the Light came down his arm, into the hilt, along the broken blade, and where the steel ended it kept going. For one heartbeat Hazza's sword was whole, the missing half made of gold as bright as noon. It met the grey blade where hilt joined steel.

The grey blade shattered.

The pieces rang down the stairs like bells. The Eldest stumbled, went down on his knees, and the white mask cracked from brow to chin and fell off him in two halves.

Under it was a tired grey-bearded man with a broken nose and old eyes. He'd been taught, I think, what to do when he lost. He lifted his chin, bared his throat, and waited.

Theyab stood over him with the golden half-blade a finger from that throat.

I'd come up the court by then, a dog again, limping, bleeding. I'll tell you what I wanted. I wanted him to do it. Every child in the dhow had been carried past that man. I'd have done it with my teeth.

The man's lips were moving. Trying, I think, to say a name. He'd been silent so long he'd forgotten how.

The gold on the blade flickered grey at the edges. Then gold.

Theyab lowered the sword.

"No," he said. Not to the man. To himself. Then, to the man: "Go home. If you remember where it is."

He stepped past him up the stairs and didn't look back. The Eldest stayed on his knees with his throat still bared, tears running into his beard. My hackles had never risen once. There was nothing false left in him. There was nearly nothing in him at all.

Then, from inside the majlis, a child screamed.

---

Inside, the dark had won.

Every lamp on its chain had gone out but one, high above the black bench, a single small flame swinging slowly. By its light I could see grey robes and white masks standing in rows along the walls where the men of Ubar had sat that morning with their coffee. Dozens of them. Humming. And beside the black stone bench, Wazir Muhaisen.

He had Zaid, held against his chest with one arm, quite gently, the way you'd hold a sleepy child. In his other hand was a grey blade, thin as a reed.

At the foot of the dais lay the Sheikh.

Theyab ran to him. Nobody stopped him. That was the worst of it.

Sheikh Mutlaq lay on his back with one hand still stretched toward his son. No blood. Grey blades don't do blood. The grey was spreading from his chest like ink through water. His beard was going white in streaks. His eyes were fading from the edges in, the brown draining toward the middle.

"He stepped in front," said Muhaisen softly. "The blade was meant for the boy, to make him easier to carry. My Sheikh stepped in front of it. I didn't expect that. He hasn't moved quickly in years."

Every word true. My hackles stayed flat and I hated them for it.

Theyab put his hands on the Sheikh's chest. The Light came, and the grey drank it, like water poured into sand. He pushed harder, until the cracks on his own skin blazed and began, at the edges, to smoke. The Sheikh's eyes cleared for a moment.

And Theyab saw. The Sheikh, young, swinging a small dark-haired boy up onto a racing camel. The same boy dragged out of this majlis in chains while the Sheikh didn't look up. Then no future. Only dark. And then his own death again, and this time he didn't scream, because he hadn't the breath.

"Hazza'," the Sheikh whispered. "I didn't listen to him. I should have… listened."

"I know," said Theyab.

"Tell him." The Sheikh fumbled at the chain at his neck and pulled, and it broke, and he pressed the ring into Theyab's palm. "Tell him I'm sorry. And give this to Zaid. When he's sheikh."

And Theyab said, "I'll tell him."

I was in the doorway, and I felt my hackles lie down along my spine, smooth, and I understood what it meant, and I wanted to be sick on the Sheikh's carpet. The boy couldn't lie. He believed with his whole heart that before the night was out he would see Hazza' to tell him.

The Sheikh's eyes went past Theyab to his younger son. He smiled at Zaid, a small, broken, loving smile, and the grey reached his eyes as he did it, and the smile stayed after the rest of him had gone. Fixed and pleasant. The smile of the emptied, on a sheikh's mouth.

Zaid stopped screaming.

---

"How touching," said Muhaisen.

High above the bench, the last lamp went out.

In the dark, Theyab's Light was the only light, and it wasn't gold anymore. It had gone grey, dim and flickering, because he was angry enough to tear that fort down stone by stone and everyone in it.

That was what Muhaisen wanted. Grey light is easy to take.

I barked. Once. Sharp, the bark a saluki gives when the hunter is about to ride his camel off a cliff after a lost falcon.

He heard me. He shut his eyes and breathed, and slowly the grey went out of him. Not all the way. Enough.

When he opened them, Muhaisen was at the door to the east roof, with Zaid under one arm. Beyond came the groaning of camels in the court and the howling of jackals.

Zaid wasn't crying. He was humming. Two notes. The chant. He'd been in the wazir's arms only a few minutes.

"Tal Moreeb, bearer," said Muhaisen. "Under the full moon. You know the way. You've been there every time you closed your eyes. Come and give me your name. Or the boy will give me his. He's already halfway to forgetting it."

Then he was gone, and the masks along the walls went with him like smoke.

From the east roof we saw the camels out through the eastern gate and running, jackals loping round them, Muhaisen at the front with Zaid before him on the saddle. At the very back a lone rider with twin blades turned and looked up at the fort a long time. Then he rode after the others.

---

Sharood came up the stair behind us with grey dust on his clothes and his spear black to the elbow. Below, the city was dark, every lamp out, except for one short lane by the east gate, where the families who'd heard a child shouting over the wall at dusk had taken their lamps inside and barred their doors. Their windows glowed. One lane. That was all.

In the streets the emptied had stopped moving and stopped speaking. They stood like grave-posts, every white face turned the same way.

East.

The desert had moved in the night.

It stood a little way out from the walls, so close you could have walked there before the moon went down. A dune higher than any dune in the world, higher than Jebel Hafeet. It rose out of the flat sand in one long, impossible curve, and as I watched, the sand on its flanks moved. Slowly. Up. Like the flank of something breathing. At its very top, white and enormous and perfectly round, sat the full moon.

Every herder in Liwa knows that dune. It stands at the edge of their date gardens, days from here. Their grandfathers' grandfathers grazed goats at its foot.

Tal Moreeb.

It had come to fetch him. Things that are hungry enough don't wait for their food to walk.

Sharood made a sound beside me that I have heard only a few times in my life. The sound a father makes when he understands he can't stop what is coming, and that when he ran from it at sixteen, all he did was keep it waiting for his son.

Theyab looked down at Hazza's ring, and threaded it onto the cord at his neck, beside the silver knot. Where his fingers touched it, it smoked.

"It's time," he said.

Neither of us answered. There was nothing left that was true and kind at the same time.

---

*End of Chapter Eighteen*


---

# Chapter Nineteen
## Tal Moreeb

> *He'll walk where the dunes write the words of the sky,*
> *And the light will be born on the night he will die.*
> — from the song of the fire-bird; the verse the mothers of Liwa will not sing aloud

---

Umm Shamma found us on the fort stairs as we were leaving.

She'd come up out of the kitchens with a ladle like a club and a long red scratch down her cheek where something had reached for her in the dark. Her burqa was gone. She saw the Sheikh's ring on the cord at Theyab's neck and understood everything at once. She didn't cry. She didn't ask. She pushed a small cloth bundle into his hands, took his face between her two enormous palms, and kissed his forehead hard, the way you'd stamp a seal into wax.

She pulled back with her lips blistered. His skin was that hot. She didn't say a word about it.

Then she went back down toward the dark of the kitchens and the hatch, with a lamp in her other hand, to look for a stable boy among the dead. I never saw her again. I have never asked what found her first.

Inside the bundle were dates. Rolled in sesame and soaked in syrup.

---

We rode out through the eastern gate, Sharood in front and Theyab behind him on the pale camel, and me across the boy's lap like a sack of grain, bleeding quietly into his shirt.

The emptied still stood where they'd stopped, every one facing east. As we passed, they began to say their last words again, very softly, all together, so that we went out of Ubar through a long low murmur of *bar the door* and *Mother, I'm here* and *where's the oil*. Then, one by one, they stopped speaking and only smiled.

Theyab unwrapped the bundle and held the dates in his palm.

He ate one. He held one out over Sharood's shoulder, and after a moment his father took it. Then he gave one to me, and I ate it, though I prefer a hare and always will.

"This is the hardest day," Theyab said. "I don't think there's a harder one."

My hackles lay still.

Tal Moreeb rose ahead of us until it filled half the sky, and its flanks went on moving. Up. Pause. Down.

"Father," said Theyab.

It was the first time he'd called him that. I felt Sharood go rigid in the saddle.

"I forgive you."

Sharood didn't turn round. His shoulders shook. "You can't. I ran. If I hadn't run, none of this— your brother, the grey, all of it. You can't forgive that."

"I just did," said Theyab. "And I can't lie. So."

I lay across his knees with every hair on me flat and soft as a puppy's, and Sharood felt it through the saddle, I think. He reached back without looking, and Theyab took his hand, and they rode like that all the way to the foot of the dune. Theyab's hand was so hot that Sharood's palm blistered. He didn't let go.

---

The emptied were waiting.

Not only the ones from Ubar. All of them. They had come from the whole of the sands in the night, from the grey wastes and the emptied villages, from Hilat al-Sarab and the caravan roads and the pearling towns on the coast, and they stood in a ring around the foot of Tal Moreeb a hundred deep. Herders. Divers. Old women. Children. Thousands of white faces turned up toward the top of the dune, all smiling the same smile, all humming the Nameless's two notes so low that the sound came up through the sand into my paws. It was like standing on the skin of a drum at a wedding where everyone is dead.

They parted for us like water.

I saw the oil-seller of Ubar among them, still holding up his one finger. I saw old Sheikh Mazyad with sand in his beard. I didn't see Nadim. I looked.

We climbed. The sand slid under our feet, and under the sand something moved with the breathing, so that every step went down a little further than it should. Halfway up, Theyab turned and picked me up without a word and carried me the rest of the way, across his shoulders, the way he'd carried me out of the dhow. His skin singed my coat. I let him. I was too tired to be proud.

And then we were at the top, and I looked up, and it was the vision.

I had seen it in his eyes every time he healed. Here it was, every piece falling into place like the last stones of a tomb.

The moon hung full and white above Tal Moreeb, so close and so swollen it looked like something that had fed. The crest was pale sand, so cold after his skin that it ached. Below us, on every side, the sands went away silver and grey to the edge of the world.

Wazir Muhaisen waited at the far end of the crest, with Zaid. Around him stood twenty of the Nameless, white masks shining like small moons, and a fire of stolen lamp-oil burning in a ring of stones. A little apart stood the Seventh, the crack in his mask so wide now that half his real face showed through, the skin around it raw where he'd been tearing at it.

And behind the wazir, bare-faced among all those masks, stood the Eldest. Grey beard. Broken nose. His long grey blade had been replaced with another. He stood where he'd been told to stand. His lips were still moving.

Zaid stood quite still in Muhaisen's grip, eyes open, humming.

And between us and them, the grey was gathering.

---

It rose out of the sand like smoke from a fire put out with water. It thickened. It took a shape.

A boy. Smaller than Theyab, barefoot and thin, in a goat-herd's shirt, with a herder's stick. Everything about him was grey. Only his eyes were different: black, completely black, like two holes cut into the night, and if you looked too long you could see things moving far down in them, small and pale, like faces at the bottom of a well. Behind him, his shadow on the moonlit sand was not a boy's. It was far too large, and it had too many joints, and it had horns.

I knew that face. I had known it before there were towns on this coast, when it was brown and laughing and smelled of goats and woodsmoke.

*Hello, Ghadban*, I said. Not aloud. I can't say anything aloud. But he is jinn now, or the jinn's house, and jinn hear me. They always have.

"Hello, old dog." A child's voice, and under it a thousand others, a beat behind, and under those, something deep that wasn't a voice at all. "You've come to watch another one burn."

*I've come to watch one choose. It's different. You'd know, if you'd done it.*

The grey boy smiled. The smile went a little too far back on both sides.

Then he turned his black eyes on Theyab.

"Look at them," Ghadban said softly, pointing his stick down at the emptied. "That one threw stones at you when you were six. Those ones called you monster and watched you starve. And you'd burn for them?"

Theyab's Light flickered grey at the edges.

"And here's what the dog won't tell you," said Ghadban, gentle as sand. "He can't, can he? So convenient. Ask him how many. Not bearers. *Children*. Ask him how old the Boy of Salt was. Ask him why, every time, it's a child. Ask him what's waiting on the other side of the Light with its mouth open. It isn't me, Theyab. I'm only the one who said no."

Theyab looked at me.

And every hair on my body lay down flat. I couldn't stop it. I have never been able to stop it.

I saw it go into him.

"And after," said Ghadban, "they'll light a lamp. One more in a row of eleven. In a hundred years no one will remember what it was for. Bearers' names come off the walls like the names in Ubar. You'll be forgotten." A whisper. "You'll be forgotten, Theyab. Just like you always were."

My hackles went up so hard it hurt, from the base of my skull to my tail, and I growled from the bottom of my chest.

But it didn't matter what I did. It was the oldest fear he had, the one he'd carried since he was a baby crying in the sand. *Nobody will remember me.* And now, beside it, a new one I had put there with my silence: *and something is hungry for me, and always has been.*

His Light went grey all the way through.

"There's a way out," said Wazir Muhaisen.

He stepped forward with one hand on Zaid's shoulder and the thin grey blade in the other. His soft voice carried along the crest the way it had filled the majlis.

"Give me your name. That's all. Say it to the wind. Your true name, the one in your blood. Ghadban will take it, and the Light with it, and it's over. No burning. You'll simply forget. And then everyone will forget, every loss, every grief, every dead son." Something moved in his faded eyes, far down. "I promise you, child, it doesn't hurt. I burned my own name to stop remembering. Forgetting is the only mercy there is."

My hackles rose. Fell. Rose halfway, and stuck there.

He believed it. That's the worst kind of lie, little one: the kind the liar has told himself so long it smells like truth.

"Give me your name," said Muhaisen.

And Theyab, grey and shaking, opened his mouth.

---

Bells.

Small brass bells, a great many of them, ringing faintly somewhere below on the slope. Even the hum of the emptied faltered.

Someone was climbing Tal Moreeb on hands and knees through the sliding sand. Around his waist hung a belt of tiny truth bells that chimed with every movement, and all the way down the slope the emptied had turned to watch him pass, and none of them had touched him.

Old Bakhit.

For three hundred years he'd been bound to the edge of the Empty Quarter, the price of the night he ran. To leave it was to die.

He had crossed the sands anyway.

He was dying as he climbed. You could see it. His beard was thin as smoke. His skin had gone papery and was splitting at the knuckles, and what came out of the splits wasn't blood but a fine pale dust. He climbed toward our voices as if they were a fire on a cold night.

He reached the crest and knelt, gasping. Ghadban turned to him.

"Bakhit," said the grey boy warmly. "My old friend."

Every bell on Bakhit's belt rang at once.

"I was never your friend," said Bakhit. "You only told me what I wanted to hear, so I'd run. I've had three hundred years in the dark to work that out." He turned his blind face. "Boy. Are you there?"

"I'm here."

"I asked you once, in my tent. *Would you die for them?* You couldn't answer." The old man swallowed. "Someone asked me the same, on this sand, three hundred years ago. I said no, and I ran, and the last thing I ever saw was the top of this dune. I hid from you that bearers die, because I was ashamed. And I hid something else. When I said no, up here, I *heard* it, child. I heard it be disappointed." His hands clenched in the sand. "I'm sorry. I'm so sorry."

Not one bell rang. I crept to him on my belly and put my head in his lap, and his hand found my ears the way it used to, three hundred years ago, when he was a boy with a feather on his chest.

"Don't answer for me," whispered Bakhit. "Don't answer for them. Don't answer for whatever's listening. Answer for you."

Theyab stood very still on the top of Tal Moreeb.

He looked at Ghadban. He looked at me, and at my back, which had told him a thing about the Light he would never now stop knowing. He looked down at the emptied. He looked at Zaid, humming, forgetting his own name a little more with every breath. He looked at his father.

Then he untied Old Bakhit's truth bell from his belt and held it up in front of him, so everyone could see.

"Yes," he said.

The bell didn't ring.

And the grey went out of his Light like a shadow when the sun comes up. It turned gold. A deeper gold than I had ever seen, the gold of wild honey from the Hajar held up to the light. It was beautiful. I want you to know that. It was the most beautiful thing I have ever seen.

It was also the colour of a coal just before it falls apart.

Ghadban took a step backward. His enormous shadow took two.

---

"Seventh," said Muhaisen sharply. "Cut him."

Saqr raised his twin grey blades, the only things in the world that could cut the Light. The blades were shaking, and through the great crack in his mask one pale eye stared, wet and wide.

He was humming. Not the chant. The fire-bird song. He didn't know he was doing it.

Theyab walked straight up to him, until the grey blades were a finger's width from his chest and the steel began to frost and smoke at once where the Light met it.

"You held me," he said. "When I was born. I saw it. You were five, and you held me, and you were scared you'd drop me."

The blades trembled. Then, slowly, Saqr lowered them.

Theyab walked past him to Muhaisen. The wazir backed away, dragging Zaid, and laid the grey blade against the boy's throat. Theyab didn't stop. He put his hand over Muhaisen's hand on the grip, and the gold went into it.

Muhaisen cried out and let go. His hand was grey to the wrist where the Light had touched it, crazed with fine cracks like an old glazed pot. The blade fell into the sand.

Zaid stopped humming. He blinked. He said, in a small, astonished voice, "Zaid," as if he'd found it in a pocket.

Then he ran.

"*Eldest!*" Muhaisen shrieked. His soft voice was gone. "Cut him down!"

The Eldest stood directly in the boy's path. Bare-faced, with a fresh grey blade in his fist and fifteen years of obedience in his arms. I saw the blade come up. I saw it rise to the height of a ten-year-old's neck, and Zaid saw it, and couldn't stop, the sand sliding under his bare feet.

The Eldest looked down at him.

Then he looked past him, at Theyab, who had stood over him on the fort stairs a few hours before with a golden blade at his throat and said *go home*.

He stepped aside.

He just stepped aside, one pace, onto the slope, and let the boy go by, so close the boy's sleeve brushed his arm. Then he opened his hand and let the grey blade fall into the sand, point down, and stood there with his empty hands at his sides, looking at them as if they belonged to someone else. His lips were still moving. I was close enough now to hear. It was a woman's name. I think it was his mother's.

Zaid ran straight to Sharood, and Sharood caught him, and the boy buried his face in the grey wrappings and shook.

My hackles had not risen at the Eldest once. Not on the stairs. Not here. I don't know what that tells you. I know what it told me.

Theyab knelt before Zaid. He untied Hazza's ring from beside the silver knot and pressed it into Zaid's hand. It left a red mark on the small palm. Zaid closed his fingers on it anyway.

"Your father wanted you to have this. And so would Hazza'."

Zaid was ten. He wasn't stupid.

"Are you going to die?"

"Yes."

"Like Hazza'?"

Theyab thought about it. "No," he said. "Hazza' didn't get to choose. I do."

---

Sharood let go of Zaid and took his son's hands, and didn't flinch at the heat.

"Let me," he said.

It was not the voice of the Veiled Rider.

"Let me do it. It was mine before it was yours, and I ran. Give it me. I'm old, I'm nothing, let it have me instead. I ran so you could live. Don't make that for nothing. Please, son. Not you. Not you."

Theyab shook his head. He couldn't speak for a moment.

"It doesn't want you," he said, very gently. "It wants the Light, and the Light's in me." He tightened his grip. "And it wasn't for nothing. You left me in the sand, and the desert kept me, and I grew up alone, so I know what it's like to be the one nobody wants. That's why I can do this. It all meant something. Even the bad parts. *Especially* the bad parts."

Every hair on me lay down.

I'll tell you what I thought, little one. I thought: *it meant something. And I no longer know whether that's a comfort or the worst thing in the world.*

Sharood bowed his head over their joined hands. I have never seen anything as brave as what he did next.

He let go.

One finger at a time, slowly, like a man lowering something precious into a deep well. The skin of his palms stuck to his son's hands and tore, and he let go anyway. When the last finger slid free, he stepped back beside Zaid and Bakhit and didn't move again.

The hardest choice of his life. He made it.

---

Theyab came to me last.

I was lying in the sand by Bakhit, too tired to stand, my coat dark with my own blood, my silver eye aching the way it always aches near the end. He knelt so our faces were level. Heat came off him like an open bread oven.

"You lied to me once," he said. "With your whole body."

I didn't look away.

"It's going to hurt," he said.

My hackles lay flat. More than anything has ever hurt.

"It'll matter."

Flat.

"Ghadban's right, isn't he. Something's waiting."

And my hackles rose halfway up my back, and stayed there, and slowly lay down again, and rose. Neither. I didn't know. Some truths are too big even for a dog's back.

He watched them a long moment. Then he smiled, the small, crooked, rusty smile he never wasted, and put his forehead against mine. It singed the fur between my ears. I'll carry the mark until the world ends.

"Look after them," he said.

Never in all my long life have I wished so hard that I could speak. I've wished it every night since.

He walked to the very end of the crest, where the dune fell away on every side into the silver dark, and stood under the full moon with his back to us all.

"I always wanted to be a hero," he said quietly. To the boy in the patched tent, maybe. "Like in the stories the old men tell."

He took a breath.

"I am the hero."

It wasn't a boast. It was only true. I have never lain so still.

Then he lifted his face to the moon, and to whatever was beyond it, and he said the last words he would ever say.

"I'm coming, Hazza'."

---

And here I have to tell it the way I told it at the very beginning, because it has never stopped happening. It's happening now, somewhere behind my eyes, and I can still smell it.

He is burning.

He stands at the top of Tal Moreeb with his arms open, as if he's about to catch something falling out of the sky. The fire doesn't start at his hands. It starts in his chest, under the mark, the small golden feather that has glowed in the dark his whole life. It splits the way a date stone splits in the ground, from the inside. I hear it crack. Light comes through every golden line that every healing left on him, and the lines split wider, and his skin curls back from them like palm leaf in a fire. It runs down his arms and up his throat. It comes out of his eyes, and his eyes go with it.

The smell reaches me. Honey and hot iron, and under that, his hair. I will never stop smelling it. A dog can't.

And Muhaisen lunges.

Grey robes, grey hand, grey eyes wide and starving. He has waited his whole life for this light, and he throws himself into it with both hands, grabbing, as if he could tear it out of the boy's chest and keep it.

"*Mine,*" he says.

It isn't. It never could be. The Light can't be taken. It can only be given.

It turns grey in his hands. The gold he's grasping goes dull, then ashen, and the grey runs up his fingers and wrists and arms like frost climbing a window, cracking as it climbs. For a single moment his face changes. He sees something in the stolen light: a small boy, and a brother, and a name he burned long ago. *Helper*, it meant. His mouth opens to say it.

"Mu—"

His jaw goes first.

It doesn't fall. It crumbles, like an old mud-brick wall in the first rain in forty years, and the rest of him follows from the face down, still kneeling, still reaching. The last of him to go is his hands, clenched in the light, trying to hold on. The wind lifts the dust off Tal Moreeb and carries it into the dark, the way it carries away a name said carelessly.

There is nothing left of him. Not even the name. He never finished it.

And the boy doesn't scream.

That's the part I always remember. He is burning to the bone and he doesn't scream, and I have never understood how, and I have never wanted to.

He looks down. Below him, all across the sands, the dunes are moving, shifting and folding and rising, writing in letters miles high. The same shapes they wrote for him on the first nights after Hilat al-Sarab, when he couldn't read a word and I couldn't have told him what they said if I'd wanted to.

He can read them now. A young man with a broken sword taught him, in the sand, with a stick, laughing the whole time.

*Nun*, the dunes write, from right to left, the way he learned. *Alif. Ha. Dad.*

*NAHIDH.* In the old tongue, *the one who rises.*

His true name, the one the One who made the sands wrote for him before he was born, the one the wind could never find, because the desert kept it safe all this time. The way you keep something safe that you mean to use. He reads it, the last thing he will ever read, with eyes that are more light than eye now, and I see what's left of his mouth smile.

Then he's looking at something only he can see. But I see it too, for a moment, in the light pouring out of him.

Bir al-Ghina', under a thin moon. The sand humming. A young man on the edge of the well with half a sword across his knees, a scarred jaw and a crooked grin and dark eyes, laughing at him the way he always did. Holding out his hand.

*You took your time,* says Hazza'.

And behind Hazza', in the dark beyond the well, standing in a row as if they've waited a very long time, I see the others. Eight small figures, the eight who gave themselves. Some barely taller than the well's edge. A boy of nine with salt-white hair. A smith's apprentice with burned hands. A girl holding a weaver's shuttle. All looking at Theyab. None of them smiling. Their faces are patient and gentle and very, very tired, the faces of children who have stood in a line too long.

Theyab laughs, the way he laughed the first time, by a fire, when a sheikh's son tried to eat a scorpion. He takes the hand. The row of children opens to let him in, and he takes his place at the end of it.

There's a space beside him. An empty space at the very end of the row, the width of one small child.

I see it every time. I have never once been able to look away from it.

The light keeps growing until it isn't a boy anymore. It's a shape, two great wings of gold unfolding across the dark, the wings of the 'anqa from the old song, wide enough to cover the dunes from one edge of the sky to the other.

Then it falls.

Gold rains down over the sands, and everywhere it lands, the grey draws back.

Somewhere below, someone is shouting a name.

It's his father, on his knees in the sand a little way down the crest, his arms around a crying boy, his raw hands, his face turned up into the falling gold. Shouting it over and over, the only name he was ever allowed to call his son.

"*Theyab! Theyab!*"

And the wind takes it, gently, and carries it out over the whole of the sands. Not to the dark this time.

There isn't any dark left.

That's what I believed, that night. I want you to know I believed it. For one whole night, I did.

---

*End of Chapter Nineteen*


---

# Chapter Twenty
## Ash into Gold

> *Ash into gold and gold into flame —*
> *The end is the start, and the start is the same.*
> — from the song of the fire-bird; the last verse, which is also the first

---

The gold fell all night.

It came down soft and slow, like the first rain after a seven-year drought, and everywhere it touched, the grey let go.

I watched from the top of Tal Moreeb. I couldn't have moved if I'd wanted to. Down at the foot of the dune, the great ring of the emptied stood with their faces turned up into the falling light, and one by one, the white went out of their eyes. Brown came back. Black. Hazel. An old herder looked down at his own hands and began to cry. A woman spun round and round in the sand, calling a child's name, and somewhere in the crowd a child's voice called back.

Not all of them. I promised you no more silences, so I'll tell you. Some of them woke screaming, because the gold gave them back everything, including what their hands had done while they were empty. And some didn't wake at all. The gold touched them, and they lay down in the sand, still smiling, and didn't get up.

Ghanim woke with his memory and nothing else, a cracked white mask at his feet. Camels, strongbox, the silver sewn into his saddle-bags against bad years, all gone to the Nameless. They say he sweeps out a coffee house in Al-Nakhlatain now, and counts every coin that crosses the floor twice, and none of them are his.

Then there was *sound*. Thousands of people talking and weeping and shouting names. The whole of the sands was saying them.

Far off, over the walls of Ubar, a light came on. Then another. Someone was lighting the lamps again. And in the wall niches, I'm told, the names of the dead came back in gold, and stayed.

The grey went out of the sands like a tide going out over the sabkha.

That's what everyone says. But I have one silver eye that sees what jinn see, and I'll tell you what I watched. It didn't go *away*. It went *down*. It drained into the dunes the way the rain drains into a wadi bed, sinking between the grains, until there was nothing on the surface but gold.

The tide goes out over the salt flats, little one. It doesn't stop being the sea.

---

The gold fell on Ghadban too.

The small grey herd-boy was standing alone at the edge of the crest, and he didn't run. He stood still and let it land on him, the way you'd let snow land on you if you had never seen snow.

And the grey came off him like dust.

Under it was a boy. Brown and thin and barefoot, with a stick of ordinary acacia and a scab on one knee and goat hair caught in his sleeve. His eyes weren't black anymore. They were brown, warm brown, the eyes I'd looked into across a thousand fires before the towns on this coast were built.

"Ghareeb?" he said, in a small voice.

I couldn't have answered if I could talk. It was the second time in one night.

He looked at his own hands, then back at me, and his eyes filled.

"I remember my name," he said.

Every hair on my back lay down so smooth it was like being stroked. It was true. He remembered his name.

I pushed my head into his chest, the way I used to when he was six. *I never forgot it*, I told him, the way jinn hear me. *Not once.*

He smiled. And I have spent every night since trying to decide what kind of smile it was. A boy's smile, healed and sorry and tired. Or the smile of someone who has finally put down a very heavy thing, and doesn't care where it lands, or who picks it up next.

He lifted his face to the last of the falling gold and came apart into light. It rose off Tal Moreeb and went away east toward the coming sun.

But his shadow didn't go with him.

It lay on the sand where he'd stood, and it was not a boy's shadow. It was the other one. Too large, too many joints, horned. For one breath after the rest of him was gone it lay there. Then it sank. Into the dune. Down, with the rest of the grey, the way a stone sinks into still water, and the sand closed over it without a ripple.

My hackles did nothing at all.

I've hated Ghadban for more years than I can count. I'll miss him for longer. And I still don't know whether I watched him healed that night, or only watched him put down the Ifrit and walk away from it.

---

The Seventh was kneeling at the very end of the crest, where the boy had stood.

The sand there had turned gold. Not gold-coloured. *Gold.* You know the old stories: sand that holds a bearer's ash turns gold and never moves again. It lay in a small, perfect circle on the top of the highest dune in the world. The wind went across it and didn't move a single grain. In the middle of it lay Old Bakhit's golden feather, the one found with the baby in the sand. It was whole. Every barb.

Saqr had put down his blades. As I watched, his white mask split along its crack and fell away in two halves. It took skin with it. Fifteen years it had been on. There were raw red lines down his cheeks where its edges had grown into him.

Under it was a young man's face. Twenty, pale-eyed, thin, with his father's jaw and his brother's watchful look.

He opened his mouth. He hadn't spoken a word of his own in fifteen years.

"Nahidh," said Saqr.

Sharood turned round.

He crossed the crest faster than I've ever seen a man move and caught his elder son in his arms. For a moment, a single moment, it was what it should have been.

Then Saqr began to remember.

Not the tent and the song and the baby. That came first, and it was sweet, and it lasted perhaps three breaths. Then the rest came. Fifteen years of it, all at once, the way the gold had given it back to the emptied. Every child he had carried into the hold of the buried dhow. Every name he had watched burn. The exact moment each child had stopped crying. The things the grey blades had done in his hands, which he had not been able to feel at the time and could feel now, every one, all together.

He screamed.

He tore himself out of his father's arms and clawed at his own face, at the raw lines where the mask had been, as though he could put it back. Sharood caught his wrists. Saqr fought him, and screamed, and then stopped screaming and simply shook, and Sharood held on, the way he had held on to Theyab's burning hands. He didn't let go. He'd learned something about letting go, and something about not.

And after a long time, in the middle of it, through his teeth, Saqr began to hum. The fire-bird song. Over and over. As if it were the only rope left down the well and he was hanging off it by his fingers.

The Eldest sat a little way down the slope, saying his mother's name.

Zaid stood beside Sharood with Hazza's ring in his fist, looking out across the sands.

"Ghareeb," he said, and put his hand on my head. "Look."

The sun was coming up.

And below us, across the whole of the Empty Quarter, the dunes had turned gold in great sweeping lines, miles long. They weren't moving anymore. They never would again. Four letters, written from one edge of the sky to the other, so large you could have read them from the towers of Ubar, so large the herders of Liwa read them still.

*NAHIDH.*

---

Old Bakhit was lying on his back in the sand.

He'd been there all night. I'd forgotten him. Everyone had. That shames me now. I limped over, and he turned his face toward the sound of me. The dust was running out of the cracks in his hands like sand from a split sack.

"Dog," he said. His voice was barely there. "Is it morning?"

I licked his hand. Nearly.

"Did he forgive me? The boy?"

I laid my head on his chest, where he could feel my hackles lying flat. He'd been a bearer. He understood me.

"He kept it," Bakhit whispered. "The whole way."

He smiled, the smile I've never been able to describe, fond and sad at once, the way a man smiles at an old letter.

"May the sand remember him," he whispered.

Then the sun came up over the edge of the world, and its first light fell across his milky eyes.

And the milk cleared out of them.

They went clear and dark and wet, the eyes of a young man, and they *saw*. He looked up at the sky going pink and gold. He looked out at the great golden letters lying across the dunes. The last thing he had seen, three hundred years before, was the top of this dune in the dark. The first thing he saw now was the dawn.

"Oh," said Bakhit. "Oh, *look* at it."

Then his eyes moved. Past the letters. Past the dunes. Out to the far rim of the sands, where the dunes meet the sky, out where no caravan has ever come back from.

His face changed.

I have tried, every night since, to decide what was in it. Wonder. Terror. Both. The face of a man who has been in the dark three hundred years and, in the first light, sees the thing that has been standing beside him the whole time.

"Ghareeb," he whispered. "Ghareeb, at the edge. There's something— it's getting *up*. Dog, it's so big, it's been lying there all along, and it's—"

That was all.

His eyes stayed open, fixed on the edge of the world. His debt was paid, and he was gone. The bells at his belt were silent.

Then one of them rang. Once. Very softly. Though nobody had said a word.

I looked where he'd been looking, with both my eyes. I saw the far dunes, and the heat beginning to shimmer on them, and a mirage like a lake, and nothing else.

That isn't the same as there being nothing else.

We buried him on the slope of Tal Moreeb, facing east, so he'd always have the dawn. Sharood dug, and I dug beside him, because digging is a thing a dog can do. I nosed his eyes shut. They stayed shut. I was glad of that. I didn't want them looking.

---

They went down at noon. Sharood and Saqr, and Zaid between them, back toward Ubar, where every lamp was burning again and the people were painting a new name on the wall of the fort court. Zaid would be sheikh. He was ten.

I stayed with the gold. I lay beside that small glittering circle through the long afternoon and into the night, and I didn't eat, and I didn't sleep. There was nothing to guard. The gold would never move. Nothing in the sands would ever touch it.

I guarded it anyway.

Some time after dark, I put my ear to the sand beside the circle.

Something was breathing down there.

Very deep. Very slow. Not the way the tombs of Ubar breathed, in and out. It went in, and then it didn't come out. It held. Like breath held under water by a pearl diver who can hold it longer than any diver who ever lived, and is patient, and is listening to the footsteps going by overhead.

The Ifrit wasn't gone. It was sleeping. And it had pulled the gold over itself like a blanket.

When the moon came up that night, just past full, I stood on the top of the highest dune in the world and howled at the sky.

Not at the One who made the sands. I'm old, not foolish, and I have never been certain He is the one doing the asking. At the dark past the sky, and the far edge where Bakhit had looked. I put every word into it that I've never been able to say.

*Not this one. You hear me? Not this one. Not again. I have walked beside twelve of them. I have watched nine of them burn on this sand. And they're children, every time, children, you only ever want the children. He never had anything. Nobody ever gave him a single thing he didn't have to dig out of the rubbish heap, and he gave you everything, and you took it, and it isn't even finished, is it? It's never finished. You'll want another—*

Nothing answered. Nothing ever does. It only waits.

The wind came up from the east, gentle, and went over the golden circle without stirring a grain. Then it went over me and smoothed down my coat, the way you'd smooth a frightened child's hair.

Or the way you'd smooth the coat of a hound you mean to keep.

I lay down beside the gold with my nose on my paws. And I wept, little one. Salukis do. Ask anyone who has ever kept one.

---

There's something I have to tell you now.

I've been putting it off the whole story. You'll have noticed. You're a great deal cleverer than you look, all pink and wrinkled like that.

I saw it happen.

The night of the full moon, one month before the end, when they argued and Theyab walked away into the dark. I wasn't by the fire. I was out on the dunes in my other shape, my shadow as tall as a watchtower, hunting the jackals that were tracking his Light. I didn't want him to see me like that. Not after the storm, when his Light burned me. Not after the way he'd looked at me.

So I was out in the dark being a monster, and that's why I was too far away.

I heard voices from the camp. Hazza's. And another one, soft, that I didn't know yet. I caught the smell on the wind before I caught the words. Rosewater.

By the time I came over the ridge, it was almost done.

Muhaisen was kneeling by the fire. He'd come alone, in a plain brown cloak, no mask, on a single camel. Hazza' had known him his whole life: the man who had sat at his father's right hand in the majlis every day he'd been alive. So when Muhaisen held out a waterskin and said *You must be thirsty. The boy has left you. Come home,* Hazza' drank. You don't refuse water in the sands. Not from a man who knew you as a child. Not ever.

There was ash in that water. Bearer's ash, stolen from a grave. False Light. That's why he went grey, little one. That's why the golden cracks. I watched them open across his face like a dropped coffee cup, and I watched him not understand what was happening to him.

I was a moment too late. One moment. If I'd let one jackal go. If I'd turned at the first voice instead of the second.

Muhaisen saw me coming over the ridge, huge and black with my coal eye, and he ran. He had Hazza's ring in his hand. He'd pulled it off while the young man was still falling.

I reached Hazza'. He was already gone. His eyes were open. He'd fallen facing the way Theyab had walked.

I tried to close them. I had to shrink back into a dog even to try, and a dog has no hands. I pushed at them with my nose. They wouldn't stay shut. They kept opening, looking the way Theyab had gone. In the morning the boy closed them himself.

And then I said nothing.

A dog can't speak. That's my whole excuse, isn't it? But I could have led him to the tracks of a single camel going north. I could have found a hundred ways. I've had a hundred reasons not to. No proof. Who'd believe a jinn-touched dog against a sheikh's wazir? If the boy had known too early, he'd have gone after Muhaisen with hate, and his Light would have gone grey. All true, more or less.

But it isn't *why*.

The why is that I was ashamed. Ashamed of what I'd been out there, and of being slow, and that the boy I loved most would have to know I watched his only friend die and couldn't stop it.

So I let him carry it alone. For the rest of his life he thought it was his fault for walking away. I heard him whisper *I'm sorry* into the dark a hundred times, and I lay a few paces off with my eyes shut and pretended to be asleep.

He would have forgiven me. That's the worst of it. He'd have put his hand on my head and said *it's all right, Ghareeb*, and his mark wouldn't have burned at all.

That's my confession. I'm telling you because you're the only one I've never lied to.

Not yet, anyway. Give me time.

---

Twelve years went by. Desert years. Long ones.

But first, the morning after. I wasn't there. I've heard Nadim tell it at the fire so often I could tell it in his voice.

The grey had taken Hilat al-Sarab that summer. Every palm-frond roof the colour of ash, every face, the well water grey in the bucket. Only one lamp in the village had kept burning through it: a little clay lamp in a widow's window, which she filled every night with oil she couldn't spare, though she could no longer have told you why, or for whom.

When the gold came down over Liwa that dawn, it came to Umm Hanan's house first.

Nadim was standing grey in the lane with his brother's name gone out of his head. He says the old peeling blue came back into her door first. Then the red of her coals. Then the brown of the bread on her shelf. For a long breath that was the only colour in the village, before the gold spread out from it down the lane like water from a broken jar.

He says he never knew why it went there first. It's true. He never knew.

---

The grey never came back up. The villages filled again with people who remembered their names. Hilat al-Sarab was rebuilt around the same deep well. And there was a new lamp on the Night of Lamps.

Umm Hanan lit it.

She was so old by then she had to be helped to the patch of hard ground between the last house and the first dune, where a patched tent had once stood. Nobody had built there since. Nobody would.

She held the taper in both shaking hands. The lamp was the same clay lamp from her window, plain and small, with a feather scratched into its side.

"For the boy," she said, thin as paper, "who I should have spoken up for. Theyab. Who was Nahidh."

She lit it.

And as the flame caught, a wind came. Out of nowhere, out of a still night, cold, from the east, from the far edge. It went down the whole line of the village like a hand, and every lamp in Hilat al-Sarab went out at once. Every one. Doorsteps, windows, the eleven on the wall of the well-house. Dark from end to end.

Except hers.

Her flame leaned. It leaned away from her, out toward the open sands, east, toward the far edge, the way a dog's head turns toward a sound you can't hear. For a breath it burned blue-grey, the colour of the emptied. Then it straightened and went gold again, and stayed lit.

Umm Hanan blinked at it, and said nothing, and nobody else said anything either.

From it, one by one, the whole village lit theirs again. Then they relit the other eleven and said the names. Not the titles. The names. Nadim had them from me, the only way he could, sitting up with me night after night until he understood. I'd decided someone ought to know they were children.

Nadim lit his last, with Shibl, nineteen now and taller than him, carrying the taper. Nadim had grown into a broad, quiet man with early grey in his beard who kept his father Saqi's well. He never talked about Al-Nakhlatain. Every Night of Lamps he stood at the twelfth lamp longer than anyone, until his wife came and took his hand.

Her name was Hessa. She was round as the full moon that year.

The baby came that same night, after the lamps were lit. A boy. Small and furious and very loud.

Hessa was holding him against her in the dark of the little mud house when she went still.

There, just under his collarbone, was a mark the colour of honey. Shaped like a feather.

It was glowing.

She didn't call out. She didn't smile. She put her hand over it. Flat, hard, the way you'd cover a lamp in a house where something is looking in at the windows. She'd grown up on the story, every child in Hilat al-Sarab had, and she'd listened to it closely, and she knew exactly what that mark was for.

"No," she whispered. "No, no. Not him. Nadim. *Nadim.* Make it stop."

Nadim looked at it a long time. Then he went down on his knees beside the bed and cried like a boy. And when he could speak again, he didn't ask anyone. There was no point. It was written across the whole of the sands in letters miles high, and the sands don't take requests.

"Nahidh," he said, and his voice broke in the middle of it. "His name is Nahidh."

I was lying in the doorway. I'd been lying in that doorway since the moon had turned nine times. Hessa had stopped trying to chase me off. She looked at me now, over her son's head, and I saw her understand, and I saw her hate me for it. She was right to.

---

Nadim doesn't sleep much. He sold one bearer, once, to the Nameless, for his brother's life. He has never said so aloud. He sleeps across the inside of the door with a khanjar under his hand. He carries the boy everywhere on his shoulders, and if a stranger so much as asks the child's name in the souq, Nadim puts himself between them. He's been wrong once about who to protect. He means to spend the rest of his life on the other side of it.

Sharood and Saqr help. They built a house where Old Bakhit's tent used to stand and hung it with herbs and little brass bells. Sharood is an old man now, older than his years. Every night, when the sun goes down, he takes his long spear and sits at the edge of the well-keeper's house, facing the dunes, and he doesn't go in until morning.

Saqr doesn't sleep at all. I don't think he has slept a full night in twelve years. He walks the edge of the village in the dark, and sometimes I hear him counting under his breath, the numbers he gave the children in the dhow, as if counting them could bring them back. And every night, at the darkest hour, he sits under the baby's window and hums the fire-bird song, and the baby stops crying to listen.

---

I'll tell you one more thing, and then I'll stop.

Some nights the wind talks.

It comes up out of the sand at the darkest hour, not a sound so much as a pressure, and it comes to the cradle. The goats break their tethers. The village dogs slink off under the palms with their tails clamped. Even Sharood, out at the edge with his spear, puts his face in his hands.

It's a voice I know. I heard it under Ubar. I heard it in Ghadban's mouth, a beat behind.

*Go, old dog,* it says. *You're tired. You've done enough. Lie down somewhere else tonight. Somewhere warm. Just one night.*

My hackles go up so hard they ache, every time. I growl until my ribs shake.

And I don't move. Not once. Not a paw's width from that cradle. It's tried every night since you were born, and I'm still here. I'll still be here when it stops trying. I've been frightened off from a great many things in my life, little one. Not from you.

The dunes out at the far edge of the sands have started writing again. Out where no caravan has ever come back from. Out where Bakhit was looking.

The first year, I could only see the shapes from the top of Tal Moreeb. It's back where it belongs now, at the edge of Liwa, and the herders say it's taller than it was. The shapes aren't letters anyone knows.

The fifth year, Sharood could see them from the towers of Ubar.

This spring, I climbed the well-house roof and saw them, very faint, on the rim of the world. They're coming closer. Every season, a little. The way the grey once came, low and flat and in no hurry. It doesn't need to hurry.

And under the gold, when I put my ear to it, something is still holding its breath.

The Light doesn't come back for no reason, little one. It comes back because it's going to be needed. Because something is hungry. On one side, or the other. Or both. I've stopped being sure there's a difference.

But not tonight.

Tonight the lamps are burning along the edge of the sands, and your mother is asleep with her hand over your chest, and your father is asleep across the door with his knife. Old Sharood is out at the edge of the house with his spear. Saqr is humming under the window. And I'm curled beside your cradle with my nose on my paws, where I'll be every night from now on, whether anyone likes it or not.

You've been listening all this time. I know you have. Your mark has been glowing brighter at every part where he was brave. It's warm against my coat. Warmer every night.

So let me start again, the way I started it. The way I'll start it for you every night, until you're old enough to tell me to stop.

Let me tell you about a boy who had no name…

He was the one I loved most. Don't tell the others. Don't tell yourself, either. Not yet.

The end is the beginning, you see. It always has been. That's what the knot means. That's what I'm for. I was made to walk beside you, and to love you, and when it's time, to be the one who wakes you. I've done it twelve times. I've never once been able to stop.

And out there in the dark, past the lamps and the golden dunes and the far edge of the world, something is already waiting for you.

The sun is coming up.

Wake up, little one.

---

*The old man stopped.*

*The fire had burned down to coals. None of the children moved. Over Liwa the Pleiades had climbed high, and in the south, low on the dunes, Suhail burned red.*

*"You'll say it's a tale," he said at last. "I told you at the start. It happened. I was there." He turned his hands over in the firelight, big hands, old, with a well-keeper's calluses. "My name is Nadim. I sold him. And then I raised him."*

*A girl at the back whispered, "And Nahidh? Your son? Where is he?"*

*The old man didn't answer. He looked out past the firelight, east, toward the far edge of the sands, for a long time.*

*"Umm Hanan's lamp still burns," he said instead. "Forty years. Nobody has ever had to fill it."*

*At his feet, the old saluki lifted his head. One eye amber, one eye silver. He got up, stiffly, the way the very old get up, and shook the sand from his coat, and walked away from the fire without looking back, across the camp to a small tent where a lamp was burning and a newborn had been sleeping for three nights.*

*He lay down across its door.*

*Out on the dunes, very faintly, the sand was writing.*

---

*End of Chapter Twenty*
